# Continuity State

## Canon Status

**Volume 02 is open. Volume 02, Batch 0002 is written: Chapters 51–70.** `outline/batches/volume-02-batch-0002.md` is the card source for the batch just written, including a closing section recording eight divergences between its cards and the page. The Volume 01 record below is closed and is not disturbed by either Volume 02 batch. **Everything above the Volume 02 sections is Volume 01 canon and governs Volume 01; everything under the Volume 02 heading is Volume 02 canon and governs Volume 02.** Where the two are looser than each other, the Volume 02 sections govern Volume 02 and the Volume 01 sections govern Volume 01, and neither governs the other.

**Volume 01 is complete and is closed.** Batches 0001 to 0005, Chapters 1–50, are written and canon below reflects what is now on the page. `outline/volume-01.md` gives the chapter range as 1–50, so there is no Volume 01 batch left to write. **The Volume 01 close at `workspace/volume-01/volume-close/` has run**, and everything it produced is in this file and in `state/current.md`. **There is no further Volume 01 phase.** The next phase of record is the **Volume 02 outline**, which is a separate phase that has not been written and may not be written, stubbed, or pre-empted from any Volume 01 phase.

**Repair pass applied to Volume 02 Batch 0001 after its review** (`logs/next.review.log`). It changed no plot, invented no fact, restarted no chapter, and left the ending alone. The reviewer cleared the batch's power discipline, its canon locks, its word counts, its filler blacklist, its duplication check and its six recorded divergences, and returned twenty-six concrete prose and bookkeeping findings. What this pass moved, all of it inside Chapters 51–60 and the files that describe them: **(1) one arithmetic contradiction that was live in the canon lock the next batch must obey.** The board at the Mosswake window read *nineteen returns did not come back* while the chapter's own breakdown gives fifty-four filed copies as thirty-one with a name and a door, eleven with a word and no name, and twelve that came back from the post stamped undeliverable, plus six never copied in a fourth cycle. Twelve plus six is **eighteen**, and thirty-one plus eleven plus twelve is fifty-four and fifty-four plus six is sixty, so the whole column now holds: **the board, Chapter 53, this file, `state/current.md`, `state/open-threads.md`, `state/batch-summary.md`, `state/chapter-summaries.md` and the Batch 0001 card file all say eighteen, and `workspace/volume-02/batch-0002/PROMPT.md` now carries the whole breakdown as a lock and forbids any other figure against it.** (2) **The word "the present holder" is three words, not six,** and the chapter that builds its climax on the count said six in four places in Chapter 54 and once in Chapter 58; all five are now three, in the two chapters, in this file, in `state/character-state.md` and in `state/chapter-summaries.md`. (3) **Silas Sowle is the district's poor solicitor and was in the room on the second of the twelfth**, so Chapter 58's *who is not her solicitor and has never heard of her* was a hard break; he is now the household's solicitor and nobody else's, who had not asked her name once in an afternoon, which is the same point and is true. (4) **Twenty-two further internal contradictions, each repaired at the line.** Full list at the end of this record. (5) **Two beats added where the reviewer found a structural miss and not a sentence fault:** Chapter 58 gave Marek Kest three lines and no emotional beat at the end of a chapter that is entirely about what she did to him, and it now ends on his answering the second half of their rule in words with a date, and on a new undertaking dated the **fourth of the first month** that a later batch must not retire; and Chapter 59 gave Gideon Harrow, who has the best speech in Chapter 57, one line in a list, and he now comes up the yard two days before the bridge is carried back to read the list because she wrote on it that he might. **(6) Two planning files reconciled, because the next batch reads them both.** `workspace/volume-02/batch-0002/PROMPT.md` pointed a writer at `volume-02-batch-0001.md`'s sibling path, which is the existing cards file, and now names `outline/batches/volume-02-batch-0002.md`; and `outline/volume-02.md` still assigned Corvin Dray's sentence and Tamsin Rook's appointment to Chapters 91–100 when the sentence is Chapter 65 and the appointment is a conversion of a holding she has had since the eleventh of the ninth month, and its midpoint-reversal card still described a Lowcross file that Chapter 58 has already delivered. **(7) Four findings are recorded here and are NOT actioned, because the files are controller-owned and this pass may not touch them:** `opencode run --agent novel-reviewer` in `scripts/novel_runner.sh:245` cannot work while `.opencode/agent/novel-reviewer.md` declares `mode: subagent`, so the review phase silently runs the writer and **there is no independent reviewer anywhere in the review-to-fix loop for any batch in this repository**; the same bad flag is at `.github/workflows/*.yml:64`. `state/phase-ledger.json` is about sixty chapters stale, is read by nothing, and is declared permanently stale in `state/current.md`. `workspace/volume-02/batch-0001/` does not exist, and it must not be created by a writing phase, because a prompt directory without a `.done` marker sorts ahead of `batch-0002` and the dispatcher would run it as a duplicate phase; the batch is traceable through `outline/batches/volume-02-batch-0001.md` and this file instead. And `workspace/continuation/next/` still has no `.done` and shadows `workspace/volume-02/batch-0002/` until the current run finishes. **None of these four is a fiction defect and none was worked around.**

**The twenty-two internal contradictions, each repaired at the line and each verifiable in the file named.**

1. **Ch 51, the room counted three ways.** Nine bundles and four loose objects is thirteen things, and the chapter said he counted the room twice and got nine. He now counts **the bundles** twice. The 440 chased documents no longer sit *four feet from his own table*, which was the Ashfall bundle's own distance and was making two facts out of one; they are in a tray on the sill.
2. **Ch 51, a garbled clause.** *He had been transferred into a chair that is the reason a document cannot be got at by asking* is now *He had been transferred to a counter, and a counter is a place a document comes to and not a place a person goes to ask for one*, which is what the next paragraph says.
3. **Ch 52, a non-sequitur.** *Twice is eighty-two and once is forty-one, and I have never once been able to make that come out the same way twice* was both a non-sequitur and a contradiction of the line above it. It is now the count holding steady, and her joke is that this is the first time.
4. **Ch 52, the register page.** The two gaps are **on two pages, not one** — the twelfth of the ninth month of last year and the sixth of the fourth month of this year — and the page now says so, which also agrees with Chapter 55, where Marrow turns the register to *the page for last year and the page for this year*.
5. **Ch 52 and Ch 55, a duplicated clause inside Marek's own page** (*a finding about the reader, and it is a finding about a reader*) is cut.
6. **Ch 53, three cycles of fifty-four.** The line read as 162. It is now *Three cycles and fifty-four filed copies*, and the 31/54/60 arithmetic in divergence five holds on the page as well as in the card.
7. **Ch 53, a wall dated after it was read.** The outgoing-rack paragraph was dated the fourth of the tenth month and the man from the works read the wall on the second of the tenth. It is now the **twenty-eighth of the ninth month**, which also stops it colliding with the fourth of the tenth, which is Volume 01's Sallow date and belongs to that line alone.
8. **Ch 54, the same copyist at two ages.** A woman of twenty wrote the four questions nine weeks earlier; the copyist who comes to the table with four pence of carriage is a copyist of about forty. She is now **a woman of about forty**, the same person, which is the point of the paragraph.
9. **Ch 54, nine weeks and eleven weeks in one chapter.** The form comes back in about nine weeks and the girl reaches her finding in nine weeks; the wall note said eleven. It is now nine.
10. **Ch 55, four names a decade.** Marrow says fourteen a year, which over thirty years is four hundred and something, and then says he has about four names a decade. It is now **about a hundred and forty names a decade**.
11. **Ch 55, inverted causality.** *There were two gaps in it, because Tamsin Rook had filled them in* is now *there was not a gap in either of them, because she had filled both in*.
12. **Ch 55, eleven weeks that were four.** The letter is the seventeenth of the **tenth** month and the visit is the third week of the **eleventh**, which is four weeks, and the Docket could not have written before the fifteenth of the ninth because that is when Tamsin's card told it. **Four weeks** throughout, and the first week instead of the first fortnight. The Batch 0002 prompt now carries the letter, the four weeks, the fifteenth of the ninth and the two pages as locks.
13. **Ch 55, a headcount used as a count.** *Four hundred and eleven crossings a week* — 411 is the number of **people** on the far side of the Weir Cut in Chapter 54 and the crossings figure is 388. Marek's page now uses **three hundred and eighty-eight crossings**, and the Batch 0002 prompt states that the two are different figures and may not be swapped.
14. **Ch 56, the levy arithmetic.** Two pounds five and a farthing a quarter on four houses is **eight pounds and a penny a quarter**, and four of those is **thirty-two pounds and fourpence**, not nine pounds a quarter and thirty-six. Roke's line read aloud now says the true figures, and the farthing is still Nery Caudle's own money out of a cloth.
15. **Ch 56, five months called eleven.** The four questions went on a stone in the sixth month and the levy was read on the eleventh of the eleventh, so *the only paper in eleven months that the four questions have made better* is now **five months**, which is the same figure the letter to Marek uses two days later.
16. **Ch 57, nine paragraphs that are seven.** *Nine paragraphs, four of them about a printed sentence, one… one… and one* is **seven**, and the afternoon of the hearing is now counted correctly.
17. **Ch 59, nineteen years of arithmetic against a nine-year cycle.** The cost is nineteen pounds three and fourpence every nine years, and the chapter then accounted for nineteen years as four lots of a pound and fifteen lots of a pound. It is now **two repairs in nineteen years and about a pound a year in the years between**, and the fund is **about six pounds** on the average of four quarters against the six pounds and eightpence committed for the quarter, which is the figure everywhere else.
18. **Ch 58, the author arguing with continuity in the prose.** *no, that was Mosswake's, and this was a different building* is cut and the sentence stands on its own.
19. **Ch 58, three copies and four destinations.** The order was Crane, Sowle, Sarn, and then a rationale that named Marek second, and a dangling *that* in the third. The three copies are now Crane, Sowle, Sarn in that order, the rationale says the household first and Marek before he knew, and the dangling clause is gone.
20. **Ch 58, an eighth hour and a fourth hour.** The chapter put the third copy on Marek's table at the fourth hour and then said the cart went at the eighth hour at night. It is the **eighth hour of that night** throughout, which is what this file's chronology already said.
21. **Ch 60, five columns of which one was a price.** The fifth heading was *A shilling a copy*, which is a price and not a column, and the chapter then entered *four of the five columns out of five* and listed four items that matched none of the named columns. The headings are now **Date. Household. Duty. Next review. Copy sold**, the fourth is named as the one she could not fill, the second column is named as where the name and the door go, and Chapter 53's description of the form was brought into line.
22. **Ch 60, three garbled clauses and a date.** *A sentence she has now heard a man in a good coat's building say out loud* is now *a man in a good coat say out loud*; the long sentence beginning *which is the half of it that was the whole of the reason* is broken into four and the self-cancelling clause is cut, and *a man in a book is a thing that can be done in six months* is kept because it is the point; and **Corvin Dray's plea was read back and not refused again on the eighteenth of the ninth, the same afternoon**, not in the seventh month.

**And the meta-language breaches, which `AGENTS.md` forbids and which a repair pass on Batch 0004 already once caught.** Five narrator lines named the volume or the book and are now in-world: Chapter 51's *the only document of the whole of Volume 02 that he writes in the first week that nobody has asked him to write* and Chapter 55's *the only document of the whole of Volume 02 that he writes in a room with another person in it* — the same construction twice, which is why it was obvious; Chapter 57's *the most honest piece of paper any office in this book has produced*; Chapter 52's *Nobody in this book can reach a private book*; and Chapter 54's *the water holder is not named on any page of this*. Chapter 55's *I have never been in this book* is the register and is left alone. **The real tics the reviewer found instead of the blacklisted ones are the copula assertion and the number eleven**, and both were worked at without being allowed to become censorship: *and it is* is down from about seventy-one to sixty-five, *that is the* from about seventy to nineteen, and *about eleven \<unit\>* from six chapters to three, with the load-bearing elevens — the eleven protected people, the sixty-one certificates, the thirty-eight lines, the eleven lines on a schedule, the eleven months, and the **eleven weeks of unpaid labour on a bridge that is carried back on the twenty-fourth of the twelfth for an application in the first week of the fourth** — all left exactly as they are, and `counted` was reintroduced once in Chapter 51 because the manuscript's subject is counting and the word is doing work there.

**Repair pass applied to Batch 0005 after its writer run** (`logs/batch-0005.review.log`). It changed no plot, invented no fact, no chapter was restarted, and the ending is unchanged. What it moved: (1) **seven canon-lock breaches** — the hooded seed-barn copyist is **four** of the twenty-six in the ninth month and **a fifth** in the twelfth and no longer reads five and five; the weekday name on the lane board is removed and the board now says the month and the week; (2) **twenty arithmetic and internal contradictions**, of which the ones that change a stated fact are: a woman on four shillings a day earns about five pounds four shillings a month, so **eleven shillings is two days of work and six months of saving** and is now said that way in Chapters 49 and 50; the eleven houses' answers now add to eleven (nine asked and told, one *I do not know*, one *nobody asked me at all*); **Emberwatch is four hundred and forty miles** in every mention; the two Emberwatch letters now reconcile — **three go to the lane on the fifth of the sixth and the fourth, the one addressed to the standing assessor, stays in the drawer**, in both Chapters 42 and 44, and the reason it stays is a reason about Marek; Chapter 50's "eleven weeks" is gone and is **the four months since the review of the fourth of the fourth**; "a man who has spent four months learning to say out loud what he wants" is now **eight months**; the Bryn Mawer roll-call entry no longer merges three women into one, and the woman of twenty-two with her own name in a box now sits inside the furnace-town entry where she belongs; and a third-distance "four hundred miles" of vats in Chapter 48 is gone; (3) **the list handover in Chapter 48 is now unambiguous** — the four pages go to **Bryn Mawer at the chandler's counter, Sarn, Sayer and Nell**, which are the four roles the card names, the unexplained "the slate" is cut, and the Greyfen road copyist is no longer a fifth holder of the list (she is the reason a name exists on Bryn's page); (4) **four chapter-summary corrections**; (5) **Chapter 46 cut from 4,626 to 4,279** and **Chapter 50 to 3,933**; (6) Chapter 43's title is now **Twenty-Three Doors and a Man Who Already Knew**, because the old title promised a discovery that belongs to Chapter 44; "I say them in beds" is now "in kitchens"; Chapter 45's four identical letter lines are varied on the fourth; and (7) real month names were replaced with numbered months throughout, so **Chapters 41–50 use no real month name and no weekday name at all.**

**Two divergences between the cards and the page, recorded and not canon changes.** (a) The Chapter 45 card requires the four *nobody read it to me* answers to be entered on the board **with a name each**; the page has **three names and one refusal** — a man of sixty-one, asked twice, who says no twice and has that written down, which the chapter treats as an answer. **A later batch may not write that four names went on the board.** (b) The Batch 0005 goal sentence and `outline/volume-01.md` both name **a red thread continuing across a burned archive wrapper** as the volume's last image, and the Chapter 50 card specifies a **roll-call** closing on *there is not one line of it that says I was right*. Both are on the page: the stitch is found in the second half of the chapter and the roll-call is the last thing in the book. **The roll-call is the volume's closing image and the stitch is the route it ends on.**

**A new canon fact carried into Volume 02, recorded here because the review asked for it.** In Chapter 46 the third order records that of the ninety-eight instances of a sold third witness mark, **twenty-two are in the Reed Cut district, six are in the Venn file, and one is in the Dunn file**, and that a filled square with a bar through it and a notch cut out of the top left corner sits in the fourth column against the carry column beside the word *paid* against thirty-eight lines in three districts. **That is a mark and not a name, it is entered as a mark, and no chapter may convert it into a named house or a named client.** The one line's presence in the Dunn file means only that the mark was used in a file the court had before it on the seventeenth of the sixth; it does not make the Dunn household a client of anything, and Corvin's clients remain untouched on the page.

## The Word-Count Record, Measured At The Volume 01 Close

**The command, so that every number here can be checked.** For each chapter, from the repository root:

`sed 's/[[:space:]]*$//' chapters/volume-01/chapter-00NN.md | wc -w`

**Two counts exist in this project's history, they do not agree, and only one of them is reproducible.** Earlier phases reported **7,944 for Chapter 10** and **7,007 for Chapter 20**, with batch totals of **43,815** (Batch 0001) and **50,028** (Batch 0002), and described that count as prose-only with headings and scene-break rules excluded. **That description cannot be right**, because all four of those figures are *larger* than the command's, and an excluded-heading count would be smaller; the differences are also not a constant per chapter, so no single convention accounts for them. **The cause is not established and is not guessed at here.** What is established is that the command above is the measuring method this project named, that it is reproducible from the files as they stand, and that **it governs. Chapter 10 is 7,919 and Chapter 20 is 6,986.** The prose is identical under either convention; only the arithmetic of the count is in question.

### The bands, and where each one comes from

Only four bands are written down anywhere in this project, and two of the five batches were never given one. That is stated here rather than papered over, because the outlier list below depends on it.

| 0001 | 2,200–3,200 a chapter; 22,000–32,000 a batch | the batch's own card file, `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0001.md`, which adds: *complete every scene, aftermath beat, and emotional turn even if a chapter runs longer* |

| 0005 | 3,200–3,800 for Ch 41–46; 2,800–3,400 for Ch 47–50; about 4,200 allowed for a hearing | recorded in the Batch 0005 "Notes for the next batch" |

### Every chapter outside the band its batch was given

**Batch 0001, against 2,200–3,200.** Inside: Chapter 2 (3,092) and Chapter 4 (2,914). **Outside: Chapters 1, 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9 and 10** — eight of ten, and a batch total of 43,785 against a 22,000–32,000 batch band. The reason is structural and was accepted in advance: this batch contains the public rehearing (6–8), the public aftermath and the collation of twenty-six copies (9), and the two-day public inventory, the refused settlement, the arrest and the nine-paragraph request (10). The card told the writer to finish every beat rather than hit a number, and the beats did not come apart.

**Batch 0002, against no band on the record.** Measured against the volume's only written per-chapter band, **2,200–3,200, all ten chapters are outside it**, and the batch total of 50,006 is the largest in the volume. The four that stand furthest outside are **Chapter 20 (6,986)**, **Chapter 18 (6,056)**, **Chapter 17 (5,921)** and **Chapter 19 (5,566)**. The reasons are the same four the volume keeps producing: 20 carries the batch climax (the rack, the circular, the run of nine hundred and forty, and the certification offer); 18 carries the rota, the child of seven, the household accounts, the signature and the lapse; 17 carries the eleven protected witnesses, the refused private settlement and the ledger statement; 19 carries two provincial files, a jurisdictional trick and a witness on the fourth column of a list. **Chapter 20 is the volume's second-longest chapter and the phase prompt's own named outlier.**

**Batch 0003, against no band of its own.** Measured against the **3,200–4,000** its own note set for the batch that followed, **eight chapters are inside and two are outside: Chapter 24 (3,153), 47 under, and Chapter 25 (4,772), 772 over.** Chapter 24 is the quietest chapter in the batch and Chapter 25 carries the batch climax. This is the batch that answered the length problem the two batches before it created, and it is the reason the last two batches are inside their bands.

**Batch 0004, against 3,200–4,200.** Nine chapters are inside. **Outside: Chapter 40 (4,659), 459 over.** It is the only chapter outside the batch's band and it was not padded: it carries the rate strike, the examination fee, the letter owed since the tenth month, the volume's second and last reading, the review, the refusal of the Dunn request with a date, the closing record, the copy list and the emotional close. It was cut from 5,415 by removal and compression in two repair passes and then grew again by about 120 words of copy-list detail that a canon check required.

**Batch 0005, against 3,200–3,800 for Chapters 41–46, 2,800–3,400 for Chapters 47–50, and about 4,200 for a hearing.** Inside: Chapters 41, 43, 44 and 45, and Chapters 47, 48 and 49. **Outside: Chapter 42 (4,007) by 207 over the pre-climax band; Chapter 46 (4,279) by 79 over the 4,200 hearing allowance; and Chapter 50 (3,933) by 533 over the 2,800–3,400 aftermath band.**

**Chapter 46 is over deliberately and by 79 words.** It is the volume climax: the caption read twice, the entry by device read aloud in full, the scale statement read in as a document and not cited, the registrar's note of five offices, four orders, the escalation, the objection, and the stair.

**Chapter 50 is over deliberately and it says so here, not quietly.** The canon lock requires that all five terms of the ruling be posted and be readable in one sheet, because four of the five are the terms a lane says by number and a reader who has heard four must be able to see the fifth. The review found that the chapter set out only the second term, a five-terms block was added, and the rest of the chapter was compressed to pay for it. **This is a one-off and it is not a licence.** A Volume 02 chapter that carries six required beats will overrun the aftermath band again, and the fix is a shorter chapter, not a longer band.

### What the length profile means for Volume 02

The profile is not random and it is not a writer's drift. **The chapters that ran long are the ones carrying a hearing, an inventory, a signature or a batch climax, and the chapters that ran short are the ones carrying one room and one decision.** Every chapter above 4,500 words in this volume is a chapter in which a document was produced, read aloud, entered or signed in front of witnesses: 7, 8, 9, 10, 12, 14, 17, 18, 19, 20, 25 and 40. Every chapter at or under 3,200 words is a chapter with a single scene and a single turn: Chapters 2, 4, 24, 47 and 49, and 47 and 49 are the two shortest because each is one clerk writing on a wall and one woman on a coach. Nothing in the manuscript is padded and nothing was split, because a hearing that is cut in the middle stops being a hearing. The working rule that follows is therefore **3,200–4,000 a chapter, 2,800–3,400 where a chapter is aftermath, and about 4,200 where a chapter is a hearing** — and the discipline that produced Batches 0003, 0004 and 0005 inside their bands is not restraint in the writing. It is **one room and one decision per chapter**, and putting a card's second beat at the top of the following chapter instead of doubling the one it was given.

## Contract Rules

- Ordinary plain and scale contracts lose force when terms, assent, witness, record, or continuation is missing; a defect does not automatically void every ordinary contract.
- A foundational compact binds only named ratifying powers to publicly stated duties. It cannot create a perpetual office, inherited debt, or beneficiary for an unnamed future population.
- The Cinder Clause is an unlawful later proxy practice, not a new compact or a sentient mechanism. Its use can be preserved as evidence and may ground restitution for harm, but it cannot enlarge the Compact or create a new inherited duty by itself.

## Rules Established On The Page In Batch 0003

Recorded because Chapters 21–30 turned the midpoint into institutions rather than findings, not because they are new mechanics.

- **A review date with no named person is not a review.** This is the volume's midpoint answer in one line, and it is what Marek accepts in place of a certified rule. A rule is survivable if a person is accountable for running the hearings under it and a date is fixed for looking at it again. A sentence with nobody under it is not a rule and is not safe; it is forty-six clerks each deciding alone.
- **A refusal can be a public act.** What makes it one is that the terms offered, the person who offered them, the person who declined, the date, and a witness are all entered, and the decliner signs the record of the refusal. The first version of Marek's reply — *the applicant does not accept and has no further observation* — was three sentences of sulking and would have read as a clerk who would not answer.
- **A standing office can have a seal, a series and a run and no person.** The **Standing Office of Provincial Continuities** is such an office. Its sheets are *issued*, not signed. The authority for the series is a line at the head of a district's flood standing instructions: *Issued under the standing authority of the Standing Office of Provincial Continuities… Printed forms of this series are issued under the same authority and no further authority is required for their use in a district holding a flood docket.* The authority for the circular is the drawer the circular is in, because the distribution line says the series is filed with the standing instructions and not with the papers. This is the volume's answer to whether the local precedent was invented or **issued**: issued, traceable to a series and a date and not to a man, and unanswerable to question three because there is nobody to name.
- **A procedure with no person standing at it is not a procedure.** The finding is that the new process is safe only while a named clerk is at a window; if the window shuts for the middle of the day a person's ask evaporates rather than failing, and she will not come back because nobody wrote to her.
- **The absence of a number is the entry.** A filing entered in the routine column gets no number, and a thing that gets no number cannot be found by anybody who has not got the book in front of them. In Chapter 30 the instrument that removed a cooper's gauge from a shop bench is a creditor's filled-in copy of the printed model, stamped by the landing office into the routine column in the tenth month, and the only record of it is a line in a clerk's book reading *guarantee, shop goods, bond, and the name of a district office.* The stamp is the whole crime and it took about four seconds.
- **A form with a run on it is a model; a form with a room on it is an instruction.** The Mosswake four-box form has **no number, no series, no seal and no office at the head of it.** The *entry in the book* is numbered, not the form. This is the deliberate structural opposite of MC/FS and it is why it can be used the next day and cannot be issued empire-wide.
- **A question asked of a paper is a tool, and tools travel.** The **four questions** in the lane are: *Is there work in it? Whose name is on it — yours, or a word that walks about, like the holder? If you stop, whose name answers for it? Who read this to you, and do they get paid if you sign?* The fourth is the one that decides whether the other three can be trusted, and it came from a woman of about sixty-five at the seed barns who would not give her name. Within three weeks of it going onto a chalk board it was in a letter from a second association in the next province and on the back of a bond receipt in a third district. The only difference from a printed form is that anybody may be asked about it.
- **A register of hearings is worth more than a register of duties, because it shows who came.** Both hang on the same board at the Mosswake clerk's window, in Sarn's two handwritings, at a shilling a copy.
- **A levy that buys a signature is a tax on doing nothing wrong.** The Greyfen craft levy of nine pounds a quarter falls hardest on the houses that took the amendment, and a house of forty years standing that has never had a dispute is being charged for a clause about a girl who leaves. Recalculated onto the four signing houses on paper, and the assessor who agreed it says in advance that it will be overruled within a year.

## Rules Established On The Page In Batch 0004

Recorded because Chapters 31–40 turned the volume's question about quiet into a finding and produced the batch's one permanent document, not because they are new mechanics.

- **A district that obeyed a form is not safe. It is quiet, and the quiet is the injury.** The absence of a document is not a defect in the system; it is the system working as designed, and it works because every person in the chain did exactly what the form asked. The volume's standing question — *who in the world is helped by quiet* — is **answered in the negative, in a furnace town, by a trade.** It is stated on the page in Iselle Oster's own hand on the twenty-first of the third month and in Tamsin Rook's marginal finding on the twenty-fourth of the second.
- **A trade that has never had a dispute is the association least able to say one.** The **Emberwatch Dyers' Combination** has a rule thirty-one years old, at the second leaf of its own book: a house may not write to a court, a guild, a factor's office, a district office, or a person it has not dealt with before, about an instrument, without the standing assessor's leave in writing first. It was written after three disputes in a year took half the trade's work off it. It works. It is not fear and nothing in Emberwatch is frightened.
- **The only trace a town has in a record is sometimes a clerk being more careful than his form required.** The Emberwatch endorsement was **itemised** and the word *Itemised* underlined for the next reviewer, by a clerk of that trade, correctly, because the instruction against itemising is a quarterly return distributed to district courts and landing offices **holding a flood docket**, and a dyers' trade in a furnace town does not hold one. The itemising is harmless and it is the reason the town exists in any file at all. **The clerk remains unnamed on the page and is not to be given a name in a later batch without a card.**
- **A machine does not have to be cruel or careless. It only has to be issued.** A true answer that nobody signed is unanswerable in a way an untruth is not, because the only thing a person can do with an untruth is prove it, and there is nothing here to prove. *Issued, not invented* was the volume's midpoint answer about a sentence; in Chapter 38 it is a shield, and the shield arrived in the exact wording Pell Vey dictated to a junior clerk in a gallery on the sixth of the twelfth month, which is how the practice works: **the man who teaches the sentence is not the man who sends it, and the man who sends it does not know his sentence is being used.**
- **A refusal entered in a room, in the same form, with a witness, and signed on the record of the refusal, is worth more than a silence.** Two such records now exist in this matter, both with **Pell Vey's name on them**, and the practice of signing the back of the record unasked was taught by Nevin Trost in the eleventh month and is now Marek's habit.
- **Giving things away has become Marek's most fluent move, and Tamsin Rook has named it as such.** He narrowed the certification request in October by his own signature, narrowed the audit request on the nineteenth of the tenth, and narrowed the limited-sitting application on the twenty-first of the second. On the twenty-sixth of the second she told him plainly that in October he hid a thing because he was frightened, in November because he had a plan, and this time because he is fluent about the moment. The rule she required — that either of them may put a choice in writing with a date on it, and the other must answer in a room, in words, with a date — is on the page and she has said she will check it in a year.
- **A notice of the person who holds can put a man in a box without anybody's hand on the pen.** The second paper at the Caudle house is a **notice** entered under the second instrument, with a box headed *the person who holds* and **Mike Caudle's name in it**, no signature, dated the second week of the twelfth month. The line under the box says the person named is not a party to the amendment, is not liable under it, holds no duty under it and enters into it of no obligation, and **the man on the rack put his thumb over that line** while reading the rest of the sheet out twice. It also demands **eight pounds** where the levy book says two pounds five and a quarter, and it is dated before the quarter that carries it is struck, which Marl Crenn said in the second week of the twelfth could not happen. Nobody can say who filled in the box. **No chapter has claimed this is the same thing as anything in the Venn file, and no chapter may.** Nell Kest wrote down on the fifteenth of the fourth month that she saw a resemblance, that she cannot prove it, and that the difference is that in the lane the man signed.
- **A question asked of a person with power works badly and does something else.** The fourth question asks *who read this to you, and do they get paid if you sign*, and an assessor is paid to read instruments to people. Asked in a room of eleven houses on the fifteenth of the fourth month, it produced one answer given clearly — *yes, I am paid to read them, and I am not paid for the answer* — and a refusal to be asked anything else, and nothing was decided, and eleven women went home knowing something they had not known at noon. The questions' value was never a case. It is that the room says out loud the thing that was being done in it quietly.

## Rules Established On The Page In Batch 0005

Recorded because Chapters 41–50 closed the volume's local promise and produced its route. These are the rules of the closing batch and a later batch may not soften them.

- **A person must be heard in a room and must sign before a guarantor duty attaches.** The Mosswake Roll Court said it on the twenty-first of the fifth, and the Cinder Court said it on the seventeenth of the sixth in a different form and binding on itself and on any court that takes a recognition from it: **no court in this empire may join a person as guarantor on the strength of a printed model of general wording without that person being heard and signing.**
- **A printed sentence is a record of a document and is not a record of a law.** The sentence of the twelfth of the ninth, as printed in the MC/FS amendment sheet of the twenty-second, is **model wording of general application and is not a decision of any court**, and may not be the recorded reason for a decision in any district without a hearing of the person it names. It is not a precedent, no district is bound by it, and no district is permitted to use it as a reason. **The district that stated it does not withdraw it, because only that district can withdraw its own practice.**
- **A seat is not a party.** Where a document bears the seal of a seat and no name, the thing that would answer it is entered by its device, and **no person of or belonging to that seat is party, cited or examinable, and no inference of any kind may be drawn in that proceeding from the fact that a person has not been named.** The form was drawn in the reign of the third of the Ashfall line, is used about twice in a decade, and its operative line was read aloud in a hearing four times in nine years, three of them by a person who was relieved. **A court that declines to name a person is not thereby wrong, and this is the only rule in the compact's own procedure that does any work on a record of nothing but names.**
- **A sworn statement made once may be read into a higher court as a document without being cited**, because the rule that forbids it is against being put to the same question twice *in another matter*, and a referral on the same record is the same matter. What enters the record is its existence, date, court, form, two boxes, the blank second box entered as complete so, and the fact that the swearer spoke only to her own duty and named nobody. **That is a fact about the record and not about the oath.**
- **A court may name its own failure and pay for it.** The Mosswake court entered that between the third month of the sixth year and the sixth year it applied that sentence in letters and without a hearing to **about sixty households in this district**, that it does not know their names and cannot get them in time, and that they are not before it and are not represented and have not asked to be. It also entered that **it will not order the district to do anything in place of the sentence, cannot, and that the district will have to work out in the sixth month what it does in a flood when it cannot use it** — a cost named so that nobody can say afterwards it was not known. **Those sixty households are owed a thing in Volume 02 or later and nobody in this volume has yet asked them.**
- **A number on a document is a fact about the book the document was entered in.** If two documents carry the same number, one of them is in the wrong book, and the person who can say which book is the person who keeps the books. **The Caudle notice is not from the Venn series, carries no substituted wording and no public-necessity sentence, and nothing in the Forms Received rack connects any two documents.** It comes out of **AS/2, a craft assessors' instruction on entering a second instrument, second year of issue, being the third year of the sixth year back, run four hundred** — four years and four months older than the Venn correction, about tool debts in a dyers' trade, and the reason a box headed *the person who holds* exists at all. **This is a fact about a number and a date. It is not a finding about a seam and must not become one.**
- **A distributed record is not a controlled record.** Of forty-one names, **twenty-nine say yes to keeping a copy of the Cinder Court's record and twelve say no**, and the twelve are the finding: four with a creditor or landlord or factor's man in the house, three who cannot read well enough to know what they are agreeing to keep, two with a damp room, one who burned hers and said why in full, one who has had a man with a tray at her door twice this year, and one who has eleven hundred pounds of debt behind her and thinks keeping a copy is a thing you do if you are going to be proved right. **A copy of a decision is a weapon and it is a target**, because the households that kept it are the households that can be shown to have had notice.
- **A list of who holds a copy is not a list of people who have reported**, and that rule now lives in **four hands and four pages that do not agree**, dated the eleventh of the seventh, with the disagreement written on all four. The holders are **Bryn Mawer at a chandler's counter in Candlewright Lane, Ivet Sarn, Wendt Sayer in a Reed Cut works book, and Nell Kest.** There is still no fifth holder and the Greyfen road copyist is not one of them.
- **The transmission mechanism of the whole case is a person who turns up.** A trade with one door, told by a woman with a bad hat and a carriage clock that the door is optional, will find out in about a year that it has four doors; it will cost nothing, leave no series and no run and no office, and in nine years nobody will be able to say which of the four doors was opened by her. The Emberwatch rule is **still in force and is still at the second leaf**, and Bettin Rahl has written **the fifth question and his own answer under his own rule**, in his own hand, on a page nobody has read for thirty-one years. The fifth question is *what a house does when the assessor says no.*
- **The first question a trade has not asked is the first question it will have to answer.** Emberwatch has had no dispute in forty years and has had four women sitting on four copies of a court ruling for nine months, and the reason is the same fact: a one-door trade is a trade where a person who says *I do not know* is never asked to say it twice in a room.

## The Flood-Season Continuity Process, As Established On The Page

This is an institution created in Chapters 22–23. It is the court's, the water office's and a register's, and it places no burden on any body.

- A **flood-season continuity filing** in a district that adopts the process is not categorised away and is heard **individually on request**, at a window, in the person's own words.
- The ask is written down, and the hearing is entered in a **public register of hearings** kept at the Mosswake clerk's window, in Ivet Sarn's hand, on the same board as the register of pending duties, at a shilling a copy.
- The **witness is not the applicant** and is not owed anything in the matter. A household that cannot name one has one named for it from a list of people who have agreed in advance to witness, and that person is told in advance in writing, may refuse, and is not held to it for refusing. A five-line **witness order** exists for this.
- The **cost ceiling is eleven shillings for six weeks**, found by the district works out of the works fund, and it buys the running of the window and the paper and nothing else. Exceeding it **stops the process** rather than continuing it unpaid, and the register records the day it stopped and why. Sarn has stated in writing that a process which costs money it cannot pay in money stops quietly and that there is no person to complain to.
- The **review is the fourth of the fourth month.** The flood docket closes and the **rate is struck on the sixth of the fourth month**, two days later, and the last relief roll must be certified on the sixth because that is the day it is struck. The review is the fourth; the rate is the sixth. Do not merge them.
- **The trial's accounting, as established in Chapter 34 and entered on a public wall.** The trial ran **the first of the twelfth month to the twelfth of the first month** with the certification window deliberately shut, so it cost the district nothing in rolls. It produced **thirty-four hearings**, **a hundred and two hours** of a six-hour window, and **nine shillings and fourpence** of the eleven stated. **Four** people said the words *I do not understand this* and are entered as having said it, which is a complete answer and not a refusal; **eleven** of the other thirty signed something else within a month, and both facts sit in the same box. Twenty-nine hearings were inside the window, three were at the seventh hour, and **two were at the ninth hour at night for the same woman, who works the channel**, and the witness slip for them was filled in by the lamp-trimmer because there was nobody else in the building. Sarn states on the wall, in the first person and with the day on it, that she asked the roll-keeper the question about running it outside the window **so that the answer would be in somebody else's hand and not hers**, and that no household in the district waited for a seed parcel because of the six weeks.
- **The review, as held on the fourth of the fourth month.** The magistrate read the wall aloud in her own words and read the third paragraph twice. It created **no precedent**, is **not binding on any district including this one**, **compelled nothing**, and the district may or may not run the process next season and will decide that itself. It is over. The **only** thing it produced is that nine people in the district now know where a hearing can be asked for and every one of them can prove it happened.
- **The rate was struck whole on the sixth of the fourth month.** Four hundred and sixty relief rolls, four hundred and sixty parcels, four hundred and sixty households, all certified. Hallis Dren wrote **two hundred and eighty-four** at the foot of the rate book in his own hand on that day. The figure was a price and it was never incurred.
- The cost, priced by Hallis Dren and counted aloud by Tamsin Rook at Sarn's window: one hearing is **three hours** and a shilling and sixpence for a witness; the district has **fifty-two** such filings in the season; fifty-two hearings is **a hundred and fifty-six hours**, which is **twenty-six days** of a six-hour window. The window is **the last forty-two days before the rate is struck — the twenty-fifth of the second month to the sixth of the fourth** — at about **eleven** relief rolls a day, which is four hundred and sixty-two against a season of **four hundred and sixty**: two rolls of slack in the whole season. Lose twenty-six days and **sixteen days of certification remain: a hundred and seventy-six rolls certified against four hundred and sixty needed, so two hundred and eighty-four households get their seed parcels off the rate late.** Those households eat in the wrong weeks. Dren has had the list of which two hundred and eighty-four in his head since the eighth month and has never shown it to anybody.
- **The two hundred and eighty-four is a price and not a bill, and the reply of the thirteenth says so in those words.** The funded six-week trial is run **outside** that forty-two-day window — from the **first of the twelfth month** to the **twelfth of the first month**, with the certification window shut — precisely so that no district is charged for the process before somebody has watched whether it works. Nineteen hearings were on the slate by the **second of the first month**. The arithmetic is therefore a price quoted in advance for a district that adopts the process and runs it inside a season, and every figure in it can be checked. **No chapter may state or imply that the two hundred and eighty-four households have already been delayed by the trial.**
- The process is **not a rule, is not a precedent, and applies to no file but the flood-season filings of a district that adopts it.** It does not cover the Venn term expiring in the second month, the Dunn barn term of the third month, the Sallow file of the fifth year, or the Greyfen guild instruments, and all four of those were named out loud before it was agreed.

## The Limited Scale Sitting, And The Sworn Statement

The batch's one permanent gain. Established in Chapters 32, 36 and 37, and canonical.

- **A limited sitting is not a hearing.** A hearing is asked for by a person with a grievance. A limited sitting is asked for by a person who has a document and has been told he cannot use it. It is granted because a court that will not hear a narrowed thing ends up hearing a wide thing.
- **What the Mosswake Roll Court granted, on the second of the third month, for the first week of the fourth month, in nine lines in Nevin Trost's hand:** it may hear a sworn statement made by a party to the instrument of the nineteenth of the second month of the year four years back as to **that party's own duty** under it, in a form the court sets; it may record what a party says about that party's own duty, enter it, and give the parties copies. **It may not** order disclosure of any document, **strike, void, amend or suspend** the correction filed on the ninth of the eighth month, **touch** the series MC/FS or any standing instruction of any district or any return made under any instrument sealed with the fire-and-water device, **be described afterwards** as an audit, a review, a hearing of the application, or a decision on any question, or **be cited** in any other matter. **On the applicant's own application** it may not be used to support any application not made in that court on that file. The seventh of the nine is Trost's own and he marked it as such.
- **The parties.** The Mosswake Roll Court and its clerk; the estate and household of Aldren Venn, and Mara Venn and Tavi Venn as the persons holding; the Reed Cut district water office as the party holding the gate schedule; the applicant. **The Standing Office of Provincial Continuities was struck off the list by Marek on Pell Vey's warning** — listing an office for notice on a sitting that cannot touch its series makes the file a series file in two years, and the audit of it will be about the series and will not be his audit.
- **A counterparty is notified and is not joined, and a dragon is not served.** The form is kept by the district water office that holds the book of scale instruments it has countersigned, is nineteen years old, is on the district's own paper, and carries the line *She is notified. She is not under the authority of this court and this court cannot require her to do anything.* It went out of Auremar on the thirtieth of the third month and up the water on the morning boat, and **Hallis Dren** found the page, stamped the copy that stays, and wrote the page number on it unasked.
- **A statement in scale form is made once.** The form drawn by the court is four leaves and has a number because it is an entry in the minute book and not a form, and there were four copies. It has two boxes: *the party swearing* and *the other party to the instrument*, and under the second one there is nothing but a rule and a note in the court's hand: *This statement is made once. A party who has made a statement in scale form in a matter cannot be asked to make another in the same matter, and cannot be asked to fill the second box. The second box is filled, if it is ever filled, by a person who was a party to the instrument at the time of the signing.* **The second box is blank and is entered as complete so**, in the keeper's own words and at the magistrate's direction, with the reason entered: the other party is Aldren Venn, who died in the fourth month after signing, and there is no proceeding under which that capacity returns. **That box is empty for ever.**
- **What Sivra Oris swore, on the third of the fourth month, at the twelfth hour, before the court, with the fixed benches carried out onto the terrace and the channel behind the window.** That she speaks to her own duty only and makes no statement as to any other person, any office, or what any person has done since, and has not seen the correction and will not describe it. That the instrument **created two duties and named the people who held them**: the field-side upkeep and the winter seed were Aldren Venn's, and the gate and the navigation were hers. That **neither of those duties is an office**, and a duty of hers is discharged by her and by nobody else, and the instrument says in the only words it had that whoever thereafter assumes a continuing field-side duty at that place shall sign a new obligation for it. That **the alteration did not alter either duty** — her gate has been shut and opened through six seasons and the district has paid the cost since the ninth month of the ninth year — and that **the alteration changed who was held by them**, by taking two people who cannot be occupied and putting in their place a word any person may stand in. That she has watched that word used in two district courts in seven years under her own seal's authority, unasked, and has no office to complain to that is not the office that did it, and has never asked this court for anything and is not asking it for anything today.
- **She named nobody and may not be made to.** She refused four things: the question of whether the holder of the field was in occupation or in possession (she does not see fields, she sees water); Marek's question about the winter seed (she does not know, and a receipt for water is not a statement that a man's debt is paid); a Notaries' Table man's request that she say which of two hands in the filed correction was a clerk's; and Tamsin Rook's request that she say the sentence about the word again, slowly, so a copyist could get it exactly.
- **The court's one sentence, entered by Leda Rell, magistrate, Mosswake Roll Court, on the third of the fourth month, with her name at the foot of it:** it cannot order the disclosure of any document; it cannot strike, void, amend or suspend the correction; it cannot touch the series MC/FS or any standing instruction of any district or any return made under any instrument sealed with the fire-and-water device; and **it cannot say, and does not know, and after four months of asking four offices and one district water office has not been able to find out, who authorised the instruction sealed with the device which stands upon the Venn file.** Nothing said in that room alters any of those things. **The statement is not a decision, is not a precedent, and may not be cited in any other matter, and the sitting is not an audit and may not be described as one.** A party to the matter may apply, on this record, **in the fifth month**, to the court named in the appeal clause for a scale instrument of that district, which is the **Cinder Court at Auremar**, and this court has no power to make that application and does not make it. **The escalation has not been taken.**

## The Promotion, And The Letter He Owed

- **The standing examiner of provincial filings** was offered by the head of the Notaries' Table on the **twenty-ninth of the third month, at the seventh hour, in the third gallery**. It is not a title: a district registrar's answer is **given to it and taken by it**, it has a room and a drawer that is not the cabinet, it is not a seal and not a commission and does not survive the building. The condition was that the correction filed on the ninth of the eighth month be treated as a routine filing defect and that no district's answer be framed by reference to a series in the corridor. **Marek refused it in a room with the intake clerk as witness, asked for the refusal in the same form as the twentieth of the eleventh, and signed the record of it unasked.** Pell Vey now has his name on a reply and **two** recorded refusals in this matter.
- **What the standing really was.** A standing examiner may issue an instruction to a district in his own hand, and a district holding a flood docket must comply, and it goes to forty-six places on the ordinary distribution. Marek wrote the whole of such an instruction in his own book on the evening of the **twenty-ninth** — no endorsement in the model form on or after the first of the current month to be treated as an individual filing unless itemised and numbered, and any endorsement of which the district has no number to be reported — **and filed it nowhere.** His recorded reason: *I would be able to. That is all. There is no principle in it.*
- **He also did not write to Nevin Trost about the ninety words.** His recorded reason: the rule he made with Tamsin on the thirteenth of the twelfth was designed to protect him and is now protecting Pell, and *a rule that only ever works in one direction is not a rule and is a preference.*
- **The letter to Mara Venn**, owed since the tenth month, read out at the Venn house on the **sixth of the fourth month** and entered. It says there were two ways to enter Aldren's file as complete and neither has happened. **The first** was ordinary — a clerk entering a summary's version over a filed leaf — and is refused, because a summary is what a later file copies from. **The second** needed somebody's word about the winter seed, and the only person who could give it swore a statement on the third of the fourth in a form made once with a box for the other party, and the other party is dead, **so Aldren Venn's file cannot be made complete and is not going to be.** Mara Venn's answer, out loud: she asked whether the field-side work was her husband's, was told it was, and then asked *what I am*, and was answered in a noun for the first time in six weeks.
- **Tavi Venn's canal examination** is arranged for the fifth month, is **not yet given**, is not in the fourth month because Odile Fenn's docket then is the Dunn review, and is paid for by **Wendt Sayer** out of the district works fund, **two shillings and sixpence**, entered under the head ***labour, examinations, engaged***, on the sixth of the fourth, as a **recruitment cost and not a kindness.** It is paid for neither by the Venns nor by Marek.

## The Second Roll-Answer

The only reading in Chapters 31–40, and it decided nothing.

- **Permission:** the **limited Venn audit** of the **twelfth of the ninth month**, issued in open court, **never discharged**, because closing it would have required somebody to write a conclusion and the conclusion was never the point. The reference is on the fifth term's schedule, which is nailed to the rail of the Reed Cut gate.
- **Taken:** on the **sixth of the fourth month**, at about the seventh hour, alone, on the Venn step, with the term expired for seven weeks.

> **Named:** the present holder of the Venn field.
> **Promised:** the present holder may be joined as guarantor until full review.
> **Carried after death:** nothing. The term is at an end and the record names no successor, and there is nobody in it to carry anything.

- **It decided nothing** and could not. It is three lines, it is accurate about a file, and it has no opinion about a woman with a gate. The finding it gave a clerk is that he has spent seven months on a file which now says nothing of its own accord, and that a file which says nothing cannot be made to say something by a clerk.
- **Cost, paid on the page:** ash and iron and something underneath it that he has never been able to name; a headache behind the left eye, worse in the second minute than the first; a nosebleed of about a teaspoon onto the rail of the gate in the rain, which is the part he has never written down in a book and has never told anybody about; and **two days of not being able to look at print.** He did not go to Doctor Perrin Ashe and his reason is on the page: there is nothing a penny buys and there is no medicine for this.
- **The reading's cost has been paid in full; the inability to lie lapsed on the page in Chapter 18 and does not return.** Chapter 36 uses the fact privately, and uses it as a cost — he is fluent at choosing the moment — and it is not a mechanic.

## The Scope Decision And Tamsin's Four Lines

- **The decision of scope arrived in the copying alcove on the ninth of the fourth month.** One page, the file number is the one the intake clerk wrote in ink in September, a seal on the corner, and **Scope: not granted** in plain form in the middle. It answers two of the five questions and refers three to the standing instructions of the applicant's own district. **There is no name at the foot of it.** It is not a refusal, not *unknown*, not *not held*, none of the words an office can be asked about twice. The second paragraph is **word for word the sentence Pell Vey dictated to a junior clerk in a gallery on the sixth of the twelfth month**, and Pell Vey has never seen the page and does not know who read it.
- **Tamsin Rook filed four lines over her own signature as a roll-keeper of the third gallery, on the ninth of the fourth month**, in her own name and not on the applicant's, on the ground that *a copyist is the only person in this building with nothing to protect.* She names **the district of Glasswater** and not the standing office, because a district has a clerk and the office has no person. She asks that district to state in writing, by a date, what its standing instructions say about the itemising of a filing made under flood authority, and offers a shilling for the copy and a shilling for the letter. It is **unanswered.**
- **Her objection is no longer in Marek's coat pocket.** She took it back on the ninth of the fourth and put it in her own book between two pages of the Millbrook account, and her reason for not moving it is on the page: filing anything about a dragon seat's paperwork in the fourth month, on the day an unanswerable answer has landed, would merge the two documents in the file, and the first one would stop being a Chancery standing office's. **The First Seat quarterly return is still not in any reply, and there is still no document anywhere connecting the two.**

## The Independent Copy List

- **Forty-one holders**, listed in Tamsin Rook's own hand in her own book: who holds a copy, in whose hand, of what, dated, and whether the holder has agreed to read it to anybody who comes to the house with a paper. Only three things are ever held: the five operative terms of the ruling of the twelfth of the ninth, the reply of the thirteenth of the twelfth, and the four questions. There is no fourth thing and there will not be one.
- **There are six things on a line and she has only ever filled five.** The sixth would be *why not*, and she will not invent it, because a list of guesses in a copyist's private book is a list of lies with a date on them. Recorded so that a later batch does not quietly add the sixth column.
- The list is **not distributed.** It is what one person has in order to know who to write to. It lives in a chest under a winter coat and **nobody in the Chancery knows it exists.**
- The cost was **two shillings and ninepence** of paper, twine and courier (a shilling and ninepence of paper, a penny of twine, elevenpence of courier, which is thirty-three pence), and eleven nights, out of her own wages. She refused a quire out of the Chancery stores, because an office's paper in a district is a list with an office's name on it and the whole value of the thing is that it has none.
- It failed on money before it failed on courage. The Greyfen press charged four pence a sheet and said why: the six free copies of the ninth month were anger and not charity.
- Two holders are not keepers. The woman in the hood refuses, and gives the reason, which is that a list saying *the seed-barn copyist* is a description of a person and there are four women in the seed barns. She made **four** of the twenty-six public copies in the ninth month and a **fifth** in the twelfth, which is five of the forty-one; the fifth is a later addition and must not be confused with the four. Ostyn Vare has stopped answering letters after his audit of the **seventh of the twelfth** and has not been chased, because a list of who holds a copy is not a list of people who have reported.
- She wrote to **thirty-eight** of the forty-one, having no address for three. **Thirty-one answered inside a month. Ten did not answer**: seven of them written to and three never asked. Four of the seven are in Emberwatch, in a dyers' trade, in the house that sent a copy of an endorsement to a lane in the tenth month unsigned.
- **As at the tenth of the fourth month: thirty-two answers and nine non-answers.** A man in Greyfen who had moved came back on the **fourth of the third** with a new address and an apology. Of the nine: a woman in Greyfen with four children and no candle; one in Reed Cut who stopped answering after the seventh of the twelfth; a man who had been in a bed in the ninth month and whose wife promised he would answer when he was up; the house in a row off a Kiln road where the copy is in no drawer at all, in a house that is not the house it was given to; **one man never asked at all, because there was never an address for him**; and **four who are one house in Emberwatch**, in a lane with no numbers. The four carry **the surname Ferren and a landmark and no first names** in the fourth column, which is all Bettin Rahl's rider of the twenty-fourth of the second could give. **Osa Ferren is not on the list and cannot be**: her first name reaches the Greyfen lane on the sixteenth of the fourth, six days after Chapter 40 ends. The column was **not tidied** and no non-responder was moved, and Tamsin has written in the margin that who in the world is helped by quiet is *answered in Emberwatch, in the negative, by a trade, on the twenty-fourth of the second month, and the answer was not fear.*
- **Four letters addressed to that lane are in a drawer in her alcove and have not been sent.** She told Marek about the rider-post letter to the assessor in the next province on the twenty-sixth of the second and did not tell him about the drawer, and said the same afternoon that she had decided to tell him in about three months and not one word sooner, and that the number of things she was not telling him had gone up by one on purpose.
- **Tamsin went up the water a second time**, from the sixteenth to the twenty-eighth of the third month, out of her own wages again, and did not go to help.

## The Reply Of The Thirteenth

- Dated the **thirteenth of the twelfth**, signed **Pell Vey, Notaries' Table**, entered in the Examinations file, and enclosed in a covering paragraph written and signed by Nevin Trost which states that a certification was offered on the ninth of the eleventh, was declined on the twentieth, is withdrawn, and that the office is under an obligation to state its terms to every district on the distribution.
- It went to the **ordinary distribution — the forty-six district courts and landing offices holding a flood docket**, which is the same list the circular went to. It had to: an office cannot offer a certification to forty-six places and decline it in one of them.
- It states four things: the offer, the refusal, the process, and the cost, including the eleven shillings and the hundred and fifty-six hours, on the ground that a person who refuses the cheap thing is entitled to be asked what he is offering instead.
- It **does not answer the nine paragraphs and does not withdraw them.** The request is alive, and the reply asks to have it answered by a person whose name is written at the foot of the answer, before the fourth month, and not by a date.
- The **First Seat quarterly return is deliberately not in it**, on Marek's argument and against Tamsin's written objection, which she tore out and folded into his coat's inside pocket and has told him she intends to collect.

## The Millbrook Reach Household

- **Iselle Oster**, forty-one, of Millbrook Reach, three hundred miles down the water, a cooper's widow doing her own trade since the seventh year. The shop is Oster and Son with the *and Son* painted over and not painted out. The son, **Dain Oster**, twenty-two, has been out of the trade for a year because the trade went out of the house.
- **The tool lost is the master's hoop gauge**, the guild-stamped iron gauge the master holds, without which no hoop can be fitted to the trade standard in that district and no apprentice can be examined for the trade. It went on the **fourth of the twelfth month**.
- **Tace Oster**, sixteen, is in the surety line of the instrument and signed nothing. She gave the fourth question twice and gave it differently the second time: the first answer was her mother's, the second was hers, and hers was that the man who read it was paid by the man who wanted the gauge.
- The instrument is a **creditor's filled-in copy of the printed model**, stamped by the landing office in the routine column in the tenth month with no number against it, and the creditor's man took the gauge on the fourth of the twelfth under it. Iselle Oster did not know her daughter was in the surety line until the man came about the gauge.
- **Sabra Ollett**, forty-one, clerk of the Millbrook Reach landing office, nine years, stamped it in about four seconds and cannot produce a document. She told Tamsin Rook the truth about the stamp before she let her in and has thought about those four seconds every day since the ninth of January.
- Tamsin wrote the account in a **register of her own, opened outside any court's process**, with no seal, no office and no authority, whose first page states in full that it has none and that nothing in it is evidence and that any person may read it or write in it. Iselle Oster and Tace Oster have both signed it in their own hands.
- This is the **third district** to be confirmed, and it is confirmed **by household account and not by document.**

## Rules Established On The Page In Batch 0001

These are recorded because Chapters 1–10 turned planning into text, not because they are new mechanics.

- **The summary is the door.** A summary is what a later file copies from. Compressing a packet is ordinary practice and is where an alteration survives; a court acting on a summary acts on the copy, not on the source leaf. The Chapter 8 reading is the proof of this in the reader's hands.
- **A witness mark is a device with a copy in it.** A notch in the left margin records who was standing in the witness line. Two means two were heard. A third is cut at a different angle because the third person stood differently. A notch that is cleaner than the original, or at the wrong height, is a copied notch. Third notches were sold as a service, at a fixed height, for a fixed fee.
- **A true seal is laid, not pressed.** A true seal is made from a scale impression, spoken assent, and a witness record; it takes about eleven seconds and the holder is present throughout. Its rim is uneven. A cold press mark in a workshop with a screw press is a copied seal, and a copied image is not a true seal. This is how Corvin's certificate is broken.
- **A general paper can shelter a particular one.** A district flood memorandum that authorizes bank works and names no parcel is a real document, and anything filed under it in the following day inherits its standing. The memorandum dated the eighth of the eighth month and the correction received on the ninth of the eighth month are a day and a night apart, and the memorandum is the schedule, not the authority.
- **A form with a run on it is not a person.** *Continuity Filings, Flood Seasons, Categorisation* requires a flood-season continuity filing to be entered in the routine column and not itemised or heard individually except on quarterly review. It does nothing and creates nothing. It arranges that a class of correction arrives already inside a rule. Its print run implies every court that files a flood docket, which is the Volume 01 volume-level question and is not answered in this batch.
- **A public-necessity sentence travels by being quoted.** A court that needs to act fast in a flood will say a general thing, and clerks in a hurry will copy it. The sentence is the first portable institutional wording of the inheritance echo; the copied Dunn summary shows the vehicle before the court supplies the authority; the authority then makes it operative. This is the mechanism Marek's narrow win turns into a second household.
- **A reading is only evidence once a recognized body admits the record.** The Chapter 8 reading was taken against the exhibit as the court had just amended it, with the ruling in the same sleeve, and it was entered in the minute book and copied into the widow's hand by Sarn's direction on Marek's request.
- **Ordinary competence defeats a forger.** The witness order was exposed by memory plus a register that lacked a third issue plus a raking lamp. The broker's missing leaves were exposed by a docket count and a boy's habit. The file number was preserved by a young intake clerk writing it in ink and initialling it. None of this is magic and all of it wins cases.
- **A duty pending full review is a duty.** A postponement is enforceable enough for a creditor to name, to hold security against, and to put a number in a demand on, and the demand does not wait for the review. This is why a postponement can be worse for a household than a refusal, and why Rell says so in open court on the twenty-third.
- **A public register of pending duties is a real and cheap protection.** Kept at the Mosswake clerk's window in Sarn's hand, with a date, a household, a duty, the next review, and a shilling for a copy. It protects nobody by itself and it puts every named household where creditors, neighbours, and other clerks can read.
- **A public reading table becomes a lane institution, and the lane uses the ruling without being taught.** The rota is four named readers on a levy sheet nailed to the table leg, with a chalk rule that a paper may be brought there and read out loud and no advice given. In Chapter 18 a child of seven is sent to ask for the fourth term to be read at a house before a paper is signed.
- **A clerk may admit an exhibit on her own authority, and a copy cannot be recalled.** A district court's clerk does not need the magistrate to admit an exhibit in a matter of record; exhibits are copied to the parties on the day of admission; a copy that has gone to a party cannot be un-copied, and an order striking the exhibit is an order about the *use* and not an erasure. This is the mechanism Tamsin uses in Chapter 19.
- **A small truthful number is more effective than a big lie.** The First Seat form is a quarterly *return* with four boxes. It does not say what the answers are and it does not tell anybody to write a zero. It works because the first paragraph puts flood-season continuity filings in a routine column, so the honest answer is nearly always a very small number. Hallis Dren answered one and yes, honestly, on the eleventh of the tenth month, and sent it four hundred miles in a sealed sleeve, and does not know what he did. **No character in the story knows this yet.**
- **A model circular is a form, and a form travels without anybody wanting it to.** MC/FS is the proof: a good clause drafted for one household becomes a numbered form that is signed by frightened people in four houses on a lane and then travels to four hundred and forty miles of furnace towns. Marl Crenn says it on the page in Chapter 15.


## Succession and the Cinder Proxy

- The original First Custodian is a human, temporary public office, limited to seven years, with no enforceable inherited asset or right at appointment. Halden Ro is the sitting lawful Custodian in the final crisis; his term expires during the convocation.
- The office is chosen at a public succession convocation by at least five of nine human provinces, five of nine dragon Seats, and three of six independent civic assemblies. The First Seat may call a convocation but cannot appoint alone.
- During a vacancy, the Emberroot Interim Keepers are two human record keepers, one dragon notary, and one civic witness selected by the affected local court, recognized dragon seat, and nearest civic assembly. They may maintain existing duties and immediate relief for no more than forty-five days, but they cannot create or transfer a perpetual duty, appoint a successor, or invoke the Cinder proxy route. The First Seat alone cannot select all of them or fill the office.
- The Cinder route pairs a First Seat seal with a human who has a recorded inherited claim, names the human a proxy guarantor, and claims control behind the proxy. Seryn Oris cannot hold the original human office; she seeks permanent First-Seat control behind a controlled proxy.
- After Halden's term expires, the First Custodian is vacant. Only a new 5/5/3-of-6 convocation could appoint a temporary successor; the final Covenant records that none will be appointed and transfers the maintenance function to rotating Roll Assemblies and a bounded Interim Continuity Council.

## Blank Heir and the Kest Record

- Original eligibility: Marek is human and the Kest property-and-debt record never became an enforceable asset or right because its claimant assent and settlement were never validly completed. He already satisfies the lawful blank-heir test, including after Ilyra's name is restored.
- Cinder proxy test: the recorded but disputed Kest property-and-debt route can be misused to identify a proxy, but it is not an enforceable asset and does not make Marek a rightful inheritor.
- Final release: the Kest record is opened publicly, each affected person receives an independent hearing, and Marek voluntarily releases his prospective share and the route as a proxy. The history, debt, and restitution duties remain. He does not erase the record, and the release does not disqualify every other family's claim; it removes the specific prepared nomination tied to him. The clause is defeated by the ratification ruling and mixed hearing, not by renunciation alone.
- Marek's final supernatural cost is surrendering eligibility for a sole foundational office and exclusive access under a true seal. Any later true-name reading requires a witnessed, revocable mixed warrant with a recognized civic body and dragon notary.

## Final Quorum

- Human provinces: Mosswake, Greyfen, Salt Teeth, Cinder Marches, Glasswater Delta, Sunward Downs, Emberwatch Uplands, Bellward Craftlands, Thornfen Pastures.
- Final Covenant votes: Mosswake, Greyfen, Salt Teeth, Cinder Marches, and Glasswater Delta; Sunward Downs and Emberwatch Uplands refuse during the first year; Bellward Craftlands and Thornfen Pastures abstain pending reform.
- Dragon votes: Oris, Aven, Ilex, Merrow, and Rusk; Vell and Nera favor a single custodian; Sorn and Thal accept only a temporary emergency office.
- Independent civic leg: six assemblies total. Craft Assessors' Guild, Riverkeepers' Mutual Ledger, and Public Roll Copyists' Compact vote; Northern Waystation Council, Freeport Dock Assembly, and Temple Carekeepers' Concord abstain during the first year.

## Volume 1 Case
