- Aldren Venn signed a fixed seven-year water-and-field instrument. Mara witnessed the original and did not sign a later continuation, correction, or inheritance stipulation.
- The later correction substitutes “the present holder” for the named signer and routes Aldren's winter-seed debt to the field's current occupant. The substituted sentence on the page reads, in part, that the field-side upkeep and the winter-seed repayment, “being a work of the ground and not of a person,” shall be held against the field, the present holder answering for the same “until full review, the named signer having ceased.”
- The original leaf, witness marks, ink, seal pressure, and filing sequence prove the correction was added later. Tamsin preserves a comparison copy. Established on the page: the leaf carries Aldren's double-reed shorthand, two ragged notches, an embossed signature, and the requirement that anyone assuming a continuing duty sign a new obligation. The back of the correction leaf carries a certificate impression pressed flat and even and a fee entry naming a broker at Candlewright Lane in Auremar, which Marek has on the page in Chapter 4 and cannot yet use.
- **The ruling of the twelfth has five operative terms, and the numbering is canon.** (1) Tavi's personal debt is void. (2) The channel and lower field are preserved to Aldren's household until the second month, when seven years from the signing are up, against any creditor, tenant, surety, or assignee. (3) Sivra Oris's gate and navigation duty is unaffected. (4) **"The fourth term" is the new-signature rule:** any person who thereafter assumes a continuing field-side duty at that place must sign a new obligation for it, the original instrument requiring it. (5) **"The fifth term" is the district works:** the district water office must perform the bank works and the gate reset under its own flood authority and not under the instrument, and the household is not required to perform the field-side upkeep for the purposes of the order. Later batches must not call these three terms and four terms; there are five, and the lane already reads them aloud by number.
- The fifth term is **Tavi's ask**, made at the step of the Venn house on the evening of the tenth, and Rell put it last on purpose so that the last thing read out of the room is an instruction to an office rather than a sentence about a woman. She told Marek not to be thanked for it in front of the lane, because a magistrate who is said to have been fetched by a girl with an iron bar will be waited for and not found. This is the beat that Chapter 11 must collect: Tavi foresees, out loud, that a girl who has cut a ditch unpaid for five months will be read as having accepted the duty the fourth term requires a signature for.
- The court's broad public-necessity sentence is applied to Orra and Nessa Dunn: “Where immediate public necessity requires a recorded continuation to prevent loss of food or shelter, the present holder of the burdened property may be joined as guarantor until full review.” The Dunn summary is the vehicle; the sentence supplies the authority that makes it operative. This is the Volume 1 mini-arc reversal and the first visible inheritance echo. The formal volume midpoint arrives in Chapters 19–25, when the sentence is traced to a higher administrative template. **On the page:** the sentence is read aloud at two o'clock, and at two o'clock, in the same minute, the Mosswake registrar enters and files the previously unfiled Dunn summary on the authority of it. The five operative terms are not ruled until twenty past three. The reversal is therefore slightly worse than a later batch might assume: the summary was made operative by the sentence **before** the court finished ruling on the file the sentence was written for.
- The Venn instrument was signed in the **second month of the year four years back** and runs seven years from that month, so it expires in the second month. It entered its fourth year when Aldren died, which left three years and one flood season of balance at his death; the batch falls in the ninth month, so rather less than that remains by the ruling. **The ruling does not state a duration. It preserves the field until the second month, when seven years from the signing are up.** Later batches must use the date, not a counted balance, and must not say that the Venns have three years in front of them. “For the fixed term” is therefore a real limit, not a formality.
- **The Sallow finding.** Ivet Sarn's four days are now a dated, named, documented fact and not a boast. The file was an upper-district barn term in the fourth month of the **fifth year**; its summary carried the substituted wording **word for word**; a minor of the household was in the surety line; Rennie Sallow came to the window twice on the fourth day and once on the fifth and was told the docket was not closed and not why; Sarn bought the seed at eleven pounds over the price; and Sarn told him herself, in her own words, on the thirtieth of the ninth month, at the top of the upper lane, with Marek Kest standing at the bottom of the lane for five hours and not permitted up. The record carries the reason and the consequence together, entered on the **fourth of the tenth month**. The wording is four *years* older than the Venn correction, which is the volume's standing finding: this is a practice, not a fraud, and there is no wrong party in the room for a minute book to rule on.
- **The Venn term expired on the nineteenth of the second month with nothing decided, and the term's ending is now on the page.** The household has a house, four quarters of the upper holding, two rooms over a shop, and a gate; **Mara Venn has said out loud that a gate is not a field**, which is a fact and not a threat, and the letter of the sixth of the fourth month does not pretend otherwise. The fifth term's schedule is still nailed to the rail of the Reed Cut gate under the water office's own authority. **Aldren Venn's file cannot be made complete and is not going to be**, because the ordinary route was refused in the ninth month and the only good route needed a water keeper's word about the winter seed, and a scale statement is made once and its other party's box is empty for ever. The reading taken on the sixth of the fourth month reports that the record now carries nothing.
- **The ruling's stated limits, which later batches must respect:** the ruling decides the Venn file and nothing else. It does not void the correction, does not open other files, and does not name who wrote the sentence. Its reach is exactly terms four and five — the new signature and the district works — and neither of those is a general rule for other files.

## The Two Documents: The Mechanism, Established In Chapters 16 And 20

Canon. Later batches must not merge them, must not make one the child of the other, and must not find a document connecting them that does not exist on the page.

**Document one, the circular — the words.**
*Model Endorsements for Continuity Filings (Flood Seasons).* Series **MC/FS**. Sealed by the **Standing Office of Provincial Continuities**, a standing office of this Chancery whose authority is not established anywhere on the page. Distribution line: *to all district courts and landing offices holding a flood docket, to be filed with the district's standing instructions and not with its papers.* Base issue of the **second month of this year**, run **five hundred and sixty**. Amendment sheet dated the **twenty-second of the ninth month**, run **nine hundred and forty**, which is ten days after the Mosswake ruling and which carries a form of words in which the third line is the public-necessity sentence **verbatim**, under a note reading *Adopted from a provincial court, the ninth month. A court may adopt this wording or any other.* The model endorsement carries blanks for the district, the instrument, the ground and the person. It lives in the **Forms Received rack** in the passage behind the third gallery, which is unlocked and indexed by the sending office rather than by what the things are. Tamsin has pulled thirty-one sheets; twenty-two carry the four lines; the oldest is older than the ninth year before last; the first issue is not in the building.

**Document two, the First Seat form — the counting.**
*Continuity Filings, Flood Seasons, Categorisation.* Four paragraphs, sealed with the old fire-and-water device of the First Seat, no office name, issued this year. Paragraph 1: a flood-season continuity filing shall be entered in the routine column and not itemised, for the purpose of the quarterly return. Paragraph 2: it shall not be itemised, scheduled, or heard individually except on quarterly review. Paragraph 3: the court is not obliged to examine such filings individually, and no request by an affected person for individual hearing shall of itself require departure. Paragraph 4: it is not a direction as to validity and confers no authority and takes none away. **It is a permission, not a crime, and its correct use is administrative.** It contains no model wording and does not need to.

**How the two work together without touching.** The circular supplies the sentence in a form a clerk can fill in. The form supplies the reason not to look at the filing. A small truthful number on a quarterly return is more useful to the machine than a big lie, because a lie gets caught. What joins them is a practice across two offices in two buildings four hundred miles apart, and there is **no document anywhere in the empire that says the First Seat issued the form because the office printed the circular**, and no character knows that both exist except Marek Kest and Tamsin Rook.

**Ages, which must not be confused.** The four substituted lines (the *present holder* wording) are in the Sallow file in the fourth month of the **fifth year** and are in the series long before that. The public-necessity sentence was stated on the **twelfth of the ninth month** and printed on the **twenty-second of the ninth**. No chapter may put the sentence in an older file, and no chapter may put the four lines in a newer form than the one already traced.

## Volume 1 Chronology Established On The Page

Use these dates rather than inventing new ones. The whole batch falls inside **one month, the ninth**, and the days below are the days of that month. Months and days are different things: the Venn correction was received on the **ninth of the eighth month**, and the batch itself runs from the **seventh to the eighteenth of the ninth month**.

- **Seventh:** Marek works the Venn packet in the Auremar Roll Hall, finds the third witness notch, and keeps the summary out of the courier's satchel. Pell refuses to countersign the withholding slip on the evening docket, so the packet goes north uncertified and without a summary, and the slip is still unsigned. Marek and Tamsin take the river boat that night.
- **Eighth:** Reed Cut and the Mosswake court. Tamsin takes Sarn's refusal in writing, dates it, witnesses it, and pins it up where the tallies go. Sarn gives the great caution of the fifth year and then, once, the four days. Tamsin indexes the received tray and copies the unfiled Dunn summary. In the evening Marek sends the Venn packet back down the river with the morning courier, because a packet left in a district tray over a flood weekend is a packet the Table certifies on Monday out of habit.
- **Ninth:** The Notaries' Table. The packet comes back by the internal tray at the seventh hour. Marek turns over the back of the correction and finds a certificate impression and a broker's fee entry naming Candlewright Lane, a name he cannot yet use. Pell concedes the correction is probably bad, refuses to open the cabinet, promises to sign the comparison request in the first hour the next morning, and does not sign it, because a comparison cannot be had by a man who is not standing in the room when the sleeve comes out of the cabinet and Marek left for Mosswake the same evening. **Pell retracts that promise himself, in open court, on the twelfth**, and says it was as much Marek's fault as his, and that he has spent nineteen years making sure only one of those is written down. The request is still unsigned at the end of the batch. The provincial hold is posted on the board: sealed, not to be enforced, removed, or assigned, on a reference that carries no office name. In the evening at the Greyfen dye-house, Nell finds the summary in the satchel and demands the hidden draft.
- **Tenth:** The hold's effect is explained. Rell offers the closed order on the emergency stair; Marek refuses it and takes the public rehearing. Tamsin reads the whole file on the stair. Sarn sends her own rider to Auremar at the seventh hour on the court's custody slip. **That evening, at the step of the Venn house, Tavi names the fifth term** — the gate reset and the west bank work put on the water office in writing, with the household expressly not required to do the field-side — and says out loud that a girl who has cut that ditch unpaid since the fourth month will be read as having accepted the duty the fourth term requires a signature for. Later that night Marek and Tamsin walk the channel and he promises not to use the Venn family as a route to the network in the first hour.
- **Eleventh:** The water-office crew is due at the Reed Cut gates for the inspection. The witness order for the twelfth goes up on the Mosswake court board at the eighth hour, and Tamsin finds the order is dated the ninth and drawn at eleven.
- **Twelfth:** The rehearing. The witness order is challenged and the raking lamp is used; Rell reads the face of the hold aloud and the First Seat device is named in open court; the source leaf is produced out of Sarn's strong box at ten in a Table sleeve and entered in the minute book by the court's direction; Pyle's certificate is broken; a limited audit is issued; the public-necessity sentence is read at two and the Dunn summary is filed on it in the same minute; the ruling is made at twenty past three in five numbered terms; and the reading is taken.
- **Thirteenth to sixteenth:** The reading table stands four days in the Venn lane; twenty-six copies are made and distributed, nine by Tamsin, six in Greyfen, and eleven in the lane; the red stitch is found in the collation on the sixteenth. The separate Dunn sitting is requested for the **twenty-third**.
- **Seventeenth to eighteenth:** The two-day inventory of Corvin Dray's office in Candlewright Lane, on a joint order of the seventeenth; the chain opened; the form found; an unnamed man giving his name as a firm offers the four Venn documents back out of the inventory, free and unsigned, at the eighth hour of the second day and is refused on the record by the applicant and the constable together; the arrest at the sixth hour of the second day; the nine-paragraph audit request signed and dated the eighteenth and taken into Examinations custody with a stamped number, its first three paragraphs already drafted in pencil on the back of a seed tally while the ledger was being copied.

### Batch 0002 calendar, the nineteenth of the ninth to the ninth of the eleventh

Use these dates rather than re-deriving them. **Weekday names are not used to anchor anything in Batch 0002 and should not be used to anchor a chain in a later batch.**

- **Nineteenth and twentieth of the ninth:** Chapter 11. Dren's posted schedule goes up on the gate rail on the twentieth; the crew arrives on the twentieth; Tavi hands the bar back at noon.
- **Twenty-third of the ninth:** Chapter 12. The separate sitting. Rell's own limitation is entered, dated the twenty-third, that the court will not apply the sentence again on its own motion without a separate hearing and the signature of a named person who has been heard.
- **Twenty-fourth to twenty-seventh of the ninth:** Chapter 13. Orra goes to four creditors; she asks for the register on the third day; the packet boat brings a woman in a river court's grey coat on the fourth day.
- **Thirtieth of the ninth to the fourth of the tenth:** Chapter 14. The bundle-coat woman at the Sarn window on the thirtieth; the Sallow record entered on the fourth of the tenth.
- **Ninth and tenth of the tenth:** Chapter 15. The assessor's room on the ninth; the amendment into the rack on the tenth; the levy of nine pounds a quarter across four houses.
- **First to the eleventh of the tenth:** Chapter 16. The Pell Vey yard conversation on the **fourth**; the chain opened and the second sealed page entered without being opened on the **eighth**; the thirteen warnings sent on the **ninth**; Hallis Dren's quarterly return answered on the **eleventh**.
- **Eighteenth and nineteenth of the tenth:** Chapter 17. Bryn Mawer's supplementary sheet written on the eighteenth; the district's settlement offer refused and the plea refused and entered on the eighteenth; Corvin Dray's statement taken on the **nineteenth**; the narrowing amendment signed on the nineteenth.
- **Twenty-third to thirtieth of the tenth:** Chapter 18. Marek copies at the vats on the twenty-third; the rota is nailed up on the **twenty-seventh**; the child of seven on the **twenty-eighth**; the wax tablet on the evening of the twenty-eighth; Nery Caudle signs on the **twenty-ninth**; Wenna Sallow's question and the lapse on the **thirtieth**; the confession in the workroom on the evening of the thirtieth; the two letters at the lane table at the fourth hour of the thirtieth.
- **The route of the two letters, fixed.** The Glasswater sheets were posted by Perry Ostrow on the **twenty-ninth**, came off the packet boat at the **Mosswake landing on the thirtieth**, and went up the lane to the reading table that evening, where they were read out before they were put down. Tamsin had left a **standing instruction with the table** before she left Mosswake — anything arriving with nobody's name on it comes to the third gallery at the Roll Hall, because she put the table where it was — so on the **first of the eleventh** the two originals were posted back down to Auremar in a sleeve with a copy of each pinned inside it, and reached the third gallery on the **second**. There is one route and one delivery, not two: the boat to the Mosswake landing, the lane to the table, the sleeve to Auremar. The lane kept its copies, which is what the rota is for.
- **Second to fourth of the eleventh:** Chapter 19. The Glasswater letter reaches Auremar on the second; Tamsin writes to Sarn on the second; the scene is the **third**; the exhibit application is signed and dated the third and goes down the river that night; Sarn admits the two exhibits on her own authority on the **fourth** and the copies issue the same day; Sarn's stamped note goes up on the fifth.
- **Eighth and ninth of the eleventh:** Chapter 20. The passage behind the third gallery on the eighth; the letter to Rell written and not sent on the eighth; the supplementary request filed at the eighth hour of the evening of the eighth; the Received Room meeting on the **ninth**.
- **Still ahead at the end of Batch 0002:** the **second month**, when the Venn term expires; the **fourth month**, when the flood docket closes, the Dunn review is decided, the Caudle levy and the Greyfen assessment both bite, and Odile Fenn's examination of Tavi comes. The answer to the original nine-paragraph request is due inside three months of the eighteenth of the ninth, that is by the **eighteenth of the twelfth**, and has not arrived; Examinations has instead granted a thirty-day extension and answered with an offer.

### Batch 0003 calendar, the eleventh of the eleventh to the twenty-second of the second month

Use these dates rather than re-deriving them. **Weekday names are not used anywhere in Batch 0003 and must not be used to anchor a chain.**

- **Eleventh to fifteenth of the eleventh:** Chapter 21. Dren compiles the district's arithmetic on the eleventh, twelfth and thirteenth, the foreman's correction on the thirteenth, the four copies sealed and posted by hand on the **fourteenth**, and the fourth column read aloud by candle to his mother that night. The letter reaches the third gallery of the Roll Hall on the **fifteenth** and is put on the same table as the unsigned offer.
- **Fifteenth to twenty-first of the eleventh:** Chapter 22. The refusal is spoken, entered, read back and signed on the **twentieth** at the second hour of the afternoon, in the Received Room, with Trost and the intake clerk. Trost's covering paragraph is written the same week.
- **Twenty-sixth to thirtieth of the eleventh and the first of the twelfth:** Chapter 23. Rell's nine minutes in the anteroom; the form taken apart and made on the twenty-eighth; the two headings written on the board on the **twenty-ninth**; the first person to use the form on the **first of the twelfth**.
- **Second to fifteenth of the twelfth:** Chapter 24. The reading failure at the table on the **second**; two days in the workroom on the third and fourth; the four questions from the woman at the seed barns on the **fifth**; the chalk board and the levy sheet off the table leg on the **sixth**; Vare down the road on the **seventh**; the paid copying work from the seventh, the Caudle levy entered on the **ninth**; the illness on the tenth to the thirteenth; the table found running on the **fourteenth**; the Millbrook letter on the **fifteenth** and put under a stone on the dye-vat flat. The **Caudle signature is the twenty-ninth of the tenth**, in her own yard, with an assessor reading the paper out of it; the **money is collected and receipted on the ninth of the twelfth** and had been in a cloth since October. Keep the two apart.
- **Sixth to twentieth of the twelfth:** Chapter 25. The first draft of the reply burned on the sixth; the standing instructions found in the corridor on the **ninth**; Pell's question on the **twelfth**; the reply signed and the letter to Magistrate Rell posted on the **thirteenth**; the packet of forty-six leaves on the ordinary distribution on the **fourteenth**.
- **Twenty-second to twenty-eighth of the twelfth and the first to ninth of the first month:** Chapter 26. The Millbrook letter off the stone on the **fifteenth** of the twelfth and **thirteen days** in a copying alcove in Auremar working out what a set costs, with the answer and the payment on the **twenty-eighth**; the press on the twenty-sixth; the woman in the hood on the twenty-seventh; the list written on the **twenty-eighth**; the reply to Millbrook on the **twenty-eighth**; its answer on the ninth of the first month; the thirty-one confirmations written at the end of the first month.
- **Twenty-ninth of the twelfth to the thirteenth of the first month:** Chapter 27. The Nine Elms signs on the **twenty-ninth**; Crenn's sum on the **first**; the next-province assessor on the **sixth**; the sheet back from the next province in the second week.
- **Second to twenty-ninth of the first month:** Chapter 28. Orra at the window on the **second**; the enquiry bought on the **fifth** and Sarn's three answers on the **eighth**; Ilsabet Kell's offer and Orra's four lines on the **fifteenth**; the serving on the **twenty-ninth** and the rye out that night.
- **Eighth of the first month to the nineteenth of the second:** Chapter 29. Nell four days in Greyfen on the copying rounds from the **eighth** to the **twelfth**, unannounced; the table found running on the **thirteenth** with eleven names in chalk and the trestle washed, and **one name already off the board that nobody has noticed**; the Caudle kitchen on the **second** of the second month; the Sallows' gate two days later at the top of the upper lane, an hour the other way from Greyfen; the Venn term **expires on the nineteenth of the second** and Nell sits on the step on the evening of the **eighteenth**.
- **Second to twenty-second of the second month:** Chapter 30. Ostrow's refusal on the **second**; the packet boat down on the **third**; Millbrook Reach on the **eleventh**; the four questions in the Oster shop on the **twelfth**; the wheelwright's copied questions on the **thirteenth**; up the water on the **fourteenth**; the register entry read back in Auremar on the **twenty-second**.

- **A card reading, recorded and not a canon change.** The Batch 0003 card file places Chapters 29 and 30 in "the ninth and tenth weeks of the twelfth month" and "the eleventh and twelfth weeks of the twelfth month." Those are read as **weeks of the run, not of the month**, because a month has not nine weeks and because the batch's own calendar line says Chapters 26–30 fall in the twelfth month and the first month. The consequence on the page is that the Venn term expires inside the batch, on the nineteenth of the second, which the card requires and the volume outline requires.
- **Still ahead at the end of Batch 0003 — DISCHARGED IN BATCH 0004.** The fourth month is now on the page: the review was the fourth, the rate was struck whole on the sixth, the scope decision came on the ninth signed by nobody, the Greyfen assessment and the Caudle levy bit on the seventh, twenty-three houses were to be walked, and **Tavi Venn's examination is arranged for the fifth month and paid for by a foreman.** **The Venn term expired on the nineteenth of the second month with nothing decided about it, and Chapters 39–40 have paid that.**

### Batch 0004 calendar, the second week of the second month to the tenth of the fourth

Use these dates rather than re-deriving them. **One weekday is on the page and the rest follow from it: the ninth of the fourth month is a Monday**, because Chapter 38 has the sealed scope decision arriving in this building on Monday morning on that date. On that chain the sixth of the fourth was a **Friday**, the seventh a **Saturday**, the tenth a **Tuesday** and the eleventh a **Wednesday**, and Chapter 40's closing line is on that Wednesday. Chapter 33's assessor comes on a Tuesday and Chapter 35's two mentions of a Thursday are a house's own arrangements, not anchors. **A later batch must not use a weekday name at all**, because the length of the fourth, fifth, sixth and seventh months is nowhere fixed on the page and inventing one would be a canon change. The Batch 0003 calendar above is canon and runs to the twenty-second of the second month; this batch picks up there.

- **Second week of the second month to the twenty-sixth:** Chapter 31. Tamsin posts the rider to the assessor in the next province on the **sixth** at four shillings and a halfpence, and **the answer takes eighteen days** and comes on the twenty-fourth; the 'twenty-two days' in the card file is not usable and the page governs; the man in Greyfen who had moved is chased down on the fourteenth and answers on the **fourth of the third**; the assessor's answer arrives on the **twenty-fourth of the second**, five days after the Venn term expired; Marek is told about the letter on the **twenty-sixth** and not told about the drawer.
- **Third and fourth weeks of the second month to the second of the third:** Chapter 32. The scoping note comes back from the river on the **twenty-second**; Marek writes his own list of nine things he is not asking for on the **twenty-first**; the parties are certified in the anteroom on the **twenty-fourth**; the application goes up the river on the **twenty-sixth**; the court grants the sitting on the **second of the third** and sets it for the first week of the fourth.
- **Sixth and seventh of the third month:** Chapter 33. The levy figures are done at the Caudle kitchen table on the **sixth**; the table, the four names in chalk and the assessor's room are the **seventh**; the returned envelope from Emberwatch arrives at the table at about the seventh hour on the **seventh**.
- **Twenty-first of the third month to the second of the fourth:** Chapter 34. The first two attempts at the accounting are burned on the **twenty-fourth**; the accounting goes on the wall on the **twenty-sixth**; Wend is asked at the ninth hour on the **twenty-seventh**; the rule about the review is written on the **twenty-eighth**; Rell reads the wall and puts her name at the foot of the third paragraph on the **twenty-ninth**; the witness order is posted out of Sarn's own drawer and **comes back signed on the second of the fourth**.
- **Sixteenth to the twenty-eighth of the third month:** Chapter 35. Tamsin arrives on the water on the **sixteenth** with the whole of the Emberwatch material; Sabra Ollett is asked what the stamp is on the **twentieth**; the Emberwatch material is read out in the shop on the **twenty-second**; the register is read aloud with a creditor's man in the shop on the **twenty-fourth**; Tace Oster asks to be asked on the **twenty-eighth**.
- **Twenty-ninth and thirtieth of the third month:** Chapter 36. The promotion is offered and refused at the seventh hour on the **twenty-ninth**, with the intake clerk as witness; Tamsin is told at the ninth hour on the **twenty-ninth**; the counterparty notification goes to Reed Cut on the **thirtieth** and up the water on the morning boat.
- **Third of the fourth month:** Chapter 37. The limited sitting is held at the **eleventh hour**, the statement is sworn at the **twelfth**, the keeper leaves before the hour is out, and the court's one sentence is entered at the **first hour of the afternoon**.
- **Ninth to twelfth of the fourth month:** Chapter 38. Tamsin collects the scope decision and puts her own objection in her book on the **ninth**; the four lines over her own signature naming the district of Glasswater are filed and read back on the **ninth**; the list of the fourth month's dates and names comes out of the intake tray on the **twelfth**. The **ninth is a Monday** and the answer came in on Monday morning.
- **Fifteenth and sixteenth of the fourth month:** Chapter 39. The Caudle house and the paper under the cloth are the **fifteenth**; the fourth question is asked of the assessor in the guild's assessment room on the **fifteenth**; the letter from Osa Ferren is read and put on the stone on the **sixteenth**.
- **Sixth to the tenth of the fourth month:** Chapter 40. The rate is struck at the water office on the **sixth**; Sayer's crew and the two shillings and sixpence are the **sixth**; the Venn house, the letter, and the second reading of the volume are the **sixth**; he cannot look at print on the **seventh and eighth** and writes the account into his own book on the **ninth**; Rell's rooms, the past-tense refusal, the refusal of the Dunn request, the closing record and the closing scene are the **tenth**. **The chapter runs backwards against Chapters 38 and 39 by five days and says so on the page** — Rell asks him at the fourth hour of the tenth, *four days after he had sat on a step in the rain* — because the sixth is fixed by the rate strike and the tenth is fixed by Rell's own sentence that Grice's promise expired on the seventh, three days ago. Do not move the sixth and do not move the tenth.
- **A second card reading and a stale figure in the Batch 0004 prompt, recorded and not canon changes.** The Batch 0004 prompt's line 41 says *four of the seven people who did not answer*, and **seven is stale**: Chapter 26 established **ten** non-answers, of whom four are in Emberwatch, and the chapters correctly carry ten, dropping to nine on the **fourth of the third** when the man who had moved answers, with the drop explained on the page in Chapter 31. The thirty-one/two-to-thirty-two and ten-to-nine moves stand and the state files above use them. The Batch 0004 card file also lists the gate, Tavi and Dunn beats *before* the Caudle and twenty-three-houses beats, which is the likely root of Chapter 40's original date chain; the card order is not canon and the page is.
- **Still ahead at the end of Batch 0004:** the **fifth month**, in which the **second Dunn hearing is set for the twenty-first**, at the second hour, in the Mosswake Roll Court's own room, with the household to be heard and the girl to be heard; in which **Tavi Venn's canal examination** is to be given by Odile Fenn, the fee already paid; in which **Ilsabet Kell** is to stand with her own note in her own hand; in which a party to the Venn matter may apply to the **Cinder Court at Auremar** on the record of the third of the fourth; and in which **Marek Kest has said he is going to find out what he actually wants.**

### Batch 0005 calendar, the third week of the fifth month to the twenty-second of the eighth

Use these dates rather than re-deriving them. **No weekday name appears anywhere in Batch 0005, and no real month name either**; months are numbered throughout, and a later batch may not derive a date from a day name.

- **Third week of the fifth month:** Chapter 41. The household's slate rewriting is the scene; Ilsabet Kell comes up the upper lane on the **seventeenth**, three months later than she had arranged, and the confirmation goes up on the **twentieth** at the fourth hour with four lines written at its foot, which Sarn reads at the sill on the twenty-first before the second hour and enters in the day's column.
- **Twenty-first to twenty-fourth of the fifth month:** Chapter 42. The Mosswake minute of the **twenty-first** comes down the river on the **twenty-second** in six pages and five numbered findings; Marek drafts the referral on the twenty-second and twenty-third; Pell Vey argues the caption; the referral is signed and **goes down on the twenty-fourth** on the morning packet, certified at the Mosswake window at a shilling and twopence which Orra Dunn did not pay. The three Emberwatch letters are written in her own hand and posted on the **twenty-fourth**, and are not sent until the fifth of the sixth.
- **Twenty-fifth to twenty-ninth of the fifth month:** Chapter 43. The twenty-three doors are on the **twenty-sixth** and the **twenty-eighth**; the professional copyist is passed on the **twenty-ninth**; the cart to the next province goes out on the **twenty-ninth** and comes back the same night, sixty miles each way.
- **Fourth to nineteenth of the sixth month:** Chapter 44. Three index cards copied on the **fifth** at the eleventh hour; Marl Crenn's four lines come back on the **ninth**; the answer from the lane in Emberwatch comes back on the **nineteenth** on the packet.
- **Second to ninth of the sixth month:** Chapter 45. The board is read on the **second**; the guild's finding is read out at the trestle on the **ninth**; the fourth question is put in the open from the fourth hour; the Mosswake window is visited at the seventh hour; the letter from the furnace town arrives under the stone at about the eighth hour on the same day.
- **Seventeenth of the sixth month:** Chapter 46. The Cinder Court, room four, second hour, the statement produced at about the fourth hour, the registrar's note, four orders, the objection, out at about the seventh hour. **Sela Vint, presiding.** The registrar is unnamed and about fifty.
- **Twenty-ninth of the sixth month to the ninth of the seventh month:** Chapter 47. The record arrives on the **twenty-ninth** in eleven days against a six-week ordinary distribution; Sarn rules the line off the board on the **fourth of the sixth**, before anybody asked her; the man in the good coat buys two things on the day it lands; the wall's second half is written on the **second** and the **third of the seventh**; the copies go out, the man with the tray comes down the lane on the **fourth of the seventh** and the dyers' lane gives him the eleventh sheet; Sarn counts eleven shillings on the evening of the **ninth** and plans to walk up the upper lane on the **fourteenth** with a line about Rennie Sallow's own house.
- **Sixth to fifteenth of the seventh month:** Chapter 48. The counting house takes the packet on the **sixth**; Bryn Mawer writes on the **seventh**; Tamsin stands in the street on the **seventh** and again on the **fifteenth**; Sarn's four Ferren first names arrive on the **third**; the four pages of the list are handed over on the **eleventh**; the trial is set on the **fifteenth**.
- **Twenty-second of the seventh to the seventh of the eighth month:** Chapter 49. Boots off at the sixth hour on the **twenty-second**; the assessor on the weigh-house steps at the second bell on the **twenty-sixth**; nine days in Emberwatch from about the second to the seventh of the eighth; the coach back down the pass on the **seventh of the eighth**. **Sixteen days away from a desk.**
- **First to twenty-second of the eighth month:** Chapter 50. The transfer is dated the **first of the ninth**; the Registrar reopens the Kest file at the counter on the **twelfth of the eighth** and the handover list is signed the same day; the five terms are posted in two places on the **fifteenth**; the letter to Nell is written on the **sixteenth**; the thread in the Ashfall wrapper is found in the first days of the month in the Received Room; the roll-call is written at about the eleventh hour of the **twenty-second**.

## Custody And Document Chain After Batch 0001

- **Where the source leaf lives.** The Venn source leaf is in the locked cabinet of the Notaries' Table at Auremar, along the north wall of the third gallery, and the key is on a chain around the neck of whichever supervisor is on duty. A certified packet sent to a district court carries the witness copy, not the leaf. This is canon and must not be contradicted: a district court has no power to open that cabinet, and a junior clerk's written comparison request cannot produce a leaf, because a comparison cannot be had by a man who is not standing in the room when the sleeve comes out of the cabinet. **The only thing that gets the leaf to Mosswake is Ivet Sarn's own rider, sent on the evening of the tenth on the district court's custody slip, with the applicant's name at the head of it.** The leaf comes back on the eleventh and is held sealed in the Mosswake strong box from that afternoon, and is produced in open court at ten on the twelfth, out of a Table sleeve with the Table's seal on the wax, on the magistrate's own direction and not on anybody's request, and is entered in the minute book as received into the custody of this court, with the rider's receipt and the date it left the building entered beside it.
- **The provincial hold** on the Venn file is sealed and its reference carries no office name. The old fire-and-water device of the First Seat is on the seal. It is named for the first time on the page by Tamsin to Marek on the channel bank at night on the tenth, in a private conversation, and for the first time **in open court** by Magistrate Rell in Chapter 7. No character may call it a First Seat instruction before Chapter 6, and no character may name it aloud in a room before Chapter 7.
- **Where the summary and the slip are.** The Venn summary was kept out of the courier's satchel on the evening of the seventh and is in Marek's inside coat; it would go north on the next courier if a supervisor ever signed the slip. The slip is in the same coat and is **unsigned** — Pell Vey refused to countersign it on an evening docket, and he retracts that promise himself in open court on the twelfth. The comparison request is a different sheet: Marek left the original on the Table's tray on the ninth, and it is still there at the end of the batch, unsigned. Do not put any of these three on a tray and in a coat at the same time.
- **How to read an hour.** The chapters use ordinal hours ("the seventh hour", "the second hour") and the sense shifts with the scene, so a later batch must not assume a single convention. What is fixed and matters: the courier leaves Auremar at dusk on the seventh; Sarn's rider leaves Mosswake at the seventh hour of the evening of the tenth and is at the Table at the second hour of the morning of the eleventh; the witness order goes up on the board at the eighth hour of the morning of the eleventh; the leaf is produced at ten on the twelfth; the ruling is made at twenty past three on the twelfth; and in Chapter 10, all on the eighteenth, in this order: the offer to have the three documents returned out of the inventory is made and refused at the eighth hour, the ledger is produced at the ninth hour of the morning and copied until the second hour of the afternoon, the chain opens at the fourth hour of the afternoon, the arrest is at the sixth hour of the afternoon, and the request is stamped into Examinations custody and the two of them come out at the ninth hour of the evening.
- **The exhibit sleeve** holds the source leaf, the witness copy, the filed correction, the Reed Cut memorandum, Pyle's certificate, and Tamsin's comparison copy, sealed and carried to the Mosswake registry and to no other office **except by a joint order of the Mosswake Roll Court and the Chancery district of Auremar made for the purpose of copying it**, which is how the sleeve lawfully reaches the room above the chandler's shop in Chapters 9 and 10. No copy of the six documents may be made except by the court or by a person the court names in open session; the twenty-six public copies are of the court's ruling and its five terms, which are not among the six.
- **The chain** also holds two sealed pages folded in behind the memorandum: the First Seat form, and a second sealed page whose contents have not been read. A thread of old red is caught in the fold. The chain was opened **twice**: once at the Mosswake courtroom on the night of the sixteenth, to receive and enter the joint order coming down from Auremar with a constable's name at the foot of it, which is the opening Chapter 9 sends the roll-keeper to, and once in Auremar at the fourth hour of the afternoon of the eighteenth, which is the opening that produces the form. **The joint order is also what puts a constable in the broker's office at all**: the room above the chandler in Candlewright Lane is where the copies are made and the record kept, and the office itself is worked in front of the constable for the two days. The exhibit is **not** opened before the fourth hour of the afternoon of the eighteenth, so nothing out of the sealed file has been produced at any earlier hour. The second page's contents are a live open thread and must not be resolved in Batch 0002.
- **The ledger** is ninety-one leaves, copied in full in the room above the chandler's shop in Candlewright Lane, with a public inventory and named witnesses from the bench outside. Corvin Dray is in the Auremar district house on a joint order.
- **The audit request** is a hand-signed nine-paragraph document dated the eighteenth, stamped *Received*, custody *Examinations*, with a file number written in ink on Tamsin's copy by the intake clerk and initialled. It is not before the Table, not before the Inheritance Desk, and its scope is undecided. **On the nineteenth of the tenth it was narrowed by the applicant's own signature** to the sale of copied witness marks and the supply of pressed matrices within the district of Auremar, the entries at Glasswater and Emberwatch being **reserved and not abandoned**, with a clerk's time written beside the signature by Tamsin. **On the eighth of the eleventh a two-page supplementary request was filed**, naming MC/FS, the base issue, the amendment sheet, both runs, the distribution line, and the signer, and it took a second number and a second *Custody: Examinations* stamp without the applicant having to ask. Examinations has granted thirty days, wants the two provincial exhibits back, has recorded the refusal to produce the second sealed page against the applicant, and has left Bryn Mawer's supplementary line in the file at the applicant's request.
- **The two provincial exhibits.** A Glasswater river-court file (a delta landing and cut, a three-year holder who is not related to anybody in the file, a sixteen-year-old in the surety line, a filing of the tenth month itemised as entry ninety-one) and an Emberwatch copy of an endorsement on a **dyers' house** instrument, itemised, with the word *Itemised* underlined beneath the number by a clerk writing for the next reviewer. They were admitted as exhibits in the Venn matter at Mosswake on the **fourth of the eleventh** on the clerk's own authority, over an application signed by Marek Kest on the third, and the copies went out to the parties the same day. Four copies were in four buildings by the fifth. The magistrate has been informed in writing and has not ruled.
- **The Reading of the ledger after Chapter 17.** The broker's business also includes **scale matrices**: the plates, not the seals, bought by people who have already decided what impression they want. Dray's statement of the **nineteenth of the tenth** is eleven pages with no person's name in it and one description of a place: a counting house on a row by the Kiln sheds, entered in the ledger as a mark — a filled square with a bar through it and a notch cut out of the top left corner — against **thirty-eight** lines in the *carry* column with the word *paid* beside it, in **three** districts (Auremar on the great majority, Glasswater on four, Emberwatch on two, one of each of the last two being the provincial entries named in the third paragraph). No street, no name, no initial. Dray has never been up the stairs.
- **The twenty-six public copies** of the ruling are in twenty-six households, not in one place, and the arithmetic is fixed and checkable: **nine** made by Tamsin, **six** by a professional copyist in Greyfen who charged nothing, **four** by the hooded woman from the seed barn, **three** by Ivo Harker out of his mother's tin, and **four** by two of the men off the Reed Cut bank who would not give their names and who made theirs together on the back of a seed tally, arguing about the fourth term. Twenty of the twenty-six were therefore made in the lane and six came in from Greyfen.

## Named Figures Added In Batch 0001

Recorded so later batches do not invent replacements. These are supporting figures with their own wants, not clues. Marl Crenn, Rennie Sallow, and Perry Ostrow are bound in Batch 0002 and are recorded in `bible/characters.md`; they are listed there rather than duplicated here.

- **Garren Pyle:** the certifier who attended the Mosswake hearing for Corvin Dray with the pressed certificate. Withdrew the record rather than read it, would not say whose hand pressed the matrix, and was held for name and instructing office.
- **Ivo Harker:** an old lane-man in the Venn lane who keeps his mother's bundle of lane copies in a tin under his bed and copies by hand. He made three of the twenty-six public copies on leaves out of that bundle, because he has not bought paper in four years, and the red-thread page is the leaf at the bottom of the bundle that he had never once wanted anything off, so the stitch sits in the margin of a page that also carries a court term two days old. He is the keeper of an uncatalogued local copy, not an agent of anything.
- **Brammel:** a client waiting on Corvin's bench, aged about sixty, who identified the green box under the window as the working box and not the archive box. One of eleven protected witnesses.
- **The unnamed man who gave his name as a firm:** the Chapter 10 offer. About fifty, well dressed, gives a firm instead of a person, and offers to have three documents of the sealed exhibit released out of the file before it is ever opened, free and unsigned, on the understanding that the copyist's record of them is altered, and says that a fourth document can be taken from the woman holding it in a lane in Reed Cut. Declined by Marek and by Dunnet together and minuted at the eighth hour of the second day. Nobody knows who sent him, he gives no name, and the attempt to reach the woman named in the twelfth is reported to the Mosswake court and is **unanswered on the page**. He is not an agent of anything on the page and must not be written as one.
- **The unnamed hooded woman of the seed barns:** makes copies in a schoolroom hand, will not give her name, and stayed until she had heard the ruling twice. Tamsin sent her a copy because a person who will not give her name is a person who intends to be hit.
- **The customs clerk on the Auremar quay:** not named, not a plot element. He told Marek a decision had reached a person he has never met, and at the end he says he is not asking for anything. He is the shape of the slow part of the work.

## Figures Named In Batch 0002

Full entries in `bible/characters.md` for the first four; the rest are recorded here so that a later batch does not invent replacements.

- **Marl Crenn,** fifty-one, the Greyfen dyers' schedule assessor, eleven years in the room, four exemptions signed. Holds the craft exemption form and its second box; frightened of a form rather than cruel. Chapter 15. Signed the exemption for this house, this year, on the foot of the amendment, and said out loud that she was not signing because she believed it.
- **Rennie Sallow,** the Mosswake upper-district household whose seed allocation was delayed when Sarn's file was open in the fifth year. Named and paid eleven pounds in Chapter 14. His daughter **Wenna Sallow**, nineteen, is on the reading-table rota.
- **Perry Ostrow,** thirty-five, a copyist in the copying room of a Glasswater river court, who entered the endorsement into the filing book on the third of the tenth month, told nobody at home, posted the sheets to the Mosswake lane on the twenty-ninth, and came up the river herself on the first of the eleventh. Her husband is blind since the spring, when he read the file summary at the top of the delta landing. Named in Chapter 19 and on the page in Chapter 16 as the bundle-coat woman at the Mosswake stair and at Sarn's window.
- **Bryn Mawer,** seventeen, Corvin Dray's copying girl **at a stone house two streets off the Kiln road**, not among the thirteen protected people and not on the bench. Named in Chapter 17, in a supplementary request of the eighteenth of the tenth. Wants to be examined in the copying and is not recorded as frightened, at her own request. **Three places in Auremar must not be conflated, because all three are named in the last four chapters.** Her *work* is the copying room in the stone house two streets off the **Kiln road**; her *lodgings* are a rented room in **Candlewright Lane** off a woman who has since taken a man from the chandler's trade into her front; and **Dray's office**, where the ninety-one leaves were copied and the public inventory was made, is the room above the **chandler's shop in Candlewright Lane** — which is not her workplace. The **row of counting houses by the Kiln sheds**, where the plates are bought, is a fourth place again. **Corrected at the Volume 01 close:** this line used to say the row was in no chapter a building anybody in the cast works in, and Chapter 48 makes that false — Bryn Mawer has been copying in the counting house for about five weeks, and the Cinder Court's record has been sitting on its counter since the sixth of the seventh. It is a workplace from Chapter 48 onward and was not one before it.
- **Ilsabet Roon,** about fifty-five, writes wills, marriage settlements, and small sea protests for the dock labourers, and cannot work because a man in a good coat told her clients to find another writer. One of the three protected people the order cannot help. Chapter 17.
- **Brammel:** now about sixty, eleven years a client of Dray's and forty years in the province, four papers in the green box with his name and mark, inventory item nine reworded at his request, and the box returned in a leather case with a receipt. He has his papers and does not have his name out of the inventory. Chapter 17.
- **The chandler of Candlewright Lane,** unnamed, whose stair has had a bar on it and a queue that has gone. Asked only that his trade loss be written down. Chapter 17.
- **Nevin Trost,** thirty-two, an examiner of Examinations with six years in the job. Granted a thirty-day extension in writing, tried to keep Bryn Mawer's line in the file, was made to record the sealed page's refusal, and handed over an unsigned offer he is not the man to make. Chapter 20. Not a villain and probably not a friend.
- **Nery Caudle,** thirty-nine, last house on the Greyfen lane, husband Mike keeps the bench and has for nineteen years, four children, one wage. Signed the amended guild clause on the twenty-ninth of the tenth with two pounds and five a quarter of her own wage in it. Chapter 18.
- **Coll Fenner,** twenty-eight, off the Reed Cut bank, one of the two men who made four of the twenty-six copies on a seed tally while arguing about the fourth term, now on the reading-table rota. Chapter 18.
- **The child of seven,** unnamed, sent by Bettany Skail to ask that a paper be read out loud before anybody signs, and deliberately left off the record by name. Chapter 18. Bettany Skail is the Orra Dunn neighbour of Chapter 13 who brought four pounds of tilling seed she could not refuse.
- **A Glasswater miller, a three-year holder who is not related to anybody in the file, a sixteen-year-old in the surety line, and an Emberwatch clerk who underlined the word *Itemised*.** All unnamed and all load-bearing. Chapter 19.
- **The Mosswake public register of pending duties,** begun in Chapter 13, kept at the clerk's window in Sarn's hand with a date, a household, a duty, and the next review. A district practice, not a new institution, and not a Roll Assembly.
- **The reading-table rota,** begun in Chapter 18: Ivo Harker, the seed-barn copyist entered as a role and left unnamed on purpose, Coll Fenner, Wenna Sallow. A levy sheet, four nails, and a piece of chalk. No clerk, no number, no court. **Superseded in Chapter 24.** The levy sheet is off the table leg and there is a chalk board on the frame of the trestle with the four questions on it, and the list of names on it is not a rota: anybody may add their own name and nobody may take another person's off. Wenna Sallow took her own name off it in the first month and put it back.

## Figures Named In Batch 0003

Full entries in `bible/characters.md`. These are the only new named figures in the batch, and the two support figures a card is allowed are listed with them.

- **Iselle Oster,** forty-one, a cooper's widow at Millbrook Reach three hundred miles down the water, doing her own trade since the seventh year. The shop is **Oster and Son** with the *and Son* painted over and not painted out. Her son **Dain Oster**, twenty-two, has been out of the trade for a year. She wrote to a trestle table in a lane in the second month because a woman on a landing told her there was one, and she lost the master's gauge on the fourth of the twelfth. She is neither a claimant nor a plaintiff; she is a person with a missing instrument and a daughter on a surety line, and she wants the gauge back and wants to know whether the paper is a debt.
- **Tace Oster,** sixteen, Iselle's daughter, in the surety line of the instrument, signed nothing, four days out of a school term, and gave the fourth question twice with two different answers. She asked in the second week of the first month whether it was her fault and has not been asked that question since.
- **Sabra Ollett,** forty-one, clerk of the Millbrook Reach landing office, nine years in it. She stamped a creditor's filled-in model endorsement into the routine column in the tenth month in about four seconds, on a Thursday, because that is what a landing office does with what is put in front of it, and she cannot produce a number for the filing and has thought about those four seconds every day since the ninth of January. She is the **obedient clerk**: the person who proves that following a form perfectly is not the same as being safe. Not a conspirator, not a fool, and she told Tamsin Rook the truth about the stamp before she let her in.
- **Wendt Sayer,** the Reed Cut district works foreman, named in Chapter 11 and used again in Chapter 21 to price a hearing. Nine men, a boy, and a wet rope. He is the reason the cost ceiling is a real figure and he is the only person in the batch who refuses to make a bad number into a good one. He is a foreman and a witness by trade and is not a plot element.
- **The professional copyist on the Greyfen road,** unnamed and left unnamed, who made six of the twenty-six public copies for nothing in the ninth month and charged four pence a sheet in the twelfth, and said the free six were anger and that her shop has a window that says *copying* on it and she would like people to know there is a price. Eleven years in the same shop. Not a replacement for anyone.
- **The assessor from the next province,** unnamed, of a Weavers' and Small Dyemasters' Association with eleven houses, who came sixty miles in a cart with the Beckery's copy in a leather case in Chapter 27, was in the room when the fifth line on the back of the exemption form was read aloud, worked out in about eleven seconds what it did to a house with a boy on a term, and wrote the fourth question out at the top of a letter to eleven houses **without saying where it came from**. A decision in her own association's favour follows in Batch 0004 and is not granted in this batch.
- **The woman of about sixty-five at the seed barns,** unnamed, who produced the fourth question. Bettany Skail, Orra Dunn's neighbour of forty-one years' standing, is a different person and is at the top of the upper lane.


## Figures Named In Batch 0004

Full entries in `bible/characters.md`. These are the only new named figures in the batch, and the Emberwatch clerk remains unnamed by design.

- **Bettin Rahl**, the standing assessor of the **Emberwatch Dyers' Combination**, whose room is over the weigh-house office on **Sillrow** and whose hours are the second bell to the sixth. He answered one question in four lines and said plainly that the person was entitled to a plain answer, and told her the cost of the next step before she asked him twice. He is the only door a house in that trade is allowed to knock on, under a rule thirty-one years old at the second leaf of the Combination's book, and he wrote three lines across the top of the witness order that came back signed, of which the last is: *You asked what it will cost them. It will cost them one thing, which is that a person they have never met has written their name down in a book, and it cannot be taken out again, and I have told them that, and they have signed anyway, and I would like it noticed that I did not ask them to be brave.*
- **Osa Ferren**, of the fourth house past the weigh-house on Sillrow, in Emberwatch, one of the four who did not answer. She apologised for it in her first letter to the lane and wrote the first letter that trade has ever sent to a court, through its assessor, asking not what she should sign but *what a person does who is owed an answer.* She is a person, not a plot device, and she is owed a scene in a later batch.
- **The Emberwatch clerk who itemised entry ninety-one and underlined the word for the next reviewer** is a clerk of that same dyers' trade. The reason the filing is itemised is not that anybody broke the rule: **the rule was never read, because it was never issued to them.** It is still unnamed on the page and is not to be given a name in a later batch without a card.
- **The young clerk of the intake desk**, twenty-five or thereabouts, is a working figure and not a plot element. He has written the time in the margin in ink and initialled it **four** times for Marek Kest across Volume 01, three of them inside six months, has never once been thanked, was the witness to the refusal of the promotion, and wrote the fourth on the twelfth of the eighth at the counter when the Registrar reopened the Kest file. **In Volume 02's first batch he wrote two more and initialled both — one on a rider down to Mosswake at his own cost and one for a copyist — and has still never been thanked for either**, and he is one of the two persons who will examine Marek Kest in the Docket of Powers on the eleventh of the first month.


## Volume 1 Named Support Cast

- Ivet Sarn, thirty-eight, Mosswake Roll Court clerk. Cautious, not concealing; released the Venn source leaf under a recorded chain of custody and later testifies about the delay.
- Ostyn Vare, forty-five, foreman at the Greyfen dye-house where Nell works. Honest about wages, afraid of losing the craft exemption over the exit clause.
- Ruhl Dunnet, fifty-two, Auremar Chancery constable. Arrests only under a joint order and only after records are copied.
- Hallis Dren, thirty, clerk of the Reed Cut district water office. Signed the flood memorandum that the later correction was filed under; not a conspirator.
- Odile Fenn, thirty-four, Mosswake guild witness for canal work. Allies with Tavi's wish to be examined for work; wants apprentices able to leave a craft.
- Ages fixed for Volume 1: Mara Venn forty-three, Tavi Venn seventeen, Orra Dunn thirty-six, Nessa Dunn fifteen.
- Chapter placement, so no later batch invents a replacement: Sarn refused to reopen the file, recorded her refusal, and stated her four days in Chapter 3, produced the leaf in Chapter 7, and must name the household her four days cost in Chapters 11–18. Dren's flood memorandum set the Chapter 6 deadline and was produced and testified to in Chapters 7–8. Sivra Oris was at the channel in Chapter 2 and is named again in the Chapter 8 ruling; she testifies in Chapters 34–40. Vare carries the exit-clause challenge in Chapters 11–18. Dunnet made the Chapters 6–10 arrest, on the seventeenth and eighteenth. Fenn refused Tavi's examination in Chapter 9 and examines her in Chapters 41–50. Doctor Perrin Ashe of the Mosswake river landing is the man who told Marek that the reading had not let go of him, and to come to a person before a book; he may recur as an ordinary physician and owes nothing to the plot.
- **Ivet Sarn's four days are on the page and are not a later invention.** In Chapter 3, told to write down her reason, Sarn gives the great caution first: a file she reopened in the eighth month of the fifth year sat open until the fourth month of the sixth, and eleven hundred households ate four months late because the docket could not be closed and the relief rolls could not be certified. Then, separately, she adds one more thing and says it once: it took her four days to open it, she looked at it for four days looking for a way not to, and on the fourth day the relief rolls still could not go out. The four days are her own smaller sin inside the great one, and the household it cost is still unnamed on the page. Tamsin wrote *four days* on the board under the date. **What is still unspent, and what Chapters 11–18 owe the reader: which file it was, that it was an upper-district barn term file carrying the same substituted wording, and that the Rennie Sallow household's seed allocation was delayed. Sarn has a paper about that winter in a drawer in her own room and has never told them why.** Do not invent a new duration, a new year, or a new household. Use the fifth year and Rennie Sallow.
- Surname collision to hold: **Sivra Oris**, the Reed Cut water keeper and the scale counterparty to the Venn instrument, is not **Seryn Oris**, the off-page First Seat and the final antagonist. Both belong to House Oris, which is exactly why the names must never be merged on the page. Seryn is not named, present, or credited with any action anywhere in Volume 1, and the sealed First Seat instruction is her seat's paperwork rather than a personal order by her. Sivra speaks only inside her own competence, cannot answer for the First Seat, and is never made a mouth for a power she does not hold.


## Current Characters and Power

- Marek opened at Stage 0. He is still Stage 0 plus an unstable first Seam-Sight, earned on the page in Chapter 8. **He performs no magic anywhere in Chapters 11–30**, and **exactly one reading anywhere in Chapters 31–40**, taken in Chapter 40 under the undischarged limited Venn audit, which decides nothing and costs him ash, a headache, a nosebleed and two days of not being able to look at print. **He writes no valid exception, counter-seal, mixed warrant, true-name reading or Roll Assembly anywhere in Chapters 1–50**, and **the word "exception" appears nowhere in Chapters 31–40.** In Batch 0004 he refused a standing examiner of provincial filings with a room, a drawer and the standing to which forty-six districts would answer, in a room with a witness, with the terms entered and his signature under it, and he wrote the whole of the instruction that standing would have let him issue in his own book and filed it nowhere. In Batch 0003 the finding is that a *review date with no named person is not a review*, which is why the thing he accepts in place of a certified rule is a procedure with a named clerk, a witness, a cost ceiling and a fixed date — none of which is his power and none of which places any burden on his body. The cost of the one reading has been paid in full: the headache went on the twenty-sixth of the tenth month, and the temporary inability to lie about one named obligation **lapsed on the page** in Chapter 18 at the fourth hour, in a lane, to Wenna Sallow, and is described as a lapse and not a cure. It was used against him at least three times before it lapsed — the woman with the soup pot at the gate rail on the twentieth, Hallow Parrow in the corridor on the twenty-third, and Tamsin in the alcove in the eleventh month of the ninth — and the last of those was used as a substitution rather than a silence. He does not revisit Perrin Ashe, and the reason given is that there is nothing to pay a penny for. He minds the loss of the shield more than the loss of the headache, and says so. That inability is on the page three times. The first is in Chapter 9, in the Venn yard: Mara asks whether anything left in the file can come back for them, he opens his mouth to give her the sentence that would let her sleep, and it does not come, and Tamsin answers in his place and writes the unanswered question in her own book. The second is on the Auremar quay on the seventeenth, where he opens his mouth to say it to a stranger and nothing comes. The third is on the quay on the eighteenth, where there are four things he would like to say and may say only the one he is permitted to, so the limitation is a substitution rather than a silence. It is not a collapse and it is not free. No counter-seal and no Stage 2 ability appears in Volume 1.
- The first Seam-Sight is shallow. Marek can see one active chain and its immediate beneficiary. He cannot identify who first introduced it, and he has said so out loud rather than guessing. As established in Chapter 10, the red stitch and the thread in the form's fold are two facts of the same colour and are explicitly not joined in any sentence, in the record or in speech, until there is evidence.
- A reading can be accurate and still wrong: it reports the present legal effect of the record in front of the auditor, so an altered or incomplete record produces a correct answer about a false document. Volume 1 opened on exactly this gap and Chapter 8 closed it in the other direction, which is the point.
- The Chapter 8 panel reads: `Named: the present holder of the Venn field. Promised: the present holder may be joined as guarantor until full review. Carried after death: the record carries a guarantor duty to whoever occupies the field unless a new hearing ends it.` It does not name Tavi Venn, whose personal debt the same ruling voids. The reversal depends on that: the file now reaches whoever occupies the field, and the same sentence travels to the Dunn summary.
- The reading was entered in the Mosswake minute book and copied into the widow's hand by Sarn's direction, on Marek's request in open court. Tamsin holds a copy. It is a public record, not a private fact, and Marek insisted on that at the time.
- A reading reports the record, not the world. The case in Volume 1 is still carried by material comparison, witness order, ink, seal pressure, and custody.
- Court orders are not Marek's exception. Magistrate Rell and the Mosswake court issued their own interim, closed-door, and final orders in Volume 1, including the closed order preserving the Venn crop and releasing seed that she offered on the tenth and never issued, the five operative terms, and the public-necessity sentence; those place no burden on Marek's body and are never his reading. What does appear in the Roll-Answer is not a court order but the **filed record as it stands after** the court acts on it, and the public-necessity sentence is part of that record, which is exactly why the reading reports the guarantor promise the sentence supplies. That is the whole of the rule: a reading reports the operative record, and the court's own gloss is in the operative record. Marek writes no valid exception, counter-seal, or mixed warrant anywhere in Chapters 1–50. The word "exception" in Batch 0001 belongs only to the invalid draft in Chapter 5, which cannot be used: it was written by a clerk with no such power, it recites a comparison the clerk never made, and it names a party who was never heard.
- Tamsin is a roll-keeper whose ordinary copying, counting, and memory preserve evidence; she is not a magical auditor, has no power of any kind, and has independent professional judgment that has repeatedly overruled Marek's. In Batch 0004 she **stopped waiting to be the person who is asked and became the person who asks**, filing four lines over her own signature as a roll-keeper, in her own name, naming the district of Glasswater rather than the standing office, entirely unprotected and expecting nothing. She also went up the water a second time out of her own wages, not to help, and said in the abstract that a person who has been the one to ask should also be able to ask a person of power, and then declined to make the assessor of a second association answer one more question.
- Nell is a dye-house worker who wants paid craft independence and refuses to be protected by concealment. Her exit clause is amended, named, and accepted in Chapter 15, and the amendment is a numbered form that four houses on her lane have signed or will sign under fear. **In Chapter 24 she gave the reading table to the lane for good** and it runs without her, which she has decided is not a slight, and in Chapter 29 she did not write to her brother, twice, and examined the reason and did not believe her own first account of it. **In Batch 0004 she published the levy arithmetic about her own neighbours in chalk, with an apology in chalk under it, at a request from the person the number was about; she asked the fourth question of a person with power and got one clear answer and built no case out of it; she brought the Caudles' second paper out from under the cloth, which has a man's name in a box on it; and she did not go to Wenna Sallow's gate and wrote down the decision and the reason.** She has not written to Marek at all in this batch and put a letter from a furnace town on a table forty miles from him. She has been told about Ilyra and has not forgiven the ninth month and has not left.
- Pell is a mentor-shaped institutional antagonist, not the final enemy. In the yard behind the Chancery on the fourth day of the tenth month he asked Tamsin how many of thirteen protected people had the form, was refused, said he did not know the form existed and would not have acted on it, and gave her her own worry about a print run. **In Batch 0004 he offered Marek a standing examiner of provincial filings on condition that the Venn correction be treated as a routine filing defect, and he said the condition out loud in advance because, he said, saying it out loud is cheaper than the other way.** Marek refused, the refusal was entered and signed, and Pell now has his name on a reply and two recorded refusals in this matter. He warned Marek that listing the Standing Office for notice would make the file a series file in two years, and was right. He has known since the sixth of the twelfth that Tamsin holds a dated record of the yard and has let her keep it, and calls that the single worst supervisory act of his career and says he would do it again. He still has not been given the ninety words. **Tamsin told Marek the substance of the yard on the thirteenth of the twelfth, in the alcove, in one sentence, having decided that the letter going out to Magistrate Rell was what made it safe to say, and then gave him the whole of it including the line she had written under it and had not acted on for two months.** Pell then signed the reply of the thirteenth under his own hand, gave Marek the finding that the Standing Office of Provincial Continuities has no person at the top of it and the exact wording to use for it, said that he has signed district standing instructions nine times without asking who the authority was, and asked for the unfiled ninety-word corridor conversation of the ninth of the eleventh. He has not been given it. Corvin is the first concrete criminal and a valuable witness, and he remains accountable for his own crimes.
- Tamsin is the only slow-burn romantic relationship; no romance begins in Volume 1. In Batch 0002 she moved from the colleague who discloses by agreement to the colleague who decides and reports, and to a reciprocal arrangement in which Marek signs what is made in his own name and she strikes out of it what will not do. **In Batch 0003 both of them fail that arrangement once more, in mirror image** — he conceals Pell's request for nine days, she has concealed the question she wrote under the yard note for two months — and both disclose without being asked, and the difference is that she disclosed first and he did not. **In Batch 0004 he fails it a third time and softer: he decides the refusal of the promotion, gets it written and witnessed, and tells her four hours later, and she does not stop copying and asks one question.** Her live objection to leaving the First Seat quarterly return out of the reply **is no longer in his coat pocket** — she took it back on the ninth of the fourth month and put it in her own book between two pages of the Millbrook account, and recorded her reason for not moving it, which is that filing anything about a dragon seat's paperwork in the fourth month would merge the two documents in the file. **The new rule is on the page:** either of them may put a choice in writing with a date on it, and the other must answer in a room, in words, with a date on it. She has said she will check it in a year.


## Ending Lock

Seryn Oris (distinct from Sivra Oris, the Volume 1 water keeper) remains the final antagonist and survives constrained and accountable. Ilyra remains dead; her record and consequences are restored. Halden Ro's First Custodian term expires, the office remains vacant, and no temporary successor is appointed after the transition. The final image is an ordinary local hearing with two public copies of a record. No new cosmic threat, secret dragon heir, or replacement final enemy is introduced.

## Open Repair Items Found At The Volume 01 Close

Recorded, **not fixed.** A repair is its own phase and this one does not get to be one. Nothing in this list has been altered in a chapter file. Each entry names the chapter, the line, what it says, and what the canon says. `git status` at the end of the close shows no chapter modified.

- **Open repair item 1 — Chapter 47, the date span in the wall's fifth paragraph.** The chapter says of the hearing slate, *thirty-four names are on the hearing slate, of which four said the words I do not understand this and are entered as having said them, which is a complete answer and not a refusal. Twenty-two households are on the register of pending duties. And a request that is not granted is on the slate anyway, with a date on it, and that is the whole of what a slate is for.* **This is correct and no repair is needed.** It is listed only because the *chapter-summary entry* for Chapter 47 had drifted from it twice — once giving the date span as ending on the fourth of the seventh rather than the ninth, and once reporting the slate as "four have been asked and not granted." Both were errors in the state file, not in the chapter, and both have been corrected in place in `state/chapter-summaries.md`. Recorded here so that the correction is on the record and not mistaken for a change to the prose.
- **Open repair item 2 — Chapter 48, the counting house and the twenty-nine.** The chapter does **not** say that the counting house on the row by the Kiln sheds is one of the twenty-nine who said yes. It says it took the packet on the sixth of the seventh *because a counting house takes every packet*, and that the packet was still on the counter, unused, unsold and unshown, nine days later. The existing *chapter-summary entry* had compressed this into "one of the twenty-nine is a counting house," which is wrong: the counting house is a building that receives every packet and is not a holder on the list. Corrected in place in `state/chapter-summaries.md`. No repair needed in the chapter.
- **No factual error was found inside any of the fifty chapter files.** The close pass checked the numbers that carry the volume — the twenty-six and forty-one and thirty-four and twenty-nine counts, the sixty-one certificates, the ninety-eight mark instances, the five offices, the four terms and five terms of the ruling, the eleven shillings and the two shillings and sixpence, the eleven hundred pounds, the two hundred and eighty-four relief rolls, the thirty-one MC/FS sheets, the twenty-two of thirty-one with the four lines, the distances of three hundred and four hundred and thirty and four hundred and forty miles, and every date in the five calendars — against the chapters, and they agree. **The one soft figure found and deliberately not raised as a defect:** Chapter 50's roll-call has Marek say of Hallis Dren's quarterly return that he has not mentioned it to one human being *in eleven months*, where a count from the tenth month of the ninth year to the eighth month of the year after is ten months, or eleven if the month of sending is counted. This is inside a character's own hand and is not treated as an error.
- **Two stale word-count figures in `state/current.md` and `state/batch-summary.md` are superseded and have been corrected at the close.** `state/current.md` said 35,503 for Chapters 31–40, 4,657 for Chapter 40 and 166,421 across forty chapters; the reproducible count is **35,505**, **4,659** and **166,423**. The cause is a change of counting convention, not a change in the prose; the earlier figures came from a different and, as the record above shows, mis-described count, and no explanation of the difference is offered here because none has been established. The Batch 0005 figures of 35,694 and 202,117 were taken under the current command and are correct. **All five batch totals, all fifty chapter counts, the 202,117 total and the 4,042 average in the word-count record above are the reproducible ones and supersede every earlier figure in this repository.**

## Volume 02 Handover

Everything in this section is canon as established on the page in Chapters 1–50. **Nothing in it contradicts anything above it in this file;** where an earlier paragraph in this file is looser than this one, this one governs. It is written so that a Volume 02 batch does not re-derive any of it, and so that a reader can be handed this section and told the book's state accurately.

### 1. The two printed documents are still two documents
