Chapter 57 is the batch's one hearing and sits inside the about-4,200 hearing allowance; Chapters 53, 54, 58 and 60 are the aftermath band. **The Volume 01 close's working rule — 3,200–4,000 a chapter, 2,800–3,400 for aftermath, about 4,200 for a hearing — is held in six of ten chapters, and Chapters 53, 54 and 60 sit a little under the floor of the ordinary band, which is the aftermath band and not a licence; it is recorded here rather than rounded.** **The manuscript stands at 235,749 words across sixty chapters.** These figures are the **post-repair** ones: the repair pass added a closing beat to Chapter 58 and a beat to Chapter 59 and took words out of Chapters 52, 55, 56 and 57, and the pre-repair figures were 33,145 total, shortest 2,873 (Ch 58), longest 3,836 (Ch 52), Chapter 54 at 3,052.

## The Two Rules Most Likely To Be Broken Again, Restated For Volume 02

- **The two documents remain two documents.** The **MC/FS circular** is a Chancery standing office's paper; the **First Seat quarterly return** is a dragon seat's paper. No instrument anywhere joins them, one is not the child of the other, and the circular was not before the Cinder Court and is not examined there. **Only Marek Kest and Tamsin Rook know both exist.** Nothing in Chapters 51–60 puts them in one sentence, one file, one reply or one argument. **The First Seat quarterly return is still in no reply and Hallis Dren's return of the eleventh of the tenth is still dormant, still armed, and still known to nobody in the story.**
- **The four lines and the sentence keep their different ages.** The substituted *present holder* wording is in the Sallow file in the fourth month of the **fifth year**. The public-necessity sentence was **stated on the twelfth of the ninth month and printed on the twenty-second**. Chapter 58 records a **fourth appearance of the substituted wording in a district works return of the fourth month of the sixth year**, which is a year older than the Sallow file and six years and five months older than the amendment sheet — and Tamsin states on the page, twice, that these are four instances of one practice and **not four documents in a chain**, that no instrument joins any two of them, and that the finding is a column and not a seam. **No later batch may write the four appearances as a chain, in one sentence, in date order.**

## The Four Additional Locks This Batch Held

- **Marek performs no magic anywhere in Chapters 51–60.** He reads nothing. The two readings that exist are the ones taken in Chapter 8 (twelfth of the ninth month of the ninth year, at the Mosswake rehearing) and Chapter 40 (sixth of the fourth month, at about the seventh hour, alone, on a step at Reed Cut), both under the same **undischarged limited Venn audit**, and both costs are paid. The batch's entire use of them is that an office wants them in a register. **No valid exception, counter-seal, mixed warrant, true-name reading, Roll Assembly or Stage 2 ability appears.** The word **exception does not appear anywhere in Chapters 51–60** and must not be used for anything Marek does in Volume 02; when it is introduced, in Volume 03 and after, it is a legal term of art in other people's mouths.
- **The second sealed page stays shut and there is no third refusal.** Examinations holds one refusal in a form; Marek's handover list holds the second as a line in his own hand. Chapter 51 has him write a *new* note about the Ashfall bundle, which is not a refusal and is not a decision about the sealed page.
- **The red stitch in Ivo Harker's tin, the thread in the fold of the sealed page, and the working stitch in the Ashfall bundle are three different things**, and no two of them are joined in a sentence in Chapters 51–60. Chapter 51 puts the bundle **four feet from Marek's own table** because he has been transferred into the room it is in, and the note he writes that morning says the reason is not the rule of the eighteenth of the ninth month but that if it is ever opened it is opened by a person with no file, on a day with no argument in it, in a room with two other people in it.
- **The source leaf did not move.** It is in the Notaries' Table's locked cabinet in Auremar and nothing in Chapters 51–60 requires it. Sarn has no Table key. The provincial hold's reference carries no office name. **Seryn Oris is not named anywhere in Chapters 51–60** and is not credited with anything; the MC/FS circular is not hers; the Cinder Court's written statement of the seventeenth of the sixth, that it knows of no person at the head of the office that printed the sheet, is unchanged.

## Rules Established On The Page In Volume 02 Batch 0001

1. **An office can accept your case and shrink it in the same sentence, and put a name under it while it does.** The limited administrative inquiry of the ninth of the ninth month is real, dated, and signed by Nevin Trost, and it can compel nobody.
2. **A request about a series becomes a request about a district, and granting it is not a small version of the thing asked for; it is a different thing wearing the same word.** No Chancery officer exists for a series.
3. **A public register with a gap in it is a shape with a hole in it, and a person does not have to lie about a shape.** Tamsin Rook enters two unauthorised readings in the Docket's register in her own hand, with a paragraph saying she did it on her own initiative, that she has not asked the person named, and that it is not a finding that either reading was authorised.
4. **A private book of one's own, with no office on it, is outside the reach of every office in this book.** The Docket's power is over registered readings and public copies. It cannot order a person to produce what they wrote down for themselves. This is the finding of Volume 01's copy work, now load-bearing, and it is stated in a room and signed.
5. **None is not nothing. None is a person who cannot be instructed.** The second box of the custody form is headed *the office of the receiving person, if any*, and Tamsin writes **None** in it, twice, and says in the file that a person who can be instructed does not protect anybody.
6. **A substituted word does worse damage to a public duty than to a private one, because a body can be dissolved and a household cannot.** This is the volume's argument in one object and it is Lowcross.
7. **A person at a desk in a hurry used words that were already in the district's mouth.** The substituted wording in a works return of the fourth month of the sixth year is not a conspiracy and has no author anybody can name, and it is the same finding as Sabra Ollett's four seconds and as a district water office's year of unargued memoranda.
8. **A correct ruling can cost four hundred people their route to market and be entirely right, and the sentence that costs more is the one that is true.** Said out loud for the first time in a district court, in the twelfth month, by a clerk who has spent eleven months winning.
9. **A duty that nobody owes is still a duty that has to be done, and it is carried by whoever is standing nearest** — and the list of the people who did it anyway is a list of about nine names, one of whom was not permitted to and one of whom would not give a name and is not invented.
10. **A review date with no named person is not a review, and a register that is honest can look like a register that has not bothered.** Thirty-one entries in the register of pending duties, each reading *next review: none named*, written over nine days.
11. **Nobody in nine years had ever gone and looked at an outgoing rack.** The sixty households of the sixth year were never lost; they were in a press eight feet from the clerk's window, and the reason nobody went is that nobody thinks to look at a press.

## The Limited Administrative Inquiry, As Established On The Page

- **Dated the ninth of the ninth month. Signed Nevin Trost, examiner, Examinations.** The request of the **eighteenth of the ninth month** of the ninth year and the supplement of the **eighth of the eleventh month** of that year are **received and consolidated into one matter** and are accepted as a **limited administrative inquiry into the filing practice of the district of Mosswake**, to be conducted by the applicant **in his own time and at his own wage**.
- **Scope, fixed on the page:** the filing, entering and itemising of filings made under flood authority in that district **between the first month of the sixth year and the first month of this year**. The applicant may compare any filing in that district against any other filing in that district and against the district's own standing instructions, and may enter what he finds in a book of his own.
- **What it cannot do, in its own words:** it has no power to compel any document, any office, any witness or any return; it has no power to touch, and does not touch, any numbered series, any standing instruction of any district, or any return made under any instrument sealed with the fire-and-water device; **the applicant is not an officer of that office and nothing in the acceptance makes him one.**
- **Trost's four lines under his own signature:** he was told to put his name to it; he put it there because he has learned since the eighteenth of the ninth month of last year that a decision with nobody's name at the foot is worth a fifth of one with a name; **he did not want to write it**; it was put to him as a form of words and the form of words had a decision inside it.
- **Marek's answer, the same afternoon, in one paragraph:** he accepts the inquiry and asks for exactly two things — that the scope be fixed on the page and not in a fuller book, and that the name of the person conducting it be on the page as well as his. He does not ask for the series. He writes that he asked for it four times and has understood since the sixth of the sixth month of last year that asking for it is asking for a name. At the foot he answers Trost's four lines: **he has his answer to the question he put to the Registrar on the eighteenth of the ninth month of last year, which was whether that office has a person in it.**

## The Docket of Powers, And The Finding Of The Third Week Of The Eleventh Month

**A new institution, and it is the first one in the book that was not invented to serve the case.**

- **Three rooms off the corridor behind the third gallery**, the same corridor as the Forms Received rack. No sign on the door; a card in the window at the height of a man's chest, the only thing in three rooms re-papered in about thirty years. **Open at the fourth hour, closed at the sixth and at any hour the Docket is at a hearing. No fee.**
- **The register.** One page to a year, four lines to an entry — name, date, instrument, authority. About **four hundred and ten entries in thirty years**, which is **about fourteen a year**, and the smallness is the point: a scale reading needs a permission, a permission needs somebody to have asked for one, and a person only agrees to one when there is no other way of getting at the record.
- **Twelve hundred years of practice:** a reading is an act performed by a person **recorded beforehand**. The record is the permission. A reading by a person not entered at the time is an **unauthorised scale reading**. **It is not void.** Nobody is to pretend it is void.
- **Oswin Marrow**, master, about sixty, twenty-two years in it, not a lawyer and never a clerk. He has three powers and used all three on the page: he **enters the finding in a public register**, he **bars the person from any audit in any jurisdiction until examined**, and he **refers to the court sitting in room four at the Crown Kiln** the single question whether a reading so performed was of any legal effect whatever. He cannot punish, fine, suspend, or refer a person. He has never once had to enforce anything, and he says so.
- **The finding, in his own words and correct in every particular:** Marek Kest performed a reading of a scale instrument on two occasions — **the twelfth of the ninth month of last year** at a public rehearing in the district of Mosswake, and **the sixth of the fourth month of this year** at about the seventh hour, alone, at Reed Cut — in each case on a limited audit issued in open court by that court, in each case on the same audit that **has never been discharged**; **neither reading is entered in the register**; a reading by a person not entered at the time of the reading is an unauthorised scale reading.
- **Marek contests the referral and not the entry**, in one page, and asks the question be reframed: **whether a record in which such a reading was entered, being a record of a court and not of the Docket, is altered in its effect by the finding that the reading was unauthorised** — on the stated ground that a finding of no effect would not touch him and would touch the only record in the empire in which a girl of fifteen is entered as heard and a household is entered as asked, and that **a person erased out of a record is a person nobody can call**, and that a person cannot be held to a record that turns out to have been made by a man with no right to make it. He says he does not know the answer.
- **Marrow refuses the reframing, countersigns a condition onto his own finding, and gives the date anyway.** The condition is that the referred question is to say what it is a finding about. The examination is **the eleventh of the first month, at the fourth hour, in the Docket's own room**, and **Marrow names the two persons and not Marek**: **the intake clerk of the Chancery and a copyist of the third gallery**, on the stated ground that a man who is being examined should not be examined in somebody else's room. Marek pays the fee of the room and asks for that on the page.
- **Marrow's private admission, in a room, to a man who is not the person it concerns because he is the person it is nearest to:** he entered an act of his own into that register in the nineteenth year of his mastership, has never told anybody, and says it was not a woman and it was not a copyist.
- **Marek is barred from any audit in any jurisdiction in any office until examined.** He does not ask for the bar to be lifted and does not ask to be examined quickly.

## The Sixty Households Of The Sixth Year, Part Paid

**Volume 01's largest unpaid debt. It is now a number and a board, and it is not finished.**

- The Mosswake Roll Court applied the public-necessity sentence **in letters and without a hearing** to **about sixty households** in the sixth year. It does not know their names, cannot get them in time, they are not before it, and it has not asked them.
- **They are not in a register, and the reason is the answer.** A register of them would have been a record of the court doing it, and there is no column in that building for a duty entered with nobody heard.
- **The mechanism, in full.** The sentence was applied in letters; letters go out on a **quarterly cycle**; a cycle files its copies, presses its returns, ties them in string and writes the bundle on an oak shelf in a **press-presser's press in the old records room at the end of the embankment corridor**, which has a cold floor and a window that does not open.
- **The arithmetic, which is the finding and is worse than a lie.** **Fifty-four filed copies** for three cycles: **thirty-one** carry a return mark with a name and a door, of which **twenty-two are marked *seen* and nine are marked *told***, all in one returning officer's upright hand; **eleven** carry a return mark with a word and no name — ***left* four times, *gone* three times, *no such* twice, and twice two letters and a blot**; **twelve** are copies with no return at all, returned by the post and stamped undeliverable, which says nothing about where. **The fourth cycle of the sixth year was never copied.** The retired third clerk of that year, sixty-one, with a child and eleven weeks of docket left and no replacement until the docket closed in the following spring, says in her kitchen that **nobody told her not to make it** and that she has gone over those eleven years four hundred times and found no moment in which anybody did anything to her. **Thirty-one plus eleven plus twelve is fifty-four, and fifty-four plus six uncopied is about sixty, and the six are a clerk who did not have time.**
- **The board at the Mosswake window**, in chalk, at the head of the block, in letters about four inches high: **THIRTY-ONE OF ABOUT SIXTY. Eighteen returns did not come back. Six copies were never made, by a clerk who was not asked to and did not, and I have her kitchen and the date and her word for it, and she is sixty-one and has a child and is alive. Nobody has been asked to do anything about this and nobody is going to be until a person with a bar comes to this window and asks.** Underneath, small, where a person has to come close: the ninth paragraph of the wall, in the first person, with the day on it, being that nine years and she has never once gone and looked at an outgoing rack, that the sixty were in it eight feet from where she works, and that that is not the district's fault and is hers.
- **The register of pending duties has thirty-one new entries** in the standard five-column form, entered over nine days from the fourth of the first month, each with a date, a household, a door, the word *seen* or *told*, the date of the officer's walk, the duty in the magistrate's own words, and a column for the copy. **The five headings, in this order and as a lock, are *Date. Household. Duty. Next review. Copy sold.*** The third of the five is the one she has filled about nine hundred times and the fourth is the one she could not fill at all; **the fifth records that a copy was sold, at a shilling, and it is the reason everything on that board costs a shilling a copy — it is not the price itself, and no later chapter may write the fifth heading as *A shilling a copy* or treat a price as a column.** **The fourth column reads *next review: none named*, thirty-one times**, because there is no review: the district has not decided whether to run the process, the Cinder Court's full sitting has no date, restitution is not a word this court can use, the magistrate will not be asked, and the flue is still booked for the fourteenth of the second month.
- **Iolanthe Rees**, sixty, of the two strips above the upper lane, ground rent a hundred and forty pounds in twenty years, came to the window at the third hour of the sixth of the first month so as not to be seen in a queue and asked one question, which was whether she was going to be called into a room. **She is not.** Nobody in that office has asked that she be called, no slate carries her name, and the court has ordered nothing. Sarn says in the entry that she cannot promise what any other office or any other court will do, **that she has been wrong four times in twelve months and says so when asked**, and takes a shilling for the copy.
- **Restitution is not begun. Nobody has been asked to do anything.** The flue in the old records room is still booked for the fourteenth of the second month and the fourth cycle's copies are still not made.

## Lowcross, As Established On The Page

- **The crossing.** A public timber cart bridge over the **Weir Cut** in **Auremar's lower district**, built in the second year of the century's second decade under an instrument **sealed with the old fire-and-water device** and countersigned by the water holder of that cut. The water holder's gate, sluice and cut duties survive. **The water holder is not named on the page and is not a character in this book, and is not made a mouth for a power it does not hold.** The instrument says the bridge-side duty of upkeep shall be held against **the present holder**, and the instrument has not been produced in this batch and is not asked for.
- **The parish of Lowcross was dissolved in the sixth year** in a district reorganisation, of which nobody in Lowcross knew, because the notice went to the parish and after the notice there was no parish to receive anything. There is still a board on the church wall with a name on it and nobody has taken it down.
- **Hester Crane**, sixty-one, bridgewarden nineteen years, widowed, a son of thirty-four in the district works, and a granddaughter of eleven, **Rosamund Crane**, who can read and whose grandmother cannot read a paragraph and has never said so in that family. Hester has signed **one instrument in sixty-one years**.
- **The signed form.** A maintenance request of the eighth of the eighth month of this year, in her own hand, on a works form, put in a box on the counter. On the ninth of the ninth month **Jonas Cray**, thirty, a works clerk of about four years, came out into the yard and could not enter her because **the column wants a body and the parish was dissolved**, and put his finger on a box headed **the present holder**, and she read the three words at arm's length and said *that is me, then*, and signed. He wrote her name in the box in a good hand and spelled it right and did not ask her to initial it, because the form does not ask.
- **The returned form** came back nine weeks later on a post with a note on the reverse: the works cannot commit money beyond the quarter in which it is spent; the crossing is not identified in the works' book because the parish box was dissolved in the sixth year and no body has been entered in it since; the form is returned unprocessed; the sender may apply at the second hour on any working morning of the next quarter and the works will then decide whether the crossing is a work of the district or of a holder; **that decision has not been made; the form is returned because the form cannot be entered and not because the work is refused.**
- **The fourth line of that note is the one that matters.** *Whether the crossing is a work of the district or of a holder.* Rosamund Crane stopped on it, unasked, at the table, and asked whose name it is, and worked out in about four seconds that the line names two and one of them is not a person, and that it says *or* and does not say who decides. That is **the second of the four questions asked by a person of eleven, of a line of a works note**, and it is the only document in the matter that was true and that nobody had asked for.
- **The substituted wording in the fourth month of the sixth year.** A quarterly return of the district works, in a form that is not in any series, in an office that is not on the forty-six addresses and is not in any district holding a flood docket, filled in at a desk in a room over a cobbled yard by a clerk with **a hundred and forty returns to do that month** and about nine minutes, in the box headed *holder of the crossing*, where the law said *the parish of Lowcross*. It says **the present holder.** That is a year older than the Sallow file and **six years and five months older than the amendment sheet of the twenty-second of the ninth month.** The totals on the return were right and the word in a box is not a total and nobody looked at the word in a box for six years.
- **The money, unchanged.** The bank, abutment and eleven feet of new kerb come to **nineteen pounds three and fourpence**. The district works fund for the quarter holds **six pounds and eightpence** and the works cannot commit money beyond the quarter in which it is spent. **Four hundred and eleven people** are on the far side of that water from the market, the wharf, the mill and the chapel, and the district's own return for the last four weeks says **three hundred and eighty-eight crossings**, and that number is on a card in a room over a cobbled yard and is not wrong. **These two figures are different figures and may never be swapped: four hundred and eleven is a headcount of people and three hundred and eighty-eight is a count of crossings, and it is the crossings figure that Marek Kest puts in his page to the Docket of Powers.**
- **The gap, which nobody has ever entered in a column.** The crossing costs **nineteen pounds three and fourpence every nine years**, which is about **two pounds two and a half a year, about a penny a day** — too small to be a line in a fund and too large to be a household's money — and it has lived in that gap for nineteen years out of a box in a woman's kitchen. **Nineteen years contains two of those repairs and not four, and the crossing has taken about nineteen pounds out of the fund twice and has lost about a pound a year in the years in between, and the fund holds about six pounds on the average of its four quarters against the six pounds and eightpence committed for any one of them.**
- **The hearing of the second of the twelfth month** was a **continuance application**, not an application for money. The works is not a party that can be compelled and sent **Gideon Harrow**, about fifty, district engineer, with a schedule in a leather case, and Jonas Cray. The household sent **Silas Sowle**, a solicitor of about fifty-five who has done this district's poor business for nineteen years and says so unasked. The magistrate was **Joane Tranter**, district court of the lower district. **The continuance was refused with reasons and with a date**, the household may apply again in the first week of the fourth month of next year, and the crossing is out of use on the **fourteenth of the twelfth month** unless the works certifies it before that day. **The refusal is correct and the chapter says so.**
- **The district's own filed answer** is the most honest paper any office in that district has produced in two years: it has not withdrawn its own practice and cannot, it is aware of a high court's order and is not bound by it, it will apply the law as it stands, and **it will therefore do nothing in this matter because there is nothing in it that the district may do.** The magistrate says out loud that this is the first of nine files in nine years where an office has told her in writing that it is powerless, and that the district gained nothing by it.
- **The line in the minute, entered by the magistrate on her own motion and asked by nobody:** that **no officer of the district works has at any time asked this district to stand the cost of any crossing**, and that she believes it because she has been in the district nine years and has never had such a paper and would have kept it. Harrow's answer, in the room, is that a works that writes down that it cannot do a thing has written down that a thing is not being done, and then a district has to answer it, and a district will find a reason to do nothing about it, and everybody will be satisfied — **and that is precisely why nobody has ever done it.**
- **The bar goes up on the twelfth of the twelfth month**, two days early, by two men with a ladder and a chain and an ordinary day's order from a foreman, because there was going to be ice in the next three days. The order says the fourteenth. The magistrate enters the two days afterwards without being asked: *the court notes that the works have anticipated the date by two days, that the court has no power over the works in that, and that the court would not have wished otherwise.*
- **What closing it cost, in a return nobody asked for and a woman paid for:** eleven households on the far side had been carrying their own four-day flour over the bridge and had sills, and the crossing took the sills out, so on the sixteenth they bought at the mill price and carried it on their backs, a difference of about eleven pounds a month across eleven households; the mill's four carts a day went from nine minutes to ninety and it sent two carts down the river and paid a road toll of ninepence a cart; about fifty went to chapel and eighteen came and the vicar wrote a line about the fourteen who did not and did not say why; and **Berta Coll, eighty-one, did not come on the second day** and a girl of nineteen from the mill, **Wyn Aldis**, went over on the plank with a sack on her shoulder three times and says she was not brave, she was nineteen and the water was low.
- **The nine names**, on the back of a form, read out and written down, and nobody asked for them: Jonas Cray, whose day's order she will not count twice; **Gideon Harrow**, the fourth, ninth and sixteenth, alone, with a spade, and who may read the list — **and he does, two days before the bridge is carried back, because she wrote on the list that he might, and says that in nineteen years of that works nobody at that gate has ever given him a piece of paper with his own work on it, that he does not know what a man does with that, that he will not stand in a yard and be thanked for it because he would not know where to put it, and asks her to go and find somebody to tell him what to do with it;** Aloys Fenn of the chapel, six days; Wyn Aldis, three times; Rennick Quill and his wife Maud with a cart and a spade and a bad back; **the two men off the Reed Cut bank, who would not give a name, the fifth time somebody has refused her a name and the fifth time it has been right**; a woman with no name at all who came from the far side and would not say anything about it; and **her son, who is in the works and is not on the list because he was not permitted, and who went four times, and who has been spoken to by a foreman, and who is not sorry.** The bridge is carried back open on the **twenty-fourth of the twelfth month**, eleven days early against the fourteen in the order, because nine people carried it.
- **Jonas Cray's sentence, written on the nineteenth of the twelfth month at her table in a young clerk's hand with a foreman's name at the foot of it:** *The district works of the lower district states, on the nineteenth of the twelfth month, that the fund of this district works for the quarter of the first month of next year cannot stand the cost of the bank, abutment and kerb works at the crossing at Lowcross, estimated at nineteen pounds three and fourpence, and that the crossing is a work of the public of this district and is not the work of a holder, and that this office asks the district to stand the cost and does not ask it to be paid by any person.* He read it to Rosamund Crane, had her read it after him, wrote down that she had, and then read it out to Hester Crane at arm's length twice. **He has undertaken to come up that yard once a month, at the seventh hour, and not in a good coat, and to say in the yard, twice, whatever it is that is bad to say.**
- **Hester Crane refuses to sign a second instrument** and gives the reason twice, the second time shorter, in a copyist's hand and not her own: she is the second woman in four hundred miles a word that walks about has got hold of, the first was fifteen and was in a court with somebody in the room, and she has a granddaughter who can read, *and I am not going to hand her the next one.* She will keep the wardenship and will not sign for the bridge.
- **The sentence she gives and then refuses to have written in a document:** *You have made it true and I am the one it is true about.* Her reason for refusing the document is that she cannot read a paragraph, that a document in a file in nine years would have her words in it, and that she would not be able to read it.

## The File, And The Wrong Column

- **A schedule, one page, headed *persons who may be answerable in the matter of the crossing at Lowcross*, compiled from the parish list of the second year and from returns received, revised in the third cycle of the sixth year.** It has **eleven lines** and every one has a name in the first column: the parish (body, dissolved in the sixth year, no successor entered); the district works (office); the district court (office); the trustees of the low crossing, appointed in the second year (office, no surviving appointment); the wharf company of the Weir Cut (office); the river navigation body of the lower cut (office); and **four returns reading *the present holder* with nothing after them**, in the correct column, since the fourth month of the sixth year.
- **The eleventh line is the only living person in the file, and she is in the wrong column:** *a maintenance form of the eighth month, this year — H. Crane, of the yard, holder in fact*, entered under the heading **persons from whom a return has been requested**, because she answered a form, and a person who has been asked a question is filed under *asked* and not under *liable*.
- **This is a finding and it is about a file and not about a clause:** the file names everybody the building could imagine being liable, and the only living person in it is the one household that can prove the clause was copied onto it. **The finding that is worth having in a court is not a seam. It is a column.** **This is delivered in Chapters 57–60 and is not the volume's midpoint reversal. `outline/volume-02.md` has been corrected: the midpoint reversal at Chapters 68–73 is the altered witness order and the fact that a supervisor's name is on the arranging of it, and that the instrument this book has relied on — *a person affected may be heard about it in a room* — does not fit that evidence. No later batch may re-run the Lowcross file as a discovery.**
- **The three copies and who holds them, and it is a lock.** She copied the finding out three times in plain words in a copyist's hand and **decided the order before she acted**: the **first to Hester Crane at the gate**, on the evening of the second of the twelfth, by a cart, four pence of carriage in a slot, addressed to a gate and not to a court, a works or an office, with a separate sheet that says *for the gate, and do not read it to her unless she asks, and she will ask, and when she asks, read all of it*; the **second to Silas Sowle**, the household's solicitor and nobody else's, at his address in the lower district, with five words on the front, *for the application in the fourth month. — T. Rook, no office*; the **third down the river to Ivet Sarn** at the Mosswake Roll Court by the morning packet, behind the other two. **Marek Kest was given no copy and received it at the table at about the eighth hour of that night, and that is the second breach of the first half of their rule in three months.**
- **Marek Kest's answer to the second half of that rule, and it is new and it is live.** He has undertaken, and put it in his own book with the day on it, that **on the fourth of the first month, at the fourth hour, in the Received Room, she will bring him every sheet that goes out of this matter to a solicitor, a household, or an office outside the Roll Hall, and he will read it in front of her before it goes, and if he has a reason against one of them he will give it to her in a room and out loud and not on a page; and that if he ever decides she is wrong again he will ask her for it before he acts, knowing she will say no, and will go and do it anyway.** He told her in terms that **this is worse than the rule they made, because it is a man who has been caught twice inventing a third one**, and asked her to sign the first line of the custody form so that he is in the file while there is still nothing in it against him. **No later batch may retire this undertaking, and the fourth of the first month is a fixed date.**

## The Custody Of The Public Copies

- **A four-line form with two boxes, required of the person holding the public copies in a matter under an inquiry, by the Docket of Powers.** Line one: the documents transferred, listed. **Box two: the office of the receiving person, if any.** Line three: the receiving person signs here. Line four: the date.
- **Tamsin Rook signed it on the eleventh of the ninth month and wrote `None` in the second box**, in her own hand, and read it back twice. The documents transferred are **the forty-one pages of the Cinder Court's record**, which are in the copying alcove and not in the Received Room, and whose original is in a minute book in room four of the Cinder Court and is not in this building.
- **The reason for the rule** is on the page in her own words: an office holding the public copies in a matter is an office that can be asked what it did with them, and a person who holds them and can be asked about them is a person who can be managed. The Docket is not being unkind; somebody at some point was burned by a copy that went missing into a building with a name over the door.
- **The Docket asked where the independent copy of the evidence is and was told there is one, in a book of her own with no office on it, in a chest she is not going to describe.** It entered *independent copy exists, not produced, no power* on a card. **A private book is outside the reach of every office in this book** and this is now the load-bearing form of Volume 01's copy work.

## The Two Register Entries, In Tamsin Rook's Hand

- On the **fourteenth of the ninth month**, on her own initiative and not on the request of the person named, she entered **both readings of the twelfth of the ninth month of last year and the sixth of the fourth month of this year** into the Docket's register, in the Docket's own four-line form, with the dates, the places, the permissions and the disposition, and initialled the entry, and wrote under it that **an entry made by one person about another person's act is a thing the second person must be able to see and dispute, and there is no other way of it**; that a register with a gap in it is worse than one with an entry in it; that a gap is a thing somebody can find in nine years and use; and that **this is not a finding that either reading was authorised and she has no power to make one — it is a finding that they happened.**
- **Countersigned by Oswin Marrow about forty minutes later**, at the eleventh hour. **Nobody had ever entered anything in that book on their own initiative before.**
- **She told Marek at the twelfth hour on the fourteenth of the ninth month, late on purpose**, per the rule they made with each other in the twelfth month of last year, and said she had broken the first half of it, and that he would want to argue with one of the two halves of what she was going to say and that she would not help him pick.
- **The cost is named on the page:** her name is in a public register as the person who recorded another person's unauthorised act, and in about four years that will be used against her, and it is the correct thing to have done and she did it in about nine minutes.

## The Glasswater Answer

- **Tamsin Rook's four lines, filed over her own signature on the ninth of the fourth month naming the district of Glasswater, are answered, and the answer came into the third gallery on the eleventh of the ninth month** on the same packet as the custody form, in two thirds of a page, signed **A. Kesk, clerk** — *and I have written Keske because two people in the last year have written it Kesk and I would like it right on the paper at least.* **The eleventh of the ninth month is the fixed date; the answer is not of the third week of the ninth and no later batch may date it so.**
- **The answer is complete and correct and is about a document and not about a practice:** the district's standing instructions say nothing about itemising a filing made under flood authority, and have been amended four times without mentioning it; **this court has never had a flood-season continuity filing entered in it, not one, in six years, in any quarter of any year**; and the itemised filing of the tenth month that is in a provincial record was entered in a **river court of the same district, which is a different office from this one**, and this court has never seen it and will not guess about it.
- **Tamsin's finding, in eight lines, which she sent to nobody:** *the third province is a district that has never had one, and the finding I have wanted for four months is that the empire is not full of this. The empire is mostly full of districts where the form never came, and the harm is where it did.* **The thread is answered. It is not converted into a province and it is not withdrawn. Tamsin's four lines are now answered and unanswered at the same time, and the answer is on the same page of her book as the question, with the date.**
- **She did not send it to Marek, on purpose, and wrote down that she did not send it on purpose**, and the reason she wrote it down was not the reason she did it.

## Greyfen, The Levy, And The Fifth Question

- **The guild's finding came back on the fourth of the eleventh month**, three and a quarter pages, against the four houses that entered a second instrument, and the reason it is against them is that they signed. The Caudles are first, because they signed first, at **two pounds five and a farthing a quarter**, and the farthing was Nery Caudle's own money and went in the same coin out of a cloth. **Four houses at that figure is eight pounds and a penny a quarter, and four quarters of that is thirty-two pounds and fourpence out of the lane — the whole of the arithmetic, and no chapter may print nine pounds a quarter or thirty-six pounds against it.**
- **Nell Kest read all of it out at the trestle on the eleventh of the eleventh and then read Roke's other half, which is the arithmetic above: eight pounds and a penny a quarter on four houses, four times a year, for a year, is thirty-two pounds and fourpence out of a lane**, having promised in the sixth month that she would stand there: it is the fifth of the eleventh, Roke said eleven months, and the next four houses are the ones who will be out, and the Nine Elms has a boy on a term. **She put the four houses' children in the room**, nine of them between nine and nineteen, because the number is a wage and a number nobody has heard of is a number that gets made true by whoever is standing in the room when it is read out.
- **The fifth question has an answer in Greyfen and it is not a door.** What a house does when the assessor says no is that it **moves**: four pounds ten each way, or eleven if you go on your own, two days lost at four shillings a day, a bench bond of eleven pounds that a house finds or gets a man to sign, and **four years before a return without a second bond**. One house went to Thornfen about eleven months ago. Eleven did not stay, not because it was asked, and were not kept in a line, because the rule is thirty-one years old and the *person* is the door.
- **Ottoline Bray**, fifty-two, at the eleventh door, has a mill bench paper about her son of seventeen **with his own name on it, spelled right, in his own hand at the top where a name goes**, which nobody read to her. The first question is yes and there is work. The second is his own name. The third, asked by him in the kitchen in the eighth month with the cloth on the table, came back *if I stop, nobody answers for it, it just stops* and then *then it is mine while I am on it, and it is nobody's after, and that is the whole of what a mill is.* The fourth — **he read it to her himself**, and said it meant a week's notice and that his week's notice is four days, and that there is nothing in this about after. **It is the only paper in five months the four questions have made better rather than frightened, and it is entered as such. Five months, not eleven: the four questions went onto a stone in a lane in the sixth month and the levy was read on the eleventh of the eleventh.**
- **The professional copyist on the Greyfen road has nine names on a waiting list inside her window, four of them trades**, and one is a woman from a third district who came sixty miles to Auremar in the fourth week of the ninth month to ask the four questions at a counter, was not in, waited two days, and left the four questions on the step in her own hand with her own name and her trade's name and the day on it. The sheet was found on the fourth day, was not thrown away, and is in a drawer. **She is not named and the trade is not named and neither is to be without a card.**
- **Nell Kest's fifth question of her own**, written in her own book and posted on the twenty-sixth of the eleventh to Emberwatch and three other trades, one of which she has never seen: *and what does it cost, in pounds, to leave, and who has paid it, and what did you have on the day you went that you did not have on the day before.* It carries **the price in the body of the letter** and the name of nobody.
- **An answer from a fourth trade arrived before the question**, two lines and a trade's name at the top, from **B. Breg, for the combination**: the answer to *what does your assessor say when you ask* is that it is not a door and not a woman, it is a bench, and if the holder stops the bench is the holder of the paper, and eleven of them have asked and eleven of them have got that, and they have written it down at the second leaf of their own book under a rule thirty-one years old because there is nowhere else to put it.
- **Nell Kest wrote to her brother on the twenty-eighth of the eleventh month**, four lines, off the vats, with the lamp off, and did not put a line of it into her book first. It is the first letter she has written to him since the twenty-ninth of the first month of last year. It is about the levy, the fifth question, and the board at four hundred and thirty miles that says *thirty-one of about sixty* and that somebody in a furnace town has read out to a room, **and it says she has not forgiven the ninth month and is not going to, and will say it to his face in about four hours.** It mentions the ninth month only to say that it is not mentioned, which is the same thing and worse.

## Figures Named In Volume 02 Batch 0001

Recorded so that a later batch does not invent replacements. These are working people with independent wants, not clues with motives. **The Emberwatch clerk who itemised entry ninety-one and underlined the word remains unnamed and is not to be given a name without a card.**

- **Oswin Marrow:** master of the **Docket of Powers**, about sixty, twenty-two years in it, not a lawyer and never a clerk. He keeps a sixty-year-old register, has three powers and used all three, and said all three out loud in advance so that Marek Kest would notice it. He refused the reframing, countersigned a condition onto his own finding, gave the examination date, named two of the three people in the room himself, and said he would not be on the side of a man who is being examined. He entered an act of his own into that register in the nineteenth year of his mastership and has never told anybody, and told a man who is not the person it concerns because he is the person it is nearest to. He wants a register that is true and says that a Docket of Powers which could say what a reading did to a file would be the second most dangerous room in the Roll Hall.
- **Joane Tranter:** magistrate, **district court of Auremar's lower district**, nine years in the district. She entered an office's written admission that it is powerless as the first such paper she has had in nine years, asked a district engineer a question nobody had asked, entered the two days by which the works anticipated her own date without being asked, and refused the continuance correctly with reasons and a date. **She is not an ally and she is not an opponent; she is a court, and she says the sentence that costs more is the one that is true.**
- **Gideon Harrow:** district engineer, **district works of Auremar's lower district**, about fifty, forty-one returns a quarter, nineteen years, a schedule in a leather case and a warding tick at the end of every sentence he says out loud. He has never written to a district to say that the works cannot do a thing, and says why: an office that reports on itself stops being an office and becomes a district's failure, and a district will find a reason to do nothing about it. **He is not a villain and has committed no wrong and is owed a scene.** He put his own name in Hester Crane's list of nine people who carried the bridge, and said in the room that somebody will have to answer a piece of paper about it one day.
- **Jonas Cray:** works clerk, **district works of Auremar's lower district**, about thirty, four years in it, a good coat and no particular wish to be unkind. He could not enter Hester Crane in a column that wanted a dissolved parish, put his finger on a box headed *the present holder*, and spelled her name right. He put a bar up two days early on an ordinary day's order. **On the nineteenth of the twelfth month he wrote, for the first time in the history of that office, that the works cannot stand the cost, that the crossing is a work of the public, and that the office asks the district to stand it** — at a woman's table, with a foreman's name at the foot, and read it out to a girl of eleven and to a woman who cannot read it. He has undertaken to come once a month and not in a good coat. He is the fourth person in this book to do what nobody did.
- **Hester Crane:** sixty-one, **bridgewarden of Lowcross nineteen years**, widowed, a son of thirty-four in the district works, and a granddaughter of eleven, **Rosamund Crane**, who can read. She has signed one instrument in sixty-one years and has refused a second. She has done a public job for nineteen years out of a box in her kitchen because the amount is too small to be a line in a fund. She cannot read a paragraph and has never said so in that family. **She is the person who says the sentence the volume is standing on and then refuses to have it written in a document**, and her reason is on the page.
- **Rosamund Crane:** eleven, **the granddaughter**, reads, and asked the second of the four questions of a line of a works note at her grandmother's table and worked out in about four seconds that the line names two and one of them is not a person. **She is not a plot device and she is not a child put in a box and she is not a girl of fifteen again.** She is on a bench list for the far-side mill and there is no school on a market day.
- **Silas Sowle:** solicitor, about fifty-five, nineteen years of this district's poor business, in the lower district. He says on the record that he is not good at it. He is the man who told Hester Crane that a person who is not in the room is a person who has not been heard, who told Marek Kest in four words that he was arguing the wrong thing, and who lost. He received a copy of Tamsin Rook's finding with five words on the front: *for the application in the fourth month. — T. Rook, no office.*
- **Iolanthe Rees:** sixty, of the two strips above the upper lane in Mosswake, **one of the thirty-one households of the sixth year**, ground rent a hundred and forty pounds in twenty years, who came to a clerk's window at the third hour so as not to be seen in a queue and asked whether she was going to be called into a room. She is not. She is not a claimant and does not want to complain. She is the reason the shilling stays.
- **Ottoline Bray:** fifty-two, **the eleventh door in the Greyfen dyers' lane**, a woman people tell things to who did not know it, who has been carrying a mill bench paper about her son of seventeen since the second month and who has her own name given to Nell Kest in June on the understanding that she would be told to ask her own boy first. **The paper has the boy's own name on it and was never read to her, and the boy read it to her.**
- **A. Kesk (or Keske), clerk of the district court of Glasswater:** signed the answer that a district with a flood docket since the second year has never had a flood-season continuity filing in it, and who said that the itemised filing in a provincial record came out of a river court of the same district and is a different office from this one. Named, and is a person, and is owed nothing further.
- **S. Roke:** dyers' lane, Greyfen. Said the other half of the levy finding out loud in a lane in front of ninety people on the ninth of the sixth month, and has since been asked twice whether she wants a copy sent to her house and has said no twice, and wants it written on the page that she was asked twice. **A first initial only has been given to a figure Volume 01 left without one. She is not the man of sixty-one, who is unnamed by his own choice.**
- **B. Breg,** of a fourth trade, wrote a trade's name at the top of two lines and said their assessor is a bench and that if the holder stops the bench is the holder of the paper, and that eleven of them have asked and eleven have got that, and they have written it at the second leaf of their own book. A trade's name is not a person's name and is not to be turned into one without a card.
- **The copyist from the shop with a price painted in the window:** about forty, a hand that writes small and fast, eleven years in a one-window shop, who came out to Auremar's lower district on a cart, paid four pence of the carriage herself and would have paid eleven if it had been eleven, and wrote down Hester Crane's sentences and the four words of her refusal and the fourth line of a works note read twice at arm's length. **She is the professional copyist on the Greyfen road and is unnamed and stays unnamed, she is about forty and no other age is on the page, and she is not a holder of the copy list and does not hold a page of it.**

## Volume 02 Batch 0001 Calendar, The First Of The Ninth Month To The Ninth Of The First Month

**Use these dates rather than re-deriving them. No weekday name appears anywhere in this batch and none may be used to anchor a chain; the only weekday on the page in the whole book is the ninth of the fourth month of the ninth year being a Monday, and nothing may be derived from it.** Months are numbered throughout, as they are on the page.

- **First week of the ninth month:** Chapter 0051. The transfer has taken effect on the first; the Ashfall note is written on the first; **the limited administrative inquiry is dated the ninth**, and Marek's answer is the same day.
- **Second and third weeks of the ninth month:** Chapter 0052. The custody form is signed on the **eleventh**; the four pages come back on the morning of the **eleventh**; the Glasswater answer is on the same packet; **the Docket of Powers' register is written in on the fourteenth at about the ninth hour and countersigned at about the eleventh hour**, and Marek is told at the twelfth hour.
- **Fourth week of the ninth to the first week of the tenth month:** Chapter 0053. The work's man comes on the **second of the tenth** about the flue; the returns are read over about four hours; the retired third clerk of the sixth year is walked to in the evening of about the ninth or tenth; **the thirty-one are named on the board in the fourth week of the ninth month** and the paragraph about the outgoing rack is dated the **twenty-eighth of the ninth month**, which is before the man from the works reads the wall on the second of the tenth and is not the fourth of the tenth, that being the Sallow line's own date and two years of Volume 01 away.
- **Second and third weeks of the tenth to the first weeks of the eleventh month:** Chapter 0054. The request is of the **eighth of the eighth month**; **Jonas Cray comes out on the ninth of the ninth**; the form comes back **about nine weeks later, so in the eleventh month**, on a post; Rosamund reads it on the evening it comes; the copyist is at the table at about the seventh hour. **The granddaughter's question is the second of the four and it is the chapter's whole finding.**
- **Third week of the eleventh month:** Chapter 0055, the Docket of Powers, at about the fourth and fifth hours. The examination is fixed for the **eleventh of the first month**.
- **Fourth week of the eleventh month:** Chapter 0056. The finding came back on the **fourth**; the levy is read at the trestle on the **eleventh**; the woman at the far end of the vats is asked on the **ninth**; the eleventh door is the **twenty-second**; the copyist's list is the **twenty-fourth**; the fifth question is posted on the **twenty-sixth**; **the letter to Marek is written on the twenty-eighth at about the ninth hour and posted on the morning of the twenty-ninth.**
- **Second of the twelfth month:** Chapter 0057, the continuance application, the second hour, refused at about the seventh. **Chapter 0058** is the same day, in the old records room from about the fifth hour to the sixth; the first copy goes to the gate by cart in the evening, the third goes down the river by the morning packet, and **Tamsin comes up to the Received Room at about the eighth hour of that night with the third copy, puts the custody form on Marek Kest's table, and gets the second half of their rule answered in words with a date.**
- **Twelfth to the twenty-fourth of the twelfth month:** Chapter 0059. **The bar goes up on the twelfth at about the second hour**; Jonas Cray comes up the yard on the **nineteenth** at about the second hour; **Gideon Harrow comes up the yard two days before the bridge is carried back, to read the list of nine because she wrote on it that he might**; **the bridge is carried back open on the twenty-fourth**.
- **First to ninth of the first month of the year after:** Chapter 0060. The register entries are made **from the fourth over nine days**; **Iolanthe Rees comes on the sixth at about the third hour**; the promise is put on the wall on the **second of the eighth month of the year before**, and **Sarn walks up the upper lane on the eighth of the first month at the seventh hour**; the trial notice comes down on the **ninth** and is read out to a queue.

**Fixed dates carried forward that Volume 02 may not move.** The Venn term expired on the **nineteenth of the second month** with nothing decided. Tavi Venn was examined on the **twenty-sixth of the fifth** by Odile Fenn and the fee was entered on the **sixth of the fourth**, seven weeks before. Hallis Dren's quarterly return is the **eleventh of the tenth** of the ninth year and is still dormant and armed and known to nobody. The Nery Caudle signature is the **twenty-ninth of the tenth** of the ninth year and the money was collected on the **ninth of the twelfth**. Ostyn Vare's audit is the **seventh of the twelfth**. The Cinder Court sat on the **seventeenth of the sixth**; the referral left Mosswake on the **twenty-fourth of the fifth**; the Mosswake record came down the river on the **twenty-ninth of the sixth** in eleven days. The copy list was handed over on the **eleventh of the seventh**. The first three Emberwatch letters went on the **fifth of the sixth**; Nell went up the pass on the **twenty-second of the seventh** and came back on the **seventh of the eighth**. **Corvin Dray's trial is the second of the second month, the second hour, the chamber of the Auremar district court**, and the four things attached to it are the four read out on the fifteenth of the seventh month of the ninth year. **The flue in the Mosswake old records room is booked for the fourteenth of the second month** and the fourth cycle's copies are still not made.

## The Volume 01 Threads This Batch Moved, And The Ones It Did Not

**Moved.** The **limited administrative inquiry** now exists, dated and signed, and is a live instrument rather than an unanswered file. **Tamsin holds the public copies** under a form with *None* in the office box, and the private book is established as unreachable. **The sixty households are thirty-one named, a press, a third-cycle bundle, a retired clerk in a kitchen, a flue, and a column of none named** — the largest unpaid debt is now half paid and its remainder is on the page in chalk. **The classification question exists** with a register, a finding, a bar, a referral and a date. **Lowcross exists** with a bill, a fund, a box, a file with a hole in it, a hearing, a refusal, a bar, a two-day error and a list of nine names. **Nell has written to Marek** and has not forgiven the ninth month. **Sarn has delivered the line to Rennie Sallow** and has entered the promise she broke.

**Not moved, and each with a named holder.** **The Cinder Court's full sitting on the sealed instruction** is on the court's own list, with jurisdiction, **with no date.** **The second sealed page is shut**, with one refusal in a form and one line in a list and **no third**. **The Ashfall bundle is not opened** and has not moved from the second shelf. **The Kest property-and-debt record is reopened and not cleared**, and the redaction stands, and a Chancery roll of the year of the fire is in a tin in Greyfen in the house of a locksmith who is not to be written to without his saying so. **The counting-house mark is a mark and not a name** and goes into a trial in the second month as a mark. **Hallis Dren's quarterly return is dormant, armed and known to nobody.** **The ninety-word corridor instruction of the ninth of the ninth month is unfiled in Nevin Trost's hands**, and Marek has not written to him, and the reason is that the rule he made to protect himself is protecting Trost. **The red thread and the thread in the fold and the Ashfall stitch are three things and are not joined.** **The two documents remain two documents.** **Ostyn Vare has still not answered a letter.** **The Millbrook hoop gauge is still gone.** **The Greyfen levy is lost and the next four houses are the ones who will be out.** **The four pages of the copy list do not agree and nobody has tidied them**, and the sixth column is still empty and will stay empty. **The professional copyist's four pence a sheet and her waiting list are still the price at which a person can answer without owing anybody.** **Bryn Mawer is seventeen and copying in a counting house with a mark on the sign.**

## What A Volume 02 Batch 0002 Writer Inherited, In One Page, As At The Start Of That Batch

*Superseded by **What A Volume 02 Batch 0003 Writer Inherits, In One Page** at the foot of this file. Kept as the record of what the batch was given, and a later batch may not write against it.*

- The **Cinder Court's full sitting** on the sealed First Seat instruction, with jurisdiction, **and no date.** The whole of Volume 01's larger question is on a list.
- **Corvin Dray's trial, the second of the second month, the second hour, the chamber of the Auremar district court**, confined to the local forgery charges, his own ninety-one leaves, and the thirty-eight lines in three districts, with the mark going in as a mark and no client named and no client to be asked for. His plea was refused once, on the eighteenth of the ninth month of the ninth year, and was read back and not refused again.
- **Marek's examination on the eleventh of the first month at the fourth hour**, in the Docket's own room, before **the intake clerk of the Chancery and a copyist of the third gallery**, and the reframed question going up to the Cinder Court in room four with a condition on its shape.
- **The application in the fourth month of next year at Lowcross**, with a copy of the schedule in a solicitor's hands, a works officer who has undertaken to come up a yard once a month without a good coat, and a bill of nineteen pounds three and fourpence against six pounds and eightpence and a penny a day.
- **A bridge carried open on unpaid labour for about eleven weeks.** Nobody is liable for it and it is being done anyway, and the list of nine names is the only record and it is on the back of a form in a woman's kitchen.
- **Eighteen returns that never came back and six copies that were never made**, and a flue booked for the fourteenth of the second month.
- **The second sealed page, shut. The Ashfall bundle, unopened, four feet from where he works. The ninety words, unfiled. The quarterly return, dormant. The fourth envelope, in a drawer, addressed to a standing assessor of a furnace town, written in the fourth week of the first month of last year, and the one still going to matter in nine years.**
- **Tamsin Rook's independent copy, in a book of her own with no office on it, in a chest she has not described, which no office in this book can reach** and which is now the load-bearing form of the whole case.
- **A copy of the Cinder Court's record still sitting unused on a counting-house counter in Auremar**, and Bryn Mawer still at seventeen.
- **Nell Kest's practice in four buildings in three districts that does not belong to her, a letter from her brother, and a fourth trade that has answered before it was asked.**

## The Volume 02 Batch 0002 Record, Chapters 61–70

**`outline/batches/volume-02-batch-0002.md` is the card source for this batch and exists**, and carries a closing section recording eight divergences between its cards and the page. The batch opened the volume's second movement on two fixed dates and brought the case back to a man with a signature.

### The Volume 02 Batch 0002 Word-Count Record

The command is the same one this project has used since the Volume 01 close: for each chapter, from the repository root, `sed 's/[[:space:]]*$//' chapters/volume-02/chapter-00NN.md | wc -w`.

| Ch | Words | Ch | Words |
