| 61 | 4,375 | 66 | 3,212 |
| 62 | 4,010 | 67 | 3,192 |
| 63 | 3,643 | 68 | 4,111 |
| 64 | 3,184 | 69 | 3,820 |
| 65 | 3,746 | 70 | 4,485 |

**Total across ten chapters: 37,778 words. Average 3,778.** Shortest 3,184 (Ch 64), longest 4,485 (Ch 70). **The manuscript stands at 273,527 words across seventy chapters** (202,117 in Volume 01, 33,632 in Volume 02 Batch 0001, 37,778 here). **These are post-repair figures**: the fix pass after `logs/batch-0002.review.log` corrected dates and counts and rewrote the discovery in Chapter 62, and the pre-repair figures were 37,307 total, 3,731 average, 3,145 (Ch 66) to 4,488 (Ch 70). **Four of the ten are inside 3,200–4,000** (63, 65, 66 and 69). **Chapters 61 and 70 are over the ~4,200 ceiling and both were kept deliberately:** 61 carries a formal examination in a room with a stove in it, and 70 carries the batch climax, the sitting date, the ninety words and the volume's door. **Chapters 62 at 4,010 and 68 at 4,111 are over the band and inside that ceiling**, 68 because it carries the fourth place and the finding that ends the volume's instrument and 62 because the repaired discovery is a longer scene that was not trimmed to hit a number. **Chapters 64 and 67 sit under the 3,200 floor** at 3,184 and 3,192 and are in or near the 2,800–3,400 aftermath band. Nothing was padded and nothing was split.

### The Five Locks This Batch Held

- **The two documents remain two documents.** The **MC/FS circular** is a **Standing Office of Provincial Continuities** paper, base issue of the second month, run five hundred and sixty, amendment sheet of the **twenty-second of the ninth month**, run **nine hundred and forty**; the **First Seat quarterly return** is a dragon seat's paper and is a permission, not a crime, and **is still in no reply**. **No instrument anywhere joins them, the circular was not before the Cinder Court and is not examined there, and only Marek Kest and Tamsin Rook know both exist.** Nothing in Chapters 61–70 puts them in one sentence, one file or one argument. **Hallis Dren's return of the eleventh of the tenth is still dormant, armed, and known to nobody in the story.**
- **The four appearances of the substituted wording are four instances of one practice and not four documents in a chain, and no chapter in Chapters 61–70 writes them as a chain, in one sentence, in date order.** The Sallow file is the fourth month of the **fifth year**; the MC/FS amendment sheet is the **twenty-second of the ninth month**; the guild's AS/2 instruction on entering a second instrument is **four years and four months older than the Venn correction** and is about tool debts; the district works return of Auremar's lower district is the **fourth month of the sixth year**. **The public-necessity sentence was stated on the twelfth of the ninth month and printed on the twenty-second and no chapter puts it in an older file. The Caudle notice remains a fact about a number and a date and is not a seam.**
- **The second sealed page stays shut and there is no third refusal of it.** Examinations holds one refusal in a form and Marek's handover list holds the second as a line in his own hand. **The fifth document Pell Vey signs in Chapter 69 is a refusal of a question about an arrangement and is a new document, not a third refusal of the sealed page.** **The red stitch in Ivo Harker's tin, the thread in the fold of the sealed page, and the working stitch in the Ashfall bundle are three different things** and no two of them are joined in a sentence in Chapters 61–70. **The Ashfall bundle is not opened** and is not moved from the second shelf of the Received Room.
- **Sivra Oris and Seryn Oris are never merged and Seryn is not named in this batch.** Sivra's sworn statement of the third of the fourth may not be cited in any other matter and her second box is empty for ever. **The Cinder Court still does not know who authorised the sealed instruction** and Chapter 70 does not supply it. The source leaf did not move.
- **Marek performs no magic anywhere in Chapters 61–70.** He reads nothing. The only two readings in the manuscript are Chapter 8 (twelfth of the ninth month of the ninth year, at the Mosswake rehearing) and Chapter 40 (sixth of the fourth month, alone, on a step at Reed Cut), both under the same **undischarged limited Venn audit**, both with their costs paid. The batch's use of them is the examination and the Cinder Court's answer, and **neither is a reading**. The inability to lie lapsed on the page in Chapter 18 and does not return. No valid exception, counter-seal, mixed warrant, true-name reading, Roll Assembly or Stage 2 ability appears, and **the word *exception* does not appear in any of the ten chapters.** **Marek is barred from any audit in any jurisdiction in any office until examined, and the examination of the eleventh of the first month does not discharge the bar.**

### Rules Established On The Page In Volume 02 Batch 0002

1. **A bar is not a punishment. A bar is a register entry.** A Docket of Powers keeps a register of persons recorded as having performed a scale reading under a recognised authority, one page to a year, four lines to an entry, about fourteen a year because a reading needs a permission. **The only procedure for being entered is a written application to the Docket stating the authority under which the person reads.** It is one line long, it has existed for about twelve hundred years, and Marek Kest did not make one in the fourth month of last year, and the reason is now on the page: he did not want to be in a room, because a person who reads under a limited audit of a file can be asked by anybody holding the file whether he read it fairly.
2. **An examination is not a hearing, because there are no rules for one, and the office that holds the bar says so in advance.** A man who is being examined is examined before two people a stranger named, in the room of the office that holds the bar, and the master of that office is in the room and is not one of the two and says in advance that he will not be on the side of the man examined.
3. **An examination record is filed in the file and not in the register, because the register is a register of persons who read and putting an examination in it would be a lie in the only book anybody trusts.**
4. **A copy of a record is worth nothing and is worth everything depending on where it is sent.** A copy of a record in a building with an office over the door can be obtained by writing to the man who runs the building. A copy in four rooms above a cooper's shop, with no office and nothing on the door, cannot. **`None` is a person who cannot be instructed and this is now applied by a clerk to himself.**
5. **A high court will answer a question about a person and will not answer a question about a record with no person in it, and it says so in a form with a name at the foot, and the form is the answer.**
6. **A date is not to be fixed for a matter in which no party can be named, and when a date is fixed for that matter it is to be fixed with a name at the foot of it.** The Cinder Court stated this in a form on the twenty-ninth of the first month and **broke it on the sixth of the third month**: the notice of the eighteenth of the seventh month carries the device and nothing at the foot of it.
7. **A document with a man's name at the top of it is a document about that man.** This is Pell Vey's own argument, from the caption of the referral of the eighth month of last year, and it is now the volume's standing instrument against itself. It works first on the **officer's return of the second of the second month**, which has the intake clerk's name at the foot of it, and which the clerk is proud of and does not yet know what it is worth.
8. **A person who signs a column headed *by whose authority* is discharging an office and is not admitting authorship of a thing.** Every sheet that has left the Notaries' Table in sixty years has a fourth line, and a supervisor's name in it is the office acting, not a man deciding.
9. **A supervisor who arranged a thing is not a person who wrote a clause.** Nobody wrote *the present holder*. Nobody wrote the public-necessity sentence. A man in a good coat used three words already in the district's mouth, and the Table issued a direction to a window, and **a window is not a room.**
10. **You can hear a person and still lose them, if the record is put in front of them first.** This is the batch's pivot and it is the finding of the whole volume so far: the instrument this case rests on — *a person affected may be heard about it in a room, in a place that recognises the room* — has a hole in it, and the hole is that a person who has been warned about a document is a person who is now afraid of it, and a person afraid of a document cannot get in front of it. The widow in the Venn matter **was** heard, and had a lawyer's husband on a wall and forty people, and was defeated by a third issue of a witness order with a certificate pressed into the first line.
11. **A delivery stub is a fact about a book and not about a document and not about a person.** Four lines on cardboard: enclosed; to whom and where; on whose instance it is sent; by whose authority it is sent. The Table numbers everything it sends and everything it makes for a thing that is going to be sent out of one run at one counter in one hand for about sixty years, so a number cut into the edge of a plate is a number of that run. **There is one book in Auremar with that run in it and it is in a press room off the Crown Kiln stair, because a stub is not a record and a record belongs in a gallery.**
12. **A matrix is not a ledger.** A plate proves that an impression was made and cut from steel. It does not say who asked for the impression. The asking is in a book and not on the steel, and a tool is a thing anybody can buy.
13. **A mark is not a signature. A mark says *this is one of ours* to somebody who already knows what ours is, and a mark carries a direction.** A signature says who did it. A mark is a way of saying a thing to the one person in the room who is looking for it, and a direction is the one thing in the world that does not need anybody's permission to be followed. Nine people learned this in one week in the second month of the eleventh year and **seven plates went into a district court and no house was identified by any of them.**
14. **The protection order protects the persons and not the objects, and the two are one arrangement.** A person who may not be spoken to except through an officer cannot be asked a question, and a person who cannot be asked a question cannot be a witness, and a person who cannot be a witness is a line in an inventory. Eleven people stood nine feet apart from each other in a corridor for three hours and nobody spoke.
15. **A copy of a book is a witness and can be asked what it does not contain.** A certification that a copy is a true copy is worthless if the copy is short. The number on the wrapper is the only thing in a city that says a leaf was ever there.
16. **A clerk who finds a filing without a reason writes *reason not known* against it and does not supply the reason**, because a clerk who fills in a gap in a reason is a clerk who has decided what the reason was. It is four words and it is worth nothing and it is what a clerk has.
17. **A price is a thing that lets a person answer without owing anybody.** A trade will answer a stranger in writing on its own paper for two shillings and a house it has dealt with for nothing, and the two shillings is not for the ink. **A door with no charge on it is still a door**, and an answer a person did not ask for is not an answer to the asker.
18. **A limited plea is a new act and not a second refusal.** It admits the counts and neither admits nor denies anything touching any person or office not named. It is not absolution and it is not a pardon. A plea refused on the eighteenth of the ninth month was read back off the record on the second of the second month so that the file shows the refusal was not made twice.
19. **A court may refuse in advance to ask a man a question, and may enter that it has not asked and will not, and the reason is that a man who cannot be got to name a client can be made to say anything by a person who wants him to.**
20. **A pad is not a book and an instruction handed to a man in a corridor leaves no stub.** The ninety words name nobody and cannot be traced, and that is the reason a person can be the only holder of the only copy of the only document that convicts him.
21. **A refusal entered in a room, in the same form, with a name at the foot, read back, and signed on the record of the refusal, is worth more than an answer**, because a refusal can be read in nine years by a person who has never met either of them, and an answer given in a room cannot. **Five documents now carry Pell Vey's name in this matter.**
22. **A temporary office is the only office in this empire that is honest about when it stops, and it is the only one nobody in the Roll Hall has ever had to administer.** Said out loud in a room by a man who refuses to say what it is for.
23. **A person is not a record, and the Cinder Court's own answer in its own hand says it cannot answer a question about a record without a person to examine, and there is nobody in it.** That sentence is the door the whole volume has been standing in front of since the twenty-ninth of the first month, and the batch closes with it still shut.

### The Examination, The Eleventh Of The First Month, At The Fourth Hour

- **Held in the Docket of Powers' own room, three rooms off the corridor behind the third gallery, with a stove in it lit before Marek Kest arrived.** Before **the intake clerk of the Chancery** and **a copyist of the third gallery**, both named by **Oswin Marrow** and not by Marek, and Marrow is in the room and is not an examiner and says in advance that there are no rules for an examination and that the two of them will make up a form of words, and that he will not be on the side of the man examined.
- **Tamsin Rook is one of the two examiners and had entered both of Marek's readings into the Docket's register in her own hand on the fourteenth of the ninth month without asking him.** She noticed the position in the first four minutes and entered one line about it in the inside cover of her own book before she asked any question, and read it back.
- **The first question asked was *what is a scale reading*, by the intake clerk, because he had three headings on a sheet of foolscap and could not find a fourth.** Marek answered it badly and then properly, and the clerk wrote down the second answer, and the three lines of the answer are the three lines of a roll-reading, and **the clerk noticed that this was not a coincidence and asked that it be left in the file as a thing nobody has looked at.**
- **Marek Kest does not contest the entry, does not contest the bar, and does not ask for it to be lifted, and says so twice before he is asked.** The bar on any audit in any jurisdiction in any office **stands and is not discharged by the examination.**
- **The finding of the morning is his and is entered in his own hand: that the procedure for entry in the Docket's register is a written application stating the authority, and that he did not make one in the fourth month of last year, and that the reason is that he did not want to be entered.**
- **His account of the second reading is entered as *the account of the person examined and is not a finding of this Docket*, and Oswin Marrow countersigned that one line specifically and countersigned nothing else.**
- **He describes the second reading out loud for the first time to anybody** — the sixth of the fourth month, about the seventh hour, alone, on a step at Reed Cut, not asked and not stopped — and the reason he had never said it in a room is that a person who says *the record carries nothing* in a room is a person who has been told what to think about by a thing that cannot be cross-examined.
- **He asked for two things and got them:** that the record be made out in the form used and filed in the Docket's **file** and not in the register; and that a copy be sent to **four rooms above a cooper's shop near the Weir Cut side**, which has no office and nothing on the door, on the stated ground that he would rather be hard to find than easy to explain.
- **The intake clerk of the Chancery has now written the time in the margin in ink and initialled it seven times for the same man and has never been thanked, and the count is on the page in Chapter 61 and again in Chapter 65, and no chapter may retire it.**

### Halvard Riet, And What He Can And Cannot Certify

- **Nine years at the Inheritance Desk, asked for the Venn box and not the Kest one, given the Kest one as well, took it on purpose, and has never told anybody he took it on purpose.**
- **The six-item handover list is in Marek Kest's hand with no heading on it** and is on the inside cover of the day's book. **Item three was answered and Riet has written to her in six lines, carrying it down himself and not leaving it on a counter**, and told her on the day that a list which says a thing is not so is worse than a list that is empty, and that the line saying *unanswered* protected the person and the line saying *answered* does not.
- **Item four is a matter that is not his and is his sister's. He has written under it, in his own hand and with a date: *not chased. Not to be chased by me.*** Nobody is to chase it and nobody is to tell her it was chased.
- **The certification.** The gallery's copy of the ledger has **ninety-one leaves numbered consecutively from one**, and **the wrapper it was made from is endorsed ninety-two** in the hand of the copyist of the sixth year, with the word *complete* under the ninety-one, **no figure against it, and in an ink that has not gone grey.** He certified what he had compared, **expressly did not certify the completeness of the copy, and recorded that no person had asked him to**, and the covering line says that nothing in the certification is in anybody's favour and one part of it is against his own gallery. It went to the district court on the thirtieth of the first month, a day and a half before the trial, with the figure ninety-two written on the outside. **The district court directed on the second of the second month that the original be produced**, and the clerk of the third gallery is named in the order as the office whose certification is the reason.
- **The Kest box has four openings and closings on the face of its wrapper, in twelve years, in four hands, and the third — the closing of the seventh year — has a line ruled for a reason and nothing in it.** Riet entered **reason not known** in the day's book against the fourth of the first month and said in the entry that he had not asked why, because a clerk who fills in a gap in a reason is a clerk who has decided what the reason was.
- **He copied the six items out again onto a fresh sheet, in the author's words and order, and added nothing to that copy.** The seventh item and the two lines under item four are on the sheet in the day's book, and the six items on the fresh sheet are the ones he was given, and **that is deliberate and is recorded in the chapter as the whole of what a clerk's decision is.**

### The Cinder Court's Answer Of The Twenty-Ninth Of The First Month

- **It answers the reframed question and only the reframed question.** The reframing was Marek Kest's, countersigned onto the Docket's own finding by Oswin Marrow: *whether a record in which such a reading was entered, being a record of a court and not of this Docket, is altered in its effect by the finding that the reading was unauthorised*, with a condition that the question say what it is a finding about.
- **First: the court will not say that the record is altered and will not say that it is not altered**, and it does not say which answer it would give if it could, and it will not answer a question about the effect of a finding upon a document it has not seen.
- **Second, with a name at the foot: a Docket of Powers keeps a register of persons and not of records; the finding before the court is a finding about a person; and this court cannot answer a question about a record without a person to examine, and there is no person before it whom it may examine.**
- **Third: the question as first referred — whether a reading so performed was of any legal effect whatever — is not answered, is not withdrawn, and stands referred.** This is the answer `outline/volume-02.md` assigns to Chapters 71–80, and it is still outstanding at the end of this batch.
- **Fourth: the court will not give a date for the escalated sitting**, and gives the reason **in a form**: a date is not to be fixed for a matter in which no party can be named, and when a date is fixed for that matter it will be fixed with a name at the foot of it. **Chapter 70 records that this form is not complied with.**
- **Copies: two, in plain words, in a copyist's hand.** One to **Silas Sowle for the household**, with four words on the front and no office on it. One into the **Docket of Powers' own file**, because the Docket referred the question. **A third thing went down the river in the same packet** — a transcription of the Cinder Court's **first** order of the seventeenth of the sixth month, for the wall at the Mosswake window, with five words on the front and no office and no name.
- **Tamsin Rook's finding, in her own book: the answer is a door and it is not a wall, and she cannot walk through it, because the door is a person to examine and she has no authority over anything and cannot compel a soul. And it does not help Lowcross, because Lowcross is about a person and this is about a record, and there is nobody in the crossing at Lowcross who is a record.**
- **Tamsin Rook has begun a list: *things I have been wrong about, and the day I found out*, with two dated entries**, and has written under them that she is not going to fill the column.

### The Trial, The Second Of The Second Month, The Second Hour

- **The chamber of the Auremar district court. The trial is confined** to the local forgery charges, the entries in his own ledger of **ninety-one leaves**, and the **thirty-eight lines in three districts** against which a mark appears, and **no count arising out of the sealed instruction or out of any office** was tried, and his appearance to be tried is not an admission touching any person or office not named.
- **A limited plea is put by the court, granted, read back, and accepted, and it is a new act.** The **minute of the refusal of the eighteenth of the ninth month** is read out in open court so that the file shows the refusal was not made a second time.
- **Finding: guilty on the counts in the schedule** — the thirty-eight lines in three districts and the sale of a third witness mark in ninety-eight instances, of which twenty-two are in the Reed Cut district, six in the Venn file and one in the Dunn file — and **not guilty of nothing; no other count was before the court and none was tried.**
- **The mark is entered as a mark and not as a name, and the court does not know what house keeps it, and does not repeat that in the room as an excuse.** Exhibit 12 is a certificate **pressed cold and not laid**, with no rim, no second impression, no variation in pressure across its width, and a card that does not appear to have been damped, described into the record in four lines.
- **The court, on its own motion and asked by nobody, enters that no person in it has asked the man for the name of a client or the name of a house, that it will not ask him in this matter or any other before it, and that if anybody does it is to be entered and put to her.** The reason is in the minute.
- **Sentence, four lines.** Three years in the district house, with the period from the ninth month of last year credited. The ledger retained in the custody of the court, a copy to be made and sent to such persons as the court directs. **The court has no power to restore the sixty-one certificates suspended on the seventeenth of the sixth month, nobody asked it to, and the question of them is not before it.** He is to be examined on the question of the mark and of the practice, and his answer is to be used.
- **Two further lines, read twice, neither asked for.** **The eleven persons named in the order of the seventeenth of the ninth month of the ninth year are not named in any account of this trial, in this court or in any published account of it.** **The original of the ledger of ninety-one leaves is directed to be produced**, because the certification from a clerk of the third gallery says the copy is short by one leaf and the copy is not the court's to make.
- **The officer at the door is the young clerk of the intake desk.** His return of the sheets has his own name at the foot of it, is the first piece of paper in his life with his name at the top of it, and he has worked out that **a document with a clerk's name at the top of it is a document about that clerk**, and has decided he would rather have the return than not have it because there is no column on a return for a clerk to write *I would rather this were not here*.

### Brammel, And The Mark That Is Not A Signature

- **About sixty-one, forty years in the province, eleven years a client of Corvin Dray's, and the man who identified the green box under the window as the working box and not the archive box on the day of the inventory.**
- **He has his four papers in a green box with his own name and mark on it, returned in a leather case with a receipt dated **the nineteenth of the tenth month of the ninth year**, signed by a clerk, which is the receipt of the return in Chapter 17 and the box has been in his house for about sixteen months.**
- **Inventory item nine was reworded at his request sixteen months ago, at a counter on the eighteenth of the tenth month of the ninth year, and now reads *one green box, four papers, of a client of eleven years*.** His name is out of it. The description is in the fourth column in the same hand as everything else, with a space where a person goes. **His mark is on the wrapper of the inventory, and a man who has seen the mark twice can find the box in a room of sixty boxes in about four seconds.** The reword made him safer on the page and more findable in the world, and both things were done correctly.
- **He cannot write. His mark is not a signature; it is the whole of what he has instead of one, and the reason he has done nothing for sixteen months is that taking the mark off the wrapper would leave him unable to hold a box, sign a receipt, or say *this is mine*.** Nobody has ever asked him what his mark is.
- **He is one of the eleven protected people and is not a party to the trial. He asked the clerk at the back of the gallery whether a person named in an inventory may be called, and was told that a party is a person named in a count, and that an inventory name is evidence that a thing was in a room and not evidence of anything about the thing's owner, and he asked for the question to be asked anyway and it was.**
- **At about the fifth hour the officer at the door asked him a question, in the court, through the proper door, by a person with the right to ask it, and it was about whether the leather case was returned to him or to the office.** He answered it truthfully. **It is the first time anybody has asked Brammel anything in sixteen months and it is about four inches of leather.** Nobody arranged it and nobody prevented it and the question is in the paper taken at the door, in a column, with a figure against it.
- **He did not go up and ask whether a person who is not a party can be added to a record on his own initiative, because what he would have got is a line in a minute about a man trying to be noticed.**
- **Four of the eleven stood about nine feet apart in a corridor for three hours and nobody spoke.** The order protects the persons and not the objects, and the two are one arrangement.
- **His item nine is not a name and no chapter may turn it into one. His mark is his own signature and is not the counting-house mark, which is a filled square with a bar through it and a notch cut out of the top left corner, and the two are never joined in a sentence.**

### Garren Pyle, The Plate, And The Nine

- **A certifier with a room over a yard in the Kiln sheds, a press he does not own, a bench he made himself, and about four hundred certificates of which sixty-one are suspended.** He attended the hearing in the district of Mosswake with a certificate in a leather case with a cold press mark and the copied third notch, **withdrew the record rather than read it, would not say whose hand pressed the matrix, and gave his name and the name of the office that instructed him, in that order.**
- **He is not sorry and says so in the first ten minutes**, and gives two things he is sorry about, in an order, and asks that it not be written in the other order.
- **He has held the press plate for eleven years.** On the face of it, cut in steel, the mark. On the edge, cut shallow, **a number in a hand he cannot place.** No name anywhere on it. It came back eleven years ago with a four-line letter saying it was not wanted back and was to be held; the letter is in a file in another building and the plate is in a drawer in this one, and **the plate is the only object in the empire he is the last owner of.**
- **He handed it in on the ninth of the second month with four lines of his own**, and a registrar told him in a form with a name at the foot that **a matrix is not a ledger** and that a tool is a thing anybody can buy, and the plate is entered as a plate and is not entered as a name, and the number cut into the edge is entered as a fact about a book and not about the mark.
- **The week: seven doors on foot over two days, three of which asked which number was on it rather than what he wanted done with it.** **A woman of about sixty who has cut plates for twenty-two years in a lane** said it is a sending number out of the Table's run, that she has cut about nine hundred plates off that run, and that nobody has ever asked her what a number on a plate is a number *of*. **Seven plates went in by the fourteenth**, three carried in by their holders and four cut and carried in by her. **A registrar's note records that nine persons produced everything they held, that two of the nine held nothing that was a plate and said so, and that the reason nobody had asked them in five months is on the face of the protection order.**
- **The finding of the week, in Pyle's own words in a room: a mark is not a signature; a signature says who did it, and a mark says *this is one of ours* to somebody who already knows what ours is, and a mark carries a direction, and a direction is the one thing in the world that does not need anybody's permission to be followed.** **No house has been identified by any of the seven plates and no chapter may identify one.**

### Nell Kest, The Fifth Question's Answers, And Four Minutes

- **Four trades have answered the fifth question, with the price in the body of the letter and no name on it**, and one of the answers **is** a price: a trade will answer a stranger in writing on its own paper for **two shillings** and a house it has dealt with for nothing, and the two shillings is not for the ink, it is so that nobody owes anybody anything.
- **The other three say the same thing in four hands:** four pounds ten each way, or eleven if you go on the day they go, two days lost at four shillings a day, a bond of eleven pounds, four years before a return without a second bond. One house went to Thornfen and is not sorry and does not know anybody here.
- **A standing assessor of a third trade, fifty-one, nineteen years in it, four houses owing money and a bench he cannot fill, wrote to her before she asked him anything**: that four questions at no charge is a mistake, and that his association will take any house pressed out of a trade at his own cost and without a bond, **and that the offer is not for the person who asks him, it is for whoever writes next.** She did not read it out in the lane and wrote that down as a decision and not an instinct. **A concession made for a reason is not a fact. It is a door, and a door with no charge on it is still a door.**
- **The number on the flat of the trestle in chalk, with no name against it:** two shillings to ask four people with power one question and be told the truth by one of them, against **two pounds five and a quarter a quarter, four times a year, thirty-two pounds and fourpence out of a lane in a year.** **Being told the truth costs a twentieth of what not being told costs** — twenty shillings against the house's own quarter, and that quarter is **two pounds and a farthing**, so 480¼ pence against 24 is twenty. **The chapter once printed *a twenty-fifth*, which is not this ratio; the corrected figure is *a twentieth*, and no later batch may print a twenty-fifth.**, and the four houses that signed are not the four that will be out, **and the reason they are not is that they signed.**
- **The four houses' children were at the back again**, nine of them, and the girl of seventeen who said in the eleventh month that it was going to be her in a year has not been asked again since.
- **Marek Kest came on the twelfth of the second month, on two days' leave out of a room with a barred door, to be told about the ninth month. It took about four minutes, not four hours.** What she said is that he was right not to tell her, that she is not going to be one of the people who forgives a thing because it has been said out loud, and what she wants is that he had to make a decision about her alone in a room with nobody to stop him and that it was not a small one. **Neither of them can afterwards remember what the other one said, and she has written that down in nine words with the day on it.** **No romance begins here and none is implied.**

### The Fourth Place, The Stub, And A Supervisor's Hand

- **The fourth place is the press room off the Crown Kiln stair, about a hundred yards from the copying alcove**, in a room where a bag is made up and documents are made and not kept, in a building where about four hundred people work every day and not one has been in it to read anything. **The first three places were the Forms Received rack, a lane four hundred and thirty miles down a river, and a shop window in Greyfen with a price in it.**
- **The book is the delivery-stub book.** A stub is cardboard about five inches by three with four lines on it in a hand of about sixty years: *one, enclosed; two, to whom and where; three, on whose instance it is sent; four, by whose authority it is sent.* The Table sends about a hundred and forty things a month and every one has a stub and every stub is kept, because a building that cannot prove what it sent is a building that gets told about things it does not remember doing. **Nothing that goes out of that room is ever a case and there is no file number.**
- **The entry for the eleventh leaf, the ninth month of last year:** *One sheet, sealed, with a direction. To the clerk's window, Mosswake Roll Court, in the district of Mosswake, by the midweek packet. On the instance of a matter in the third gallery. **P. Vey.*** The form of words in the third line is used about nine times in thirty years in a building of four hundred people.
- **The sheet is gone.** It went to a river court four hundred and thirty miles away and neither Marek Kest nor Pell Vey nor any court in this city can get it, because a district court in another province does not answer a letter about a sheet. **The finding therefore rests on four lines of cardboard, and what it establishes is a method, not a wording.**
- **How the book was got out of the room: asked for in a room, in words, out loud, in front of somebody.** The clerk of the Table **said the no out loud first and then fetched his supervisor**, and asked for it to be written down that he said it in that order. **The deputy of the Notaries' Table, about forty, unnamed, brought the book down off the top shelf himself with a stool**, and asked whether the copyist had come for the stub or for the plate, and was told: for the number. He said that in eleven years he has watched people come into that room and go straight to line three, which is the client's name, and take it, and that every one of them took a name out of the building that did not belong to them.
- **The undertaking of the fourth of the first month is tested and works.** It is a sheet going out of the matter to an office outside the Roll Hall, so she brought it to the Received Room and he read it in front of her before it went, **and gave his reason in a room and out loud and not on a page** — a stub is a fact about a book and not about a man, and a document with a man's name at the top of it is a document about that man — and she agreed, **and it went anyway**, and they wrote *agreed* and the day on it.
- **The chapter's real finding is that the instrument this case rests on does not fit.** A person affected may be heard about it in a room, in a place that recognises the room; the widow in the Venn matter was heard, and had forty people and a lawyer's husband and a warning from a man in a good coat, and was defeated by a third issue of a witness order with a certificate pressed into the first line, because when the record spoke the record was a thing she had understood. **The person was heard. The record answered first.** And a person who has been warned about a document is afraid of it, and cannot get in front of it.
- **Marek Kest's name goes on that finding with the day on it and the word *accountable* and not the word *right*,** on the stated ground that there is not one line of this matter that says he was right, and that he would rather it were a document nobody could hold up in a room to prove that he had been right about something.

### The Refusal Of The Last Week Of The Second Month

- **Marek Kest asked Pell Vey one question in the press room off the Crown Kiln stair, having told him what the question was before he put the document down, and offered to bring no one.** The question is whether the Notaries' Table issued a direction to the clerk's window of the Mosswake Roll Court on the ninth month of last year to reissue a witness order with a certificate of the district entered first.
- **Pell Vey does not deny the hand and does not answer**, and his first sentence is the volume's standing instrument: *a document with a man's name at the top of it is a document about the man.* It is his own argument from the caption of the referral, where he put Marek Kest's name in the second line instead of the first.
- **The refusal is one page in the same form as the recorded refusals of the twentieth of the eleventh and the twenty-ninth of the third month, with his name at the foot**, and it states that he has answered no part of the question and denied no part of it, and gives the reason: **a person signing the fourth line of a delivery stub is discharging an office and is not thereby admitting authorship of a thing.** It also states, in the form, that a refusal with a name at the foot of it is worth more to the applicant than an answer would have been, because a refusal can be read back in nine years by a person who has never met either of them and an answer given in a room cannot.
- **Marek Kest read it back to him and Pell Vey signed the record of the refusal on the back of it, unasked, in about four seconds,** which is the practice Nevin Trost taught in the eleventh month of last year and which is now a habit of a man in a building where the person who taught it has left for a room with a barred door in it. **Five documents now carry Pell Vey's name in this matter.**
- **Pell Vey has still not asked about the yard of the fourth of the tenth month** and says why: the day he asks is the day the conversation becomes a negotiation, and he has watched about nine negotiations and every one of them ended with a document with a man's name at the top of it.
- **What he says, in a room with no witness, and which is not to be put in a file:** that he did not arrange a thing and he issued a form to a window, and a window is not a room; and that **Marek Kest will not be able to name the man who put the certificate into the first line, and that this is not a failure of his**, because a man who looks for a name for four years finds one, and then somebody will have the thing Pell Vey has just refused to be, made out of a man. He asks not to be thanked and says his speech is worth nothing.
- **Marek Kest's finding, in his own book:** a name since the seventh month of the ninth month of last year and a hand since the eighteenth of the second month, and **neither of them is a person**; the standing office that printed the sheet is still an office with no person in it; and the reason he stopped is that **building a case towards a person is the same move as building a chain out of four true facts and one invented join.** Three copies of the refusal exist and no fifth, and he wants that written down.

### The Date, The Ninety Words, And Oren Tal

- **The notice of the sixth of the third month: the sealed-instruction sitting is appointed for the eighteenth of the seventh month, at the second hour, in room four, with jurisdiction, on the court's own motion over the applicant's objection entered on the seventeenth of the sixth.** The notice is printed, it has the device at the head of it, and **there is nothing at the foot of it — about a half-inch of white paper where a signature goes.**
- **This is the sitting Sela Vint said in room four on the seventeenth of the sixth would come "in about a year," and it is four months and about twelve days after the sixth of the third month, and it is the second time the form has been used: the answer of the twenty-ninth of the first month said a date is not to be fixed for a matter in which no party can be named, and that when one is fixed it will be fixed with a name at the foot of it. It is not.**
- **What four months costs**, in Nevin Trost's own arithmetic: the Standing Office of Provincial Continuities prints an amendment sheet and distributes it on the ordinary list to forty-six addresses holding a flood docket; four districts receive it and believe a court has decided it; a river comes up in a district in the fourth month and a clerk at a window reaches for a form because the form is the only thing in the room. **The sentence that makes a date like that expensive is a magistrate's**, and it is **Joane Tranter's**, said in a room in the district court of Auremar's lower district in the twelfth month over a bridge, and Trost writes it out on the back of the notice and then puts her name on it.
- **The ninety words.** One sheet of the Notaries' Table's own writing paper, ninety words, **no heading, no office at the head of it, no signature**, handed to Nevin Trost in the corridor outside the fourth room of the Chancery on the **ninth of the eleventh of last year** with *this for the file*, and the person who handed it to him walked away. It is the instruction to receive and consolidate the request as a limited administrative inquiry, to fix the scope on one page, to put the name of the person conducting it on that page, and to write the reason for the name under it. **Every single thing the applicant asked for.**
- **He filed it on the sixth of the third month, in his own hand, with the date**, with a line asking that the filing be entered **as a cost and not as a finding.** It has been in a drawer about two months and he has written his reason for not filing it five times and crossed out four. The reason it stood is the date: a record is a thing that a person can be shown, and a document nobody has produced is a document nobody can be shown.
- **He gave a copy to Tamsin Rook and asked her to hold it**, because the Docket would enter it and its master would know where it came from for the rest of his life, and because **a man who files a thing about his own office has to have it in a book somewhere, and if it is in that book it is with somebody who cannot be instructed.** She copied it, read it back twice, and wrote *held by T. Rook, no office, the sixth of the third month* on the back. **She has stated in her own book that there is one more person who could be asked about that sheet and that she is not going to ask, because a pad is not a book, nobody keeps a pad, and there is no stub for an instruction handed to a man in a corridor, so what she is describing is the absence of a record and not the presence of one.**
- **Oren Tal entered on the eighteenth of the third month, in the third room off the corridor, unasked, and is a retired scale notary, sixty-one or two, with a burn-scarred hand.** He said he was trained in that building by a woman called Kest about thirty-one years ago, and that he does not know what became of her and has not asked. **He said out loud that the old office of First Custodian was supposed to be temporary** — a word in the operative words, and every office that has widened that office has done it by not putting the word in the sentence. **Tamsin Rook asked him what that is for and he refused her in a form**, saying he had watched three people in this matter do it better and that he had about four years' worth of things he could say and was going to say none of them. He put a folded leaf on the table, a copy of a schedule of the term made about forty years ago in a copyist's hand whose name he would not give in that office. He added, to the copyist and not to Trost, that **a temporary office is the only office in this empire that is honest about when it stops, and the only one nobody in that building has ever had to administer**, and he would not say what that was for. He asked not to be thanked and said he would leave if anybody thanked him. **He refused Tamsin Rook's question about the woman Kest in one word, and went down a stair of the Crown Kiln that smells of fired scale, and neither of them saw him again in this batch.**
- **Trost did not thank him**, put the leaf in a file of his own, and wrote the rest in a book he keeps at home with the day on it, on the nineteenth of the third month, because a thing said in a room by a man who is not an officer of anything is a thing said in a room. **Oren Tal's leaf is in a file of his own and Trost's own note about it is in a book with no office on it. Neither is in the Docket's register and neither is in the Cinder Court.**

### The Volume 02 Batch 0002 Calendar, The Eleventh Of The First Month To The Third Week Of The Third Month

**Use these dates rather than re-deriving them. No weekday name appears anywhere in this batch and none may be used to anchor a chain; the only weekday on the page in the whole book is the ninth of the fourth month of the ninth year being a Monday, and nothing may be derived from it. Months are numbered throughout.**

- **Eleventh of the first month:** Chapter 0061, the examination, at the fourth hour.
- **Nineteenth to thirtieth of the first month:** Chapter 0062. **The Glasswater answer is not found in the post on the nineteenth; the fact that it was answered is**, in the intake clerk's post-book, in a receipt line for the eleventh of the ninth month with T. Rook's initials against it — **the letter itself is hers, in a drawer in the copying alcove, and Riet never reads it and does not ask to.** The certification is written standing up on the **twenty-first** and sealed; the Kest wrapper is read on the **twenty-fourth**; the certificate goes down on the **thirtieth**.
- **Last week of the first month to the first of the second:** Chapter 0063. The answer comes down on the morning packet of the **twenty-ninth**; the transcription for Sarn's wall and the copy for Sowle go out with it; the letter to Sarn goes down the river on the **second of the second month**.
- **Second of the second month, the second hour:** Chapters 0064 and 0065, one trial, two points of view. The plea, the finding, the mark, the sentence and the return; Brammel in the gallery from the **second hour**, the court rising at about the **fifth**, the officer's question at about the **fifth hour**; the return written at about the **half hour after the sixth**; the woman of forty-eight at about the **seventh**.
- **Fourth to the fourteenth of the second month:** Chapter 0066. The plate is handed in on the **ninth**; the seven doors are walked on the **tenth** and the **eleventh**; the sentence about a mark is said in a room on the **twelfth**; all seven plates are in by the **fourteenth**.
- **Middle of the second month:** Chapter 0067. The four answers are read at the trestle on the **ninth**; the assessor of a third trade's letter is not read out in the lane; Marek Kest comes on the **twelfth** and the four minutes happen.
- **Second to the third week of the second month:** Chapter 0068. Sarn's refusal comes down on the **fifteenth**; the plate is entered in Tamsin's book on the evening of the **ninth**; the number is followed on the **tenth**; the stub is read and the finding brought to the Received Room on the **eighteenth**.
- **Last week of the second month:** Chapter 0069, the press room off the Crown Kiln stair. The refusal and the signature on the back of it are the same morning.
- **First to the third week of the third month:** Chapter 0070. The notice of the sitting is on the morning packet of the **sixth**, and the ninety words are filed on the evening of the same day; Oren Tal is in the room on the **eighteenth** at about the third hour of the afternoon; Trost's own note is written on the **nineteenth** at about the seventh hour at home.

### Figures Named In Volume 02 Batch 0002

Recorded so that a later batch does not invent replacements. These are working people with independent wants, not clues with motives. **None of them is a villain and none of them is a mouth for a power they do not hold.** **The Emberwatch clerk who itemised entry ninety-one remains unnamed and is not to be given a name without a card.**

- **Oren Tal:** a retired scale notary, **sixty-one or two, with a burn-scarred hand**, who trained in the Roll Hall with Ilyra Kest about thirty-one years ago and does not know what became of her and has not asked. **He says out loud in a room that the old office of First Custodian was supposed to be temporary, and refuses to say what that is for, in a form.** He gave a copy of a schedule of the term, made about forty years ago, in a copyist's hand whose name he would not give. **He is Volume 03's door and is not this batch's answer, and a later batch may not make him explain himself.**
- **The deputy of the Notaries' Table:** about forty, unnamed, who brought a sixty-year-old book down off a shelf himself with a stool and asked whether the copyist had come for the stub or for the plate. **He is not Pell Vey and he is not a replacement for him.** He said the no out loud first, before he fetched his supervisor.
- **The clerk of the Table with a name over the door:** unnamed, who said that a person who is not of this office could not take a book off a shelf in that room, and said it out loud on purpose so that his supervisor would be asked about the whole of it, and asked for it to be written down that he said it in that order.
- **The woman of about sixty who has cut plates for twenty-two years in a lane:** unnamed, who asked what number was on a plate, wrote it down, and cut and carried in four plates that nobody had thought to ask her for. **She is the third person in this batch who found a thing by asking a question she had not been asked.**
- **The standing assessor of a third trade:** fifty-one, nineteen years in it, unnamed, with four houses owing money and a bench he cannot fill, who **said yes before Nell Kest asked him anything**, offered to take four houses at his own cost and without a bond, and said in the same letter that the offer is not for the person who asks him. **He is not sorry and has not been asked to be, and a later batch may not turn him into a villain or a suitor.**
- **The young clerk of the intake desk:** unnamed on the page, about twenty-five, and the page now contains **a document with his name at the foot of it** — the officer's return of the sheets of the second of the second month. He counted every sheet at the door in two columns, in the order a thing arrives and the order it leaves, and has worked out that a document with a clerk's name at the top of it is a document about that clerk. **A later batch may use the return and may not turn him into a character without a card.**

## What A Volume 02 Batch 0003 Writer Inherits, In One Page

- **The original referral comes back, not this batch's reframed question.** The Docket's own question — *whether a reading so performed was of any legal effect whatever* — is **unanswered and stands referred**, and the answer to the reframed question has not touched it, and the answer that is coming is the worst of both: that the readings were of no legal effect whatever, **and** that the record they were entered in stands.
- **The limited audit that comes with it** will be in a form so narrow that it cannot be used at Lowcross, and the application at Lowcross is in the **fourth month**, with Silas Sowle holding a copy of the finding, a bill of **nineteen pounds three and fourpence** against a fund holding **six pounds and eightpence**, a works officer who has undertaken to come up a yard once a month at the seventh hour and not in a good coat, and **nobody liable for the crossing.**
- **The sealed-instruction sitting is the eighteenth of the seventh month, at the second hour, in room four, with jurisdiction, and the notice carries the device and nothing at the foot of it**, four months out, and everything the volume is trying to stop goes on happening until then.
- **The instrument the case rests on has a hole in it.** A person affected may be heard about it in a room, in a place that recognises the room; the person **was** heard; the record answered first. A person who has been warned about a document is afraid of it and cannot get in front of it. **Any second answer the volume finds must be a different instrument and not this one repeated.**
- **Pell Vey has refused in a form with a name at the foot and signed the record of it**, and has said in a room with no witness that he issued a form to a window and a window is not a room, and that Marek Kest will never name the man who pressed the certificate into the first line and that this is not a failure. **A later batch may not make him a villain and may not make him explain himself.**
- **The ninety words are filed, in a file of the matter, and a copy is in a book of Tamsin Rook's with no office on it.** There is no stub for them, there is no pad, and the person who handed them to Nevin Trost in a corridor is not on any page and cannot be found.
- **Oren Tal has been in a room and has said one sentence out loud and has refused to say what it is for, and has given a leaf in a copyist's hand whose name he would not give.** A temporary office is the only office in this empire that is honest about when it stops, and the only one nobody in the Roll Hall has ever had to administer.
- **The original of the ninety-one leaves is to be produced to the district court**, and one leaf is not in the copy, and a clerk of the sixth year wrote *complete* with no figure against it, and a clerk of nine years has entered *reason not known* against a filing of the seventh year.
- **Seven plates and nine certifiers, and no house identified by any of them**, and about nine people who know in one week what a mark is for.
- **Eleven protected people who may not be spoken to except through an officer, one of whom came to be asked a question and was asked one about a receipt**, and one of whom is about to be found by about forty more people who know there is a green box with a mark on it.
- **Still shut, still unjoined, still unasked:** the second sealed page, with one refusal in a form and one line in a list and **no third**; the Ashfall bundle, unopened, four feet from where he works; the red stitch, the thread and the Ashfall working stitch, three things, never joined; the two documents, which remain two documents, with **only Marek Kest and Tamsin Rook knowing both exist**; the **counting-house mark**, which is a mark and is not a name; **Seryn Oris, not named**; the fourth envelope in a drawer; the first Seat quarterly return, in no reply, with a dormant armed return of the eleventh of the tenth behind it that nobody in the story knows about.

## The Volume 02 Batch 0003 Record, Chapters 71–80

`outline/batches/volume-02-batch-0003.md` is the card source for this batch and exists, and carries a closing section recording the divergences between its cards and the page. **The batch took the volume's largest question and answered it, and the answer is two answers and a name on a document.**

The command is the same one this project has used since the Volume 01 close: for each chapter, from the repository root, `sed 's/[[:space:]]*$//' chapters/volume-02/chapter-00NN.md | wc -w`.
