**Total across ten chapters: 38,324 words. Average 3,832.** Shortest 2,882 (Ch 76), longest 4,245 (Ch 72). **The manuscript stands at 311,851 words across eighty chapters** (202,117 in Volume 01, 33,632 in Volume 02 Batch 0001, 37,778 in Volume 02 Batch 0002, 38,324 here). **Four of the ten are inside 3,200–4,000** (73, 75, 78 and 80). **Four are over 4,000 and two of the four are over the about-4,200 ceiling and were kept deliberately:** **72 at 4,245 carries a formal sitting in room four, two scenes in a received room, and a nine-item dictated list of paper; 74 at 4,204 carries a document discovery, a refusal with a reason in it, a form explained in a room, and the batch's structural finding**; **71 at 4,178 and 77 at 4,065 are over 4,000 and under that ceiling** — 71 carries the answer of a high court landing in a copying alcove and the whole of what it costs, and 77 is the seal, the name and the objection. **Two sit under the 3,200 floor: Chapter 76 at 2,882 is the aftermath band and is the shortest chapter in the volume so far**, and carries one question asked in a lean-to and four lines in a minute; **Chapter 79 at 3,166 is under the floor too**, carries a caught man, a question he will not answer and the finding that a caught person is a quantity and not a source, and is recorded here because this repository records every chapter that falls outside its band. Nothing was padded and nothing was split.

### The Locks This Batch Held

- **Marek performs no magic anywhere in Chapters 71–80.** He reads nothing. The only two readings in the manuscript are Chapter 8 (twelfth of the ninth month of the ninth year) and Chapter 40 (sixth of the fourth month, at Reed Cut), both under the same undischarged limited Venn audit, both costs paid. **The inability to lie lapsed on the page in Chapter 18 and does not return. No valid exception, counter-seal, mixed warrant, true-name reading, Roll Assembly or Stage 2 ability appears, and the word *exception* does not appear in any of the ten chapters.** **Marek remains barred from any audit in any jurisdiction in any office, the examination of the eleventh of the first month did not discharge it, and the answer of the eighth of the third month does not lift it and says so in the third line of its own face.**
- **The two documents remain two documents.** The **MC/FS circular** is a **Standing Office of Provincial Continuities** paper, base issue of the second month, run five hundred and sixty, amendment sheet of the **twenty-second of the ninth month**, run **nine hundred and forty**. The **First Seat quarterly return** is a dragon seat's paper, is a permission and not a crime, and is **still in no reply**; **Hallis Dren's return of the eleventh of the tenth is still dormant, armed, and known to nobody in the story.** **No instrument anywhere joins them, the circular was not before the Cinder Court and is not examined there, and only Marek Kest and Tamsin Rook know both exist.** Chapter 77 names the circular once, in a registrar's mouth, for the single purpose of establishing that the seat's notice **mentions no series, no run and no sheet**; **the First Seat quarterly return does not appear on the page in this batch at all**, deliberately, and its status is this paragraph.
- **The four appearances of the substituted wording are four instances of one practice and not four documents in a chain, and no chapter in Chapters 71–80 writes them as a chain, in one sentence, in date order.** **Nobody wrote the substituted wording and nobody wrote the sentence** — Chapter 69's finding, inherited and restated in Chapter 77, and it is the reason a notice that has no series in it is more dangerous than a sheet with nine hundred and forty copies of it.
- **The second sealed page stays shut and there is no third refusal of it.** **The red stitch in Ivo Harker's tin, the thread in the fold of the sealed page, and the working stitch in the Ashfall bundle are three different things** and no two are joined in a sentence in any of the ten chapters. **The Ashfall bundle is not opened** and does not move from the second shelf of the Received Room.
- **Sivra Oris and Seryn Oris are never merged.** **Sivra does not appear in this batch, is not her agent, and is not a mouth for her.** **Seryn Oris is named for the first time in this book, in Chapter 77, and is credited with nothing she did not do.**
- **No court in this batch is asked to and does find a person.** The Cinder Court **states again, in the fourth line of the answer of the eighth of the third month and again in a form in room four in the third week of the fourth month, that it does not know and cannot compel the authority under which the sealed instruction was given**, and that it knows of no person at the head of any office whose standing authority sets in type the sheet of the twenty-second of the ninth month.
- **The source leaf did not move.** **Corvin Dray is not re-litigated, no court re-refuses him, he is asked for no client, and the counting-house mark remains a mark.** **Seven plates are still in a district court and not one of them identifies a house.**

### Rules Established On The Page In Volume 02 Batch 0003

1. **The worst of both answers is the one that is final.** The two unauthorised readings were **of no legal effect whatever**, and the record they were entered in **stands**, is not altered, and every act done in reliance on it stands and no person who acted on it in good faith is to be questioned. **A reading of no legal effect is better than a permitted one and worse than a disowned one:** it reaches nothing, because a Docket keeps a register of persons, and it makes the record permanent.
2. **The sentence with no cost on it is the most dangerous sentence in the book.** *Every act done in reliance upon it stands, and no person who acted upon it in good faith is to be questioned* is eleven words and **it is the only sentence this case has produced that anybody might actually be glad of**, and it is the reason a district which wrote to about sixty households in letters in the sixth year is now answerable to nothing at all.
3. **A record that stands is the only proof either of two people will ever have.** The only record in the empire in which a girl of fifteen is entered as heard, and in which a household is entered as asked, in the wrong column, is now on a standing record with a judge's name at the foot of it. **It cannot be un-heard and it cannot be un-asked, and the same sentence that guarantees the first is what guarantees the second.**
4. **A grant is a size and not a shape, and the person who has to say so is the one person in the world who wanted it.** Nine paragraphs became one district in the ninth month of last year and a bar became one room in the eleventh month of the first; **the same sentence in different clothes, and the only difference was that last time the thing was still going to be useful somewhere.**
5. **A rule made between two people is an instrument in the empire and can be picked up by a stranger.** Silas Sowle learns *either of them may put a choice in writing with a date on it and the other must answer in a room, in words, with a date* off a sheet of paper with four words on the front and no office on it, and uses it, and it works, and the cost is that **the rule's own author has to be on the other side of it one day.** This is the undertaking of the fourth of the first month exercised a second time and **it is not retired and it is not amended.**
6. **`None` in the box headed *the office of the receiving person, if any* protects a holder and does not protect a paper.** It is a door with nothing behind it and the nothing is a person who cannot be instructed, and it is the only instrument anybody in this book has produced that has been any use against a document that cannot be compelled. **It protects the holding. It does not protect the loss.** The moment a question is asked about the paper rather than the holder, the door is behind the questioner. **And `None` is also the reason a person who cannot read a paragraph can use a form at all**, because the second box is about a person and a clerk at a counter fills it in four seconds without asking anything.
7. **A copy is a witness and can be asked what it does not contain, and a copy that has been carried in a coat for forty years is short by a line, and the line cannot be recovered, because paper does not remember ink.** And **a man who hands a room a short copy and calls it a copy has been telling the truth by handing over a thing.**
8. **A man who signs a column headed *by whose authority* is discharging an office** — Chapter 69's finding, running in reverse and now against the man who used it — **and here is a person signing for a seat, and the document is about a seal, and an office cannot be examined.**
9. **A device is a way of being in a room without being a person.** The form published in the reign of the third of the Ashfall line exists so that a person is not compelled by a seal. It is the volume's own instrument pointed the other way, and **a court that declines one has to write down why, and there is no why, and a judge who refuses a form her own court published has to invent a reason, and an invented reason in a minute book of that court cannot be told in nine years from a real one.**
10. **A document with a person's name at the top of it is a human clerk's instrument.** It works on a district works, a provincial court and a man in a good coat with a tray. **It does not cross a species.** The finding is a judge's, in a corridor, and it is the most expensive thing anybody has said to Marek Kest in a year.
11. **A bill is not the problem and never was.** The application at Lowcross failed for a reason that had nothing to do with nineteen pounds three and fourpence. **What the application needed was a name — a body that can be made answerable for a penny a day — and there has not been one in that district since a parish was dissolved in the sixth year and nobody in Lowcross knew, because the notice went to the parish.**
12. **The same nineteen words on the back of a returned form had been in a solicitor's coat for four months and nobody had ever read them out in a room.** *The form is returned because the form cannot be entered and not because the work is refused.*
13. **A number on a card is a number of a bar.** It is not a number of people, it does not correspond to any number of people, and it is not to be used as one. **A guess with a division in it that comes out near the right number is the most dangerous kind of thing there is**, because it is right enough that nobody checks it, and in nine years there is a figure in a fund's book with a decimal in it and nobody alive remembers that it came out of a tin frame in a lean-to on a bar's end.
14. **Four hundred and eleven and three hundred and eighty-eight are different figures and are never the same, and a headcount of people cannot be produced from a count of a bar by anybody, and a man of sixty-four has said so on the record before anybody asked him to.**
15. **A court that enters a question asked and answered is not a court that has made a rule, and the two are not the same instrument.** A judge who finds that a number is a number of something has found nothing and has made a rule out of a man in a lean-to. **The finding is entered on the magistrate's own motion, asked for by nobody, and the judge who entered it has entered in the same minute, under the same line, that she is the magistrate who did not ask for four months.**
16. **A caught person is a quantity, not a source.** A name handed in under pressure is evidence of nothing above him, **no house is identified by it, and about forty people in four trades now know to the penny what the other four or five are worth, and nothing in the empire can make a man write his own name, and the reason is not a legal rule.** A name is the only thing a person has that is theirs.
17. **A matrix is not a ledger, and a mark on a board is not a door, and a penny is a trade price and four pence is a price that lets a person who has nothing answer without owing anybody.** A man who counts plates goes to doors; the four or five who were not caught did it by not writing anything down; and a man of about sixty stood across a road for a quarter of an hour and did not cross it, because a girl of seventeen on a counter does not need him and he is not going to go in there.
18. **What happens to a rule about records when the person it is about cannot get to the room is nothing, and it has always been nothing, and the thing that changed in three weeks is only that a person can now say out loud what a number on a card is a number of.** Stated in a lane by a plate-cutter of about sixty, who is not a lawyer, has no office, is not owed a remedy, and is not going to hand over the year.
19. **The year in the run is the only protection anybody in this book has, and it is not a form.** A sending number is a number of the run; the run does not stop; there is one year in the middle of it at which a run changes. **The person who can say which numbers are hers is a ledger**, and a ledger is what the seven plates could not be and what the run needs most. She has about three weeks left of being not worth a tray, by accident, and she is sixty.
20. **The instrument is not a discovery. It is a bench under a window.** The sheet with two boxes was made in the last week of the fourth month in a room with a barred door and **was not sent to anybody**; on the sixth of the fifth month a woman in a lane who has never seen it wrote a number in a corner with her own mark beside it and the day on the back, without stopping to think. **Something that two people invented in a room in four months has been being done in lanes for eleven years and nobody wrote it down.**

### The Answer Of The Eighth Of The Third Month, In Full

- **One side. Five points. Received the eighth of the third month in the court sitting in room four, upon the question as first referred by the Docket of Powers, being whether a reading so performed was of any legal effect whatever.** First, the question is answered: a scale reading by a person not entered in the Docket's register at the time is of no legal effect whatever, and that extends to the two readings of the twelfth of the ninth month of the ninth year and the sixth of the fourth month of this year. Second, the record in which such a reading was entered is a record of a court and is not altered and is not disturbed; a finding of the Docket is a finding about a person; a record of a court is not altered by a finding about a person; **the entry stands, every act done in reliance upon it stands, and no person who acted upon it in good faith is to be questioned for having done so.** Third, **the bar of the applicant upon any audit in any jurisdiction in any office is not discharged by this answer and is not varied by it, and no part of the answer varies it.** Fourth, **the court names no person**, and states again in a form that it does not know and cannot compel the authority under which the sealed instruction was given, and that it knows of no person at the head of any office whose standing authority sets in type the sheet of the twenty-second of the ninth month. Fifth, **nothing in it is a finding that any person has done wrong and nothing in it is a finding that any record has been altered.**
- **The finding does not touch Marek Kest at all** and the chapter says so in a copyist's book on the morning of the eighth.
- **Three copies in a copyist's hand, in plain words, and the order written down before she acted:** first to the **Docket of Powers**, because the Docket referred the question; second to **Silas Sowle, for the household, with four words on the front**; third down the river to **Ivet Sarn, for the wall, with five words on the front** — *for the wall. read it twice. — T. R.* **The Docket went first, and the reason is written down, and the reason is that a room three corridors away with a man of sixty in it reads a thing this morning or tomorrow and there is no third thing.** The household got the first copy in the twelfth month and Tamsin Rook told Marek Kest four days afterwards at about the eighth hour of the evening, and **she wrote down that the order is now right for a different reason, because a warning and a permission are not the same object and do not go in the same bag.**
- **The two copies that go to a solicitor and to a wall are read by Marek Kest in the Received Room before they go, in front of her, and he gives one reason in a room and out loud and not on a page, and she agrees, and they go anyway, and *agreed* and the day are written on it.**

### The Limited Audit Of The Eleventh Of The Third Month

- **Granted in room four on the eleventh of the third month, at the fourth hour, in about nine minutes, and signed at the foot where the conducting person's name goes.** The whole sitting was eleven minutes.
- **The grant, in its terms.** One: the subject is **the two entries in the Docket's register for the twelfth of the ninth month of the ninth year and the sixth of the fourth month of this year, and nothing else**, and it is expressly **not** an audit of any record of any court, **of the Venn file, of the instrument of nineteen years back at the crossing at Lowcross, of the returns of the district works, of any numbered series, or of any standing instruction.** Two: **one room in the city of Auremar and not in any other place, and it may not be carried out in any district.** Three: within fourteen days, by a person named at the foot, in his own time and at his own wage. Four: **it may take a document a person produces of its own motion; it may compel no document, no office, no witness and no return; no finding made under it may be used in any proceeding in any court other than this court, nor produced in any proceeding without leave.** And the closing line: **the bar is not discharged and is not varied.**
- **The registrar read the whole of it out at the top of his voice in a room and offered a second reading, and Marek Kest said read it as many times as you like, and Tamsin Rook's finding is that he would have said yes in nine seconds and read it afterwards if he had not been reading it while it was being offered.**
- **It was conducted in the Received Room in the third week of the third month, in two afternoons.** It produced one sentence, written out and **not entered anywhere**, because the grant said a finding may be used in no proceeding in any other court, and there was no proceeding, and he was not going to be a man who made a proceeding to put a sentence into. **Oswin Marrow came in at about the fourth hour of the second afternoon, unasked, and stood at the end of the table because the room was in his building**, and said it was correct, that it was one line, and that it would fit on the back of a form.
- **It cannot be used at Lowcross and cannot be used in any district**, which is the whole of it, and **the person who had to say so out loud, in a room, in about eleven minutes, standing, was Marek Kest, and the person he said it to was the only person in the world who wanted it.**

### Oren Tal's Leaf, And A Form That Is One-Sided

- **Oren Tal's leaf does not leave a file of Nevin Trost's own and is not entered anywhere.** A copy of a schedule of the term, made about forty years ago, in a copyist's hand whose name is on it and which Oren Tal will not say in that office; **folded in four, and the fold has gone through the last line in two places and worn a channel about the width of a thumbnail.** What remains is a heading — *schedule of the term, the days named, the term held for seven years and no longer* — the copyist's ordinary rule at the foot, and the copyist's name at the foot of the last line that is left. **There were two lines after the rule. There is one.**
- **Marek Kest asked twice and was refused twice**, and the second refusal is in a form with a reason in it, and the reason is that he has no file for the matter, and that if he had one he could answer.
- **There is no form in the empire that lets a clerk of the Received Room take a document out of a file of the Examinations office**, and Marek did not ask properly and Trost saved him the reasons.
- **The finding of the chapter: the four-line custody form with two boxes, in which the second box is headed *the office of the receiving person, if any*, protects a holder and does not protect a paper.** Every protection in that building protects a holder: the Docket a register of persons, the Chancery a locked cabinet with the key on a chain, a district itself by saying it is powerless. **Every one is a wall against a person with a tray and not one of them has ever been a wall against an office.**
- **Nevin Trost copied one line out, in his own hand, with the date, and gave it to Marek Kest. The leaf stays in the drawer under the dead man's desk.** The line is not to be entered in any register, minute, list or file and is not to be sent to any person. **Marek Kest did not obtain the leaf and did not ask twice, and the line goes nowhere, and there are two of them in the empire and he is one of them.**
- **Trost's own finding, which is the sixth sentence of the batch: a form that protects a holder protects the loss, and the protection a copyist built for herself in the ninth month has put her whole method out of reach at the exact moment the method needed to be in reach, and neither of them did that on purpose.** He entered the refusal in the day's book of the Examinations office with his own hand at the foot, and it is **the only thing in that book for fourteen months that is not a refusal.**

### The Application At Lowcross, The Third Of The Fourth Month

- **Made by Silas Sowle for the household. Refused, correctly, with reasons, with a date, and with a free copy to each party and no fee in either direction.** Two things were asked for: a direction that the crossing is a work of the public and not the work of a holder, and a direction that the district stand the cost of **nineteen pounds three and fourpence.** Neither can be made by a district court. **The refusal says in terms that nothing in it is a decision that the crossing is a work of a holder, and that the court has made no such decision and could not make it.**
- **The works filed an answer, put on the table by Gideon Harrow and not read by him, because it is his office's paper and not his.** It says the office cannot stand the cost and cannot commit beyond the quarter; that **on the nineteenth of the twelfth month this office asked this district in writing to stand the cost, and the request has not been answered, and the office does not know whether the district saw it and declined it or has not decided or has not seen it, and it has not asked, because it has in nineteen years never once written to a district to say that it cannot do a thing**; that **this court is not the district and a district court may not direct a district to stand a cost**; that **the office will not certify, because certifying is a promise to inspect and a certificate given now would be a promise the office has no means of keeping, and the office has given one in one year in nineteen and is not going to give another**; and that the crossing is open and has been since the twenty-fourth of the twelfth, on the labour of nine unpaid persons of whom one is an officer of the office.
- **The magistrate entered the answer as filed and not as a concession, on her own motion, saying that in nine years the only paper of that kind in that district was the one of the first of the sixth month of last year, and that she is not going to have this one read as a man wriggling.** And she entered a second line on her own motion and asked for by nobody: that the answer states the works has never once written to a district to say that it cannot do a thing, that it has now asked, and that the request has not been answered, **and that the court notes the second of those three facts and does not propose to do anything with either of them, because a court that does something with them in the fourth month is a court that has found a way of making this district's problem into this court's work.**
- **Hester Crane refused a second instrument for the third time, and refused before the form was put in front of her, because her granddaughter read it through first and found the second person's name line empty and said so out loud.** Nobody wrote anything down, which was Silas Sowle's doing. She said her reason: *I do not want my granddaughter to hand me the next one.* She has signed one instrument in sixty-one years.
- **Jonas Cray performed his undertaking** — once a month, at the seventh hour, not in a good coat, saying the bad thing twice — and the bad thing this month is **that nothing has happened in four months.** He also brought the news that a clerk of the intake desk came out on his own time on the twenty-ninth of the third month, stood in the yard for about an hour and a half, asked what the figure on the works' return is a number of, and wrote the answer in a book he had made up himself, and that the man of sixty-four in the hut at the top of the crossing is paid a penny a day to count it and nobody has asked him either.
- **Sowle's arithmetic, at about the ninth hour, on the back of a form at the head of the Weir Cut: the bill has never once been the difficulty, and what the application needed was a name, and there is not one in that district and has not been since a parish was dissolved in the sixth year.** And the one sentence he is going to put in at the second hour: **nineteen words on the back of a returned form that nobody has ever read out in a room**, and he has had it in a coat since the second of the twelfth.

### The Crossing's Own Figure, And The Man Who Keeps It

- **Emrys Rudd, sixty-four, keeps the tally at the head of the Lowcross crossing in a lean-to about nine feet by six, at a penny a day, paid whether anybody crosses or not, for nineteen years, and is in no record of the district, the works or the court.** The figure on the works' return, **three hundred and eighty-eight crossings in four weeks, is a number of times the bar was raised and lowered, and is not a number of people**, and he has known it every day for nineteen years.
- **A magistrate of the district court of the lower district came to the lean-to herself at about the sixth hour on the third of the fourth month, with a clerk, and told him out loud that she had read the figure four times since the twelfth month and had never once asked.** She entered it in the minute on her own motion, asked for by nobody, as a question asked and answered and not as a finding, and entered under the same line that **she is the magistrate who did not ask for four months.**
- **The arithmetic of the penny: about three pounds a year, about fifty-seven pounds over nineteen years, against about thirty-eight pounds of repairs in two lots and about a pound a year lost in the years between.** And the finding, from the man in the hut: **there is no line for the bridge in the fund, and a fund that had a line for a bridge would have to answer for the bridge.**
- **The reconciliation that cannot be made:** four go over on a cart with a load on, a woman crosses on a plank with a sack three times a morning and would be three in a headcount and is one in the count of a bar, and **a headcount cannot be produced from a figure of crossings by anybody, and a guess with a division in it that comes out near the right number is the most dangerous kind of thing there is.**
- **He says in advance that a figure about him in a file in the city would be the first thing anybody has ever written about him**, and that it would make him findable, and that he has watched four people in this city try to get at a document in a drawer.

### The Notice Of A Seat, The Tenth And Fourteenth Of The Fourth Month

- **Received by the court sitting in room four on the tenth of the fourth month. One side, not printed, a device at the head and a seal and a name at the foot.** Entered by its device as a respondent; **no person of or belonging to the seat is party, cited or examinable, and no inference of any kind may be drawn from the fact that a person has not been named**; the seat does not contend either way about the crossing and has no position on the question; it asks that no lower court make a finding on the construction of an instrument sealed with the fire-and-water device and that the matters touching it be held; **it will pay the carriage of any person the district court thinks should be heard, without asking to know why, and will pay it twice if the person comes twice**; and the sixth line states that the First Seat holds the old authority to call a succession convocation, without any claim upon the matter and without any threat. **Seryn Oris, First Seat.**
- **She is not present, is not a villain, and is credited with nothing she did not do.** She is not the author of the substituted wording, of the public-necessity sentence, of the altered witness order, or of the sealed instruction. **The MC/FS circular is not hers** and the office that prints it has no person at the top of it. **The First Seat quarterly return is her seat's paperwork and is still in no reply.**
- **Marek Kest is not a party to the matter at Lowcross and has never been**, and says so, and that is what stopped him for nine minutes. He was heard for about nine minutes and used them badly and has written that down himself. Three arguments: that a seat which enters itself by a device has connected two matters no instrument joins; that the Fifth line is an offer to pay the carriage of a witness, and a payment to a witness made by a party is a thing that is asked about in rooms; and **the third, which was eight words: the document is about a seal.**
- **The objection is entered verbatim, read back twice at his request, and decided against him in about four minutes with no reason on the bench.** It is the **second** time an objection of his has been entered in a room in this matter; the first was the seventeenth of the sixth month of last year, against the escalation, entered and lost.
- **The registrar states, out loud and in the room, that the notice mentions no series, no run, no number, no sheet, no office, one instrument of nineteen years back, one district court, one crossing and one carter — and that this is why it is the most dangerous paper to have come up that stair in fourteen months, because the whole of the last four months has been about a series, and this document has no series in it and is still going to hold up a bridge.**
- **Sela Vint's reason, in a corridor, in about eleven minutes:** that the seat has found the only door in that building that it fits through, and that she published the form and has never been sorry about it once in nine years; **and that she cannot refuse a form her own court published, because a judge who refuses it has to invent a reason, and an invented reason in that minute book cannot be told in nine years from a real one.**
- **Her answer to the question he actually wanted to ask: a document with a name at the top of it cannot make a person examinable, not for him, not in this matter, not in that building.** A person who signs for a seat is discharging an office, which is the thing his own supervisor said in a press room off that stair in the second month of last year, and it worked on him and it does not work here, **because the thing he wants is a seat and a seat is not examinable and there is no form in the empire for examining a mark.** And: **it is a human clerk's instrument, made by a man who meant it kindly, and it does not cross a species, and a seat has handed him the first name in fourteen months and he cannot use it.**

### The Sheet With Two Boxes, The Second Instrument

- **Made by hand, at her own cost, in the Received Room, in the last week of the fourth month, and not sent to anybody.** One side of foolscap ruled by hand in two boxes with three lines of rule under them, and the other side ruled once. **No number, no series, no seal, and nothing at the head of it at all.**
- **Box one: *what the person was told, in the person's own words.*** **Box two: *the office of the receiving person, if any*, and `None` goes in it when there is none.** The three lines: *you are not asked anything and nothing is asked of you; you are not to be given advice; write the day and your initial on the other side and hand this back.* The other side: **the day. Your initial.**
- **The column, in a separate book at a separate counter, has one heading and it is one word: Given.** Four other headings were written out and crossed out, and the reason is in her book: *Received is what the clerk does. Given is what the person does, and the only thing this is evidence of is that a person gave something.*
- **Four pence a sheet**, costed standing up: foolscap about a halfpenny, about a minute and a half to rule by hand because a ruled box cannot be bought in the city, and **four pence is the price on the glass in a shop on the Greyfen road, and a penny is a trade price, and a trade price is a price that a person who is not in a trade cannot use because they do not know the counter exists.** The price is on no sheet and is in a book with no office on it, in her hand, with the day, and the reason is that in nine years somebody will want to know why the rule everybody is reading was priced.
- **Its limits, said out loud in a room and not on a page:** it does not stop a document, does not put a household back in a field, does not open a door, does not lift a bar, and is not evidence in any proceeding, because it is evidence of a time and not of a thing. It cannot be used at Lowcross, is adopted nowhere, and **has not been sent to the Docket of Powers, or to Examinations, or to a district court, or to a wall, or to a solicitor, or to anybody** — and the reason is the **undertaking of the fourth of the first month, which is live and is not retired and is not amended**, and is used here as a reason not to send something.
- **It is beaten by the case it was made for.** The widow in the river court had four months' warning and was in a court with forty people in it and a lawyer's husband on a wall, and being told the truth is the warning, and an afraid person goes to the hearing and sits down. **It fixes the next thing, and the next thing is the one where nobody warned anybody, and there will be a great many of those, because a warning costs a man in a good coat and a day of walking.**
- **Neither of them filled in the first one, and that is the finding.** A document with a clerk's name at the top of it is a document about that clerk: Marek Kest is in the Docket's register, in a minute book three times, named at the foot of a grant, and named at the foot of a return at a door; Tamsin Rook is in two registers and on a list of forty-one names in four hands. **Two people cannot carry the first one, because the two of them are the two people in this matter that a document is about.** He did not ask her to put his name on it and she did not put it on.
- **The instrument already exists in the city and nobody named it.** A clerk of the intake desk has kept a book for two years with one column, a time and an initial and nothing else, and **no heading at the top of it**, and nobody asked him what the figures in it were a number of. **Marek Kest did not ask him either. Somebody has to leave one thing in this book without asking.**

### The Market For Names, The First Week Of The Fifth Month

- **The examination directed by the sentence of the second of the second month was taken in the second week of the fourth month. It is forty-one pages and it names no client, no house and no person above any person.** What came out of it is a practice, eleven lines long: the mark is a way of saying *this is one of ours* to somebody who already knows what ours is; a signature says who did a thing and a mark says which of a set of people did it; **and a set of people is a thing that can be counted.**
- **About nine certifiers learned what a mark is for in one week in the second month. About forty more have learned in the last three weeks what a name is for**, and none of the forty learned it from the man who was caught and about six of them learned it because of him and none of them will put that in a book.
- **One certifier of about sixty, of a town whose name is not on the page, held a client's name under his own certificate in his trade's book since a year he will not give, and put it in a form of his own on about the first of the fifth month. It went in at the second hour and was read back and initialled at the fourth. He is not sorry, gives the two things he is sorry about in an order, and asks that the order be kept. No house is identified by it and nothing above him is proved.** **The page refuses the figure and this record refuses it with it: he has held the certificate since a year he will not give, and Garren Pyle's thirty-four years in a trade is a different man in a different room.**
- **What a caught person is worth: four pence a sheet and a clerk's half hour, and the half hour is in a column with a figure in it.** He is the end of it and not a source. **A name is worth about a plate, and the price is public, and it was set on the ninth of the second month in a registrar's note by a man who meant no harm by it — a matrix is not a ledger and a tool is a thing anybody can buy. He was talking about steel. He was talking about all of us.** There is no line in the empire that stops a trade reading a mark at each other, and one person has handed in the only thing any of them held that was a name.
- **Garren Pyle walked four doors on foot in the evening of the second of the fifth month.** One said thank you and shut the door; one said nothing; one said he would think about it, then asked a question — *if I hand it in, does anybody come to my house* — and then said it again; one was not at home and nothing was left and he did not knock twice. **The list of which is which is in a book of his own with no office on it and in nine years nobody will be able to get at it, and in nine years if somebody does, the first thing they will do with it is not find out about a plate.**
- **The chapter's finding: a name is the only thing a person has that is theirs, and nothing in this book or in this empire can make a man put his name down, and the reason is not a legal rule and there is not one.** A man who has worked out what it is worth will simply not write it, and he will be right not to, and the four or five who were not caught are the most protected people in that building and they did it by not writing anything down. **The instrument that finds people does not work on a man who will not write his own name, and there is no appeal from a word.**
- **The counting-house mark on a board on the Kiln row is a mark and is not a name, is ninety years old, and is not anybody's client, and no court in this book has ever known what it is.** Pyle stood across the road for a quarter of an hour on the fourth of the fifth month and did not cross it, because **a mark on a board is not a door, and a girl of seventeen on that counter already knows what a copy is, which is a weapon and a target.** Bryn Mawer is seventeen, has a page of a list of four pages in her own hand, and the sixth column, headed *why not*, is empty and is going to stay empty.

### The Woman Who Cuts Plates, The Fifth To The Seventh Of The Fifth Month

- **A clerk of the district court came on the fifth of the fifth month with a form of his own, and did everything right, and was told: *Nobody gets my name.* Four words, and the form went under the weight with about nine hundred other forms. **She is not named on the page and stays unnamed.**
- **The reason, which she has never said to anybody.** A number in the edge of a plate is a **sending** number — a number of the run of the Notaries' Table, out of one counter, in one hand, for about sixty years. **The run does not stop. There is one year in the middle of it at which a run changes, because a run changes when the counter changes or the hand changes, and she was in the room when it happened and the man who was doing it before her is dead.** So she is the only living point in the empire at which a number on a plate can be broken into the part that is hers and the part that is not, and a person who can do that can tell a court which certificates in a tray came out of which year, **and clients have numbers too and they go out on the same run.** **If she gives her name she is a person in a file. If she gives the year she is a ledger.**
- **The other thing she has never said, and it is eleven years old.** A woman at the far end of the lane has brought papers to be stamped about three days in ten for eleven years, cannot read, has never said she cannot read, signs a line with three fingers on it and takes the paper away; and eleven years ago a certifier brought a plate to be cut here, gave a card made out to a firm of about four words, and she cut the number into the edge because the run is the run and did not look at what the matrix was for, **because nobody has ever asked her what a matrix is for and she is a plate-cutter and a matrix is steel and a plate is steel.** She thought about it for about a year and then stopped, because there was nothing to think about it with. **About four hundred people have told the truth in a room in this matter and she is one of about four who has not.**
- **The volume's question for its final two batches, in a lane, in her own hand, with the day on it: *what happens to a rule about records when the person it is about cannot get to the room.*** And the answer: **nothing. Nothing happens. There is no rule about records that reaches the far end of this lane and there never has been, and if a paragraph does touch her then she signs a line with three fingers on it, and that has been going on in this lane since before she was born.**
- **What changed in three weeks is only that a person can now say out loud what a number on a card is a number of, and about four hundred people in a city could not have said it before the eighth of the second month of last year, and she was one of them, and she was not being careful, she was in a lane.**
- **On the sixth of the fifth month a girl of about thirteen walked two hours to the bench with a certificate in her hand and asked whether it was hers.** The plate-cutter took the edge of the card between her own thumb and forefinger the way you find a cut in a coin, moved it about an inch, stopped, and said: *that is not mine, and I will not tell you whose it is because I do not know whose it is, and I have cut about nine hundred plates off that run and not one of them carries a name.* Then the girl asked her to write the number down for her mother, in case anybody asked her, **and the number was written in pencil, large, in the top left corner of the card in a quarter-inch of unprinted space, and the day was written on the back in a corner with three short cuts in a triangle beside it, because three short cuts in a triangle is the whole of what she is and there is no office at the top of it and there is no room to put one.** And she said, out loud, to a person of thirteen: *that is a mark and not a name, and in about four years somebody is going to come to your house with a tray and ask you what a mark is.*
- **There was no box. There was no form and no series and no office and no heading.** The woman was the receiving person and had none, which is the word `None` on a form that has not been written and has not been sent to anybody, **and the day and the mark on the back of a piece of paper is the whole of the second instrument, arrived at on its own, in a lane, eleven years late, in about four minutes.**
- **The finding, in her own hand, at about the seventh hour of the seventh of the fifth month, with the day on it: about forty people have learned in three weeks what a name is for, and she is the only person alive who can break a number on a plate into the part that is hers and the part that is not, and the only thing standing between those two facts is that she is sixty, she has three cuts in a triangle, she has no office, and a man with a tray has to knock on a door to get at her and he does not knock on doors. That is the whole of her protection. It is not a form. It is that she is not worth a tray, and she has been not worth a tray for twenty-two years by accident, and she has got about three weeks of it left.** And: **she is not going to hand the year over, not to a court, not to a trade, not to a girl of thirteen who walks two hours, because if she does she is a ledger and nothing else, and then in nine years there is a file with a line in it saying that a woman in a lane can read a plate, and the number is the thing they would want next.**
- **The Reed Cut image is not spent.** The fifth term's schedule on the gate rail, fourteen months old, and a woman of eleven who cannot read it and is read to, is the volume's last page and not this batch's.

### Volume 02 Batch 0003 Calendar, The Fourth Of The Third Month To The Seventh Of The Fifth Month

**No weekday name appears anywhere in this batch and none may be used to anchor a chain.** The only weekday on the page in the whole book is the ninth of the fourth month of the ninth year being a Monday, and nothing may be derived from it. **The lengths of the ninth, tenth, eleventh, twelfth, first, second, third, fourth and fifth months are nowhere fixed on the page, so any day name would be a canon change. No real month name appears.**

- **Fourth to the tenth of the third month:** Chapter 0071. The answer is on the morning packet of the **eighth**; the second sheet, a notice of the eleventh, is behind it. The three copies go out on the eighth; the passage at about a minute and a half; the arithmetic at the ninth; the book at about the seventh hour.
- **Second week of the third month:** Chapter 0072. The sitting in room four on the **eleventh at the fourth hour**, eleven minutes. Silas Sowle comes up on the **thirteenth**; the four lines are written on the thirteenth; the answer in a room, in words, with a date, on the **fifteenth at about the eleventh hour**; the list of paper on the same day.
- **Third week of the third month:** Chapter 0073. The transcription is on the morning packet of the **eighteenth**; paragraphs eleven is put up on the **nineteenth**; the eleventh shillings is the twentieth; **Iolanthe Rees and her neighbour come at the third hour of the twenty-first**, and the answer is the same and the reason is not.
- **Last week of the third month:** Chapter 0074. The room at the **twenty-seventh, tenth hour to about the eleventh**; the line copied; the day's book entered the same day; the book at about the seventh hour.
- **First week of the fourth month:** Chapters 0075 and 0076, the same day, the **third**. **The application is on the third and is filed on the second.** The yard before the second hour; the chamber at the second hour and refused at about the fourth; the yard in the afternoon; **Jonas Cray at about the half past seventh**; Sowle's arithmetic at about the ninth. **Chapter 0076 is from the sixth hour**, the lean-to at about the sixth, the minute entered at about the ninth, the clerk instructed at about the tenth.
- **Second and third weeks of the fourth month:** Chapter 0077. The notice is received on the **tenth**; **the audit of the third week of the third month is recalled at the head of the chapter**; room four on the **fourteenth**; the corridor afterwards; the book at about the seventh hour of the **fourteenth**.
- **Fourth week of the fourth month:** Chapter 0078. The twenty-second to the twenty-sixth, at the fourth hour, at the sixth, and at about the ninth on the twenty-sixth.
- **First week of the fifth month:** Chapters 0079 and 0080. **Chapter 0079: the second, the third and the fourth.** **Chapter 0080: the fifth, the sixth and the seventh.**

### Figures Named In Volume 02 Batch 0003

Recorded so that a later batch does not invent replacements. Full entries in `bible/characters.md` under **Supporting Figures Added In Volume 02, Batch 0003**. **None of them is a villain and none of them is a mouth for a power they do not hold.** **The Emberwatch clerk who itemised entry ninety-one remains unnamed. The registrar of the Cinder Court remains unnamed and is not a plot point. S. Roke has a first initial only. The young clerk of the intake desk stays unnamed. The woman of about sixty who has cut plates for twenty-two years in a lane stays unnamed, and is the POV of Chapter 0080 without a name being given to her.**

- **Seryn Oris, First Seat:** named for the first time in this book, on the page of a notice of a seat, in the second and third weeks of the fourth month. **She is not present, is not a villain, and is credited with nothing she did not do.** The chapter describes **the seat, which holds the old authority to call a succession convocation, and not the woman.** She is the volume's final antagonist and she is not this volume's answer, and **the MC/FS circular is not hers.**
- **Emrys Rudd:** sixty-four, nineteen years at a penny a day counting a free bridge, in a lean-to, in no record, asked one question in his life.
- **One certifier of about sixty, unnamed and of a town not named:** caught in the first week of the fifth month. **Not sorry, and not going to be talked out of it.** Worth four pence and a clerk's half hour. **A caught person is a quantity and not a source.**
- **Silas Sowle, Ivet Sarn, Hester Crane, Rosamund Crane, Jonas Cray, Gideon Harrow, Joane Tranter, Garren Pyle, Bryn Mawer, Oswin Marrow, Nevin Trost, Sela Vint, Iolanthe Rees** are all already bound above and are all moved by this batch; the record of what each of them did is in the sections above and in `state/character-state.md`.

## The Volume 02 Batch 0004 Record, Chapters 81–90

`outline/batches/volume-02-batch-0004.md` is the card source for this batch and exists, and carries a closing section recording seven divergences between its cards and the page. **The batch took the volume's escalation item 4 as written and got all three of its parts onto the page, and it produced the volume's public win, which is narrow.**
