- **Second week of the eighth month:** Chapter 0091. The reading of the order in room four; the reading of the registrar's entry of the twenty-sixth of the fifth; the price of the sentence; the nine people; the marginal note about adding up the grants wrong.
- **Third week of the eighth month:** Chapter 0092. The packet on the seventeenth; the undertaking at about the fourth hour; the four given away at about the half past four on the nineteenth; the clerk of the lower district in the corridor on the twentieth at about the eleventh hour; the two halves put on the same table at about the tenth hour of the twentieth; the entry in her own book at about the ninth hour on the eighteenth.
- **Fourth week of the eighth to the first week of the ninth:** Chapter 0093. The certification received on the twenty-fourth; the chamber at about the third hour and the reading in about six minutes; the minute in the afternoon; the yard at about the half past four; the engineer's date.
- **Second week of the ninth month:** Chapter 0094. The Docket at about the fourth hour of the twelfth; the register; the line; the entry; the doorway at about the fifth hour; his own book at about the ninth hour of the twelfth.
- **Third week of the ninth month:** Chapter 0095. The question referred on the fifteenth and answered on the nineteenth; the day and a half; the reading of the answer at about the fifth hour on the nineteenth; the undertaking at about the sixth hour; the corridor at about the seventh hour; his own book at about the ninth hour on the nineteenth.
- **Fourth week of the ninth to the first week of the tenth:** Chapter 0096. The form read on the twenty-first at about the fourth hour; the four lines written on the twenty-second at about the fifth hour; the walk down the lane on the twenty-fourth at about the sixth hour; the two books; the last day of the ninth month at about the seventh hour.
- **Second week of the tenth month:** Chapter 0097. The packet in the first week; the transcription on the wall; the answer of the eighth of the third read out at the sill on the tenth; the shilling and the question at the third hour; the fourth form on the wall; the entry about the letters on the twelfth at about the seventh hour; the embankment corridor.
- **Third week of the tenth month:** Chapter 0098. The original produced on the eighteenth; the bench and the minute; the sheet of the nineteenth with the question and the date; the answer in Silas Sowle's own office on the twenty-first at about the sixth hour; his own book at about the ninth hour on the twenty-first.
- **Fourth week of the eleventh to the first week of the twelfth:** Chapter 0099. The Inheritance Desk, the seventh hour to about the sixth hour of the next day, the second of the twelfth, and the account written in the day's book.
- **Second to fourth week of the twelfth month:** Chapter 0100. Reed Cut, the gate, the rail, the bank, the seventh hour and the half past seven and the ninth, and the twentieth of the twelfth.

### Figures Named In Volume 02 Batch 0005

Recorded so that a later batch does not invent replacements. **One new figure only, and she is unnamed and is not given a reason and may not be.**

- **A girl of eleven at the Reed Cut gate, unnamed**, who cannot read the fifth term's schedule and who comes to the rail at about the seventh hour on the twentieth of the twelfth month with a pail, and who asks the four questions in order and gets a better answer than anybody has got out of them in fourteen months, and then asks a fifth one that is not one of the four and is the volume's closing question: *and who is it that reads it to the ones who cannot read it.* **She is not a device and not a symbol and she is owed nothing and she asked for one thing, and her mother is called Nery and works the channel, and the mother is not in any record.** **That mother is not Nery Caudle.** Nery Caudle is thirty-nine, is the last house on the Greyfen dyers' lane in Auremar, is married to Mike who keeps the bench, has four children and one wage, and signed the amended exit clause on the twenty-ninth of the tenth in her own yard with two pounds five and a quarter of her own wage in the levy (Chapter 18). The Reed Cut gate is in the district of Mosswake, four hundred and thirty miles from Auremar, and the woman there works a channel and is in no record at all. **The two are separate people who share one given name, and no batch may merge them, move the Caudles to the river, or give the Caudles a daughter at Reed Cut.** This is recorded because the girl says the name out loud in Chapter 0100 and the name already belongs to somebody four hundred and thirty miles away.
- **Every other person in the batch is already bound above** and none of them is new: Sela Vint, the registrar of the Cinder Court (unnamed), Tamsin Rook, Joane Tranter, Silas Sowle, Oswin Marrow, Halvard Riet, Marek Kest, Ivet Sarn, Tavi Venn, Hester Crane, Rosamund Crane, Jonas Cray, Gideon Harrow, Emrys Rudd, the clerk of the district court of Auremar's lower district (unnamed), the young clerk of the intake desk (unnamed), the copyist of the Greyfen road (unnamed), and Seryn Oris, First Seat (named, off-page). **The counsel of the First Seat does not appear. The Emberwatch clerk who itemised entry ninety-one stays unnamed. S. Roke has a first initial only. The woman of about sixty who has cut plates stays unnamed. The woman of about seventy at the far side of the Lowcross crossing stays unnamed. The one certifier who was caught stays unnamed.**

### What A Volume 03 Writer Inherits, In One Page

- **The volume's winning sentence is two sentences nine days out of order with each other and neither court has read the other**, and the half that is missing is the half that makes the other half work, and both halves are on the page. The two are not joined anywhere in Volume 02 and may be joined by a later volume, in a room, with a witness, and not on a page.
- **A finding that binds nobody who has not been heard is a sentence with no supply of hearings in it.** Who supplies the hearing, who pays the carriage, and what a court of limited jurisdiction can build out of a sentence that is better than it looks, is the open question of the whole series and is not answered.
- **A certification that is local is worth one place**, and about four hundred and forty people in four offices and one trade are untouched by it, and the sheet of the twenty-second of the ninth is in nine hundred and forty copies on an ordinary distribution and is expressly not touched.
- **Marek Kest is eligible for Continuity Auditor training and under formal watch**, has read nothing, has performed no reading in fifty chapters of Volume 02, and may not be in a room without a person in it who is not from an office that is not named. **Tamsin Rook holds an office that does not exist, with a limit on it, and can be asked to stop by anybody.**
- **The thirty-one at Mosswake are thirty-one and the fourth column reads *next review: none named* thirty-one times**, and a high court has made the harm permanent instead of beginning to repair it, and **no sentence in this empire is addressed to a court about a letter it wrote**, and that is the hole Volume 03 inherits as a live question.
- **The question the volume closes on is asked out loud on a bank and not answered:** whether a water office that cannot always do a public work can hand the work to anybody in between, and if there is anybody in between, then what is the child at the end of it. **And, from `outline/series.md` and not yet on the page: what Ilyra Kest found in the Ashfall archive that was worth dying for, and why the original document names a blank heir.** Neither is answered and neither may be answered in the first chapters of the next volume.

### The Fix Pass Over Chapters 91–100, And What It Corrected

**A fix pass ran over the ten chapters after the word-count record above was first written, and two defects were found on the page and corrected. This section is the authoritative list.** It is still the writer and not an independent reader, and that limitation is not fixed here and cannot be fixed from inside a writing phase; see the infrastructure finding in `state/current.md`.

1. **A real month name had got into Chapter 0099.** The Inheritance Desk's first uncheckable item read *A figure of about forty was given in a room in June*, and **Volume 02 does not use calendar month names anywhere in its ninety-nine earlier chapters**: it says *the sixth of the third month*, *the last three weeks of the fourth month*, *the twentieth of the ninth*, and a check of every file in `chapters/volume-02/` for the twelve month names returns nothing. **Volume 01 does use them**, in about nine chapters, out of the early practice of a district that thought in them; that is a fact about Volume 01 and is not a licence, and **a Volume 03 chapter that slips one in is a slip and not a callback.** **The line now reads *given in a room in the fourth month***, which is where the figure of about forty comes from — a magistrate's own words in a room in Chapter 0086, *I have read four hundred minutes this month and about forty of them are about a person learning what their name is worth*, in the last three weeks of the fourth month. **A later batch may not reintroduce a calendar month name, and may not give the figure of about forty a date other than the fourth month.**
2. **Chapter 0099 called the schedule's contents five names, and the schedule is a list of jobs.** Halvard Riet's eighth and last item read *the rail, and the sheet on it, and the five names a girl of eleven cannot read*, and **the fifth term's schedule is a list of five works with a number of days against each — a ditch, a bank, an abutment and kerb, a gate reset and the linkage, and the rail's own new pins — and it carries the water office's device, a memorandum number, a date, and one person's name at the foot, which is a foreman's.** It was never five names. **The line now reads *the five jobs on it a girl of eleven cannot read***, and a later batch may not call the contents of that sheet anything but jobs.
3. **The word-count record was measured before the last three prose edits and was therefore wrong by a small amount, and the figures are now post-fix and reproducible.** Chapters 91, 98 and 99 each grew by a few words after the first measurement, and the corrected table is the one above: **36,958 for the batch, 386,123 for the manuscript.** **No chapter was trimmed to hit a figure and nothing was padded.**
4. **Checked and found clean, with nothing to correct:** no weekday name in any of the ten; no calendar month name; **no use of the word *exception* or *precedent* of the certification**; no *Roll Assembly*; no joining of the MC/FS circular and the First Seat quarterly return; no third refusal of either sealed page; no name for the fourth-form authority; no finding of an altered record or of a person at fault; no liability created at Lowcross; **31 + 11 + 12 = 54 and 54 + 6 = 60, with eighteen returns not coming back**; the fifth heading of the register of pending duties still *Copy sold* and not *A shilling a copy*; the Venn ruling still in five terms with the fourth the new-signature rule and the fifth the district works; the bill still **nineteen pounds three and fourpence** and unpaid; **four hundred and eleven** on the far side and **three hundred and eighty-eight** crossings; the count of the sitting still nine people with the registrar's own name fourth; **the date the schedule was nailed up still printed nowhere**, so that the fourteen-month age stands as a figure without a date to check it against.

# VOLUME 03

**Volume 03, *Water Debt*, Chapters 101–150, is OPEN at Chapter 110. `outline/volume-03.md` is the volume outline of record and `outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0001.md` is the card source for Chapters 101–110; both were written by the run that wrote the chapters, before any prose, and the cards file carries a closing section of eight recorded divergences between cards and page.** The Volume 01 and Volume 02 canon above this heading is undisturbed by any of it.

## The Rules Established On The Page In Volume 03 Batch 0001, And The Locks Held

1. **A duty needs a holder that can stop.** An office can be instructed and can be pushed to act and can be told not to; a person can stop, can be asked, can refuse and can die; **a body can do neither.** A duty put on a body is put on every person in it and every child born into it. This is the volume's second instrument and it was found on the page in the first ten chapters, stated by a dragon water keeper in a room with four people in it and then said again by a foreman in a shed and then again by a magistrate in a chamber.
2. **A body in a box is not the substituted phrase and is not entered as an instance of it.** The box is headed **who is to answer** on a district flood form of about a hundred and forty years. The substituted wording remains *the present holder* and stands in its four files of its four ages and **nobody wrote it.** **No chapter in this batch adds a fifth instance, writes the four in a row in date order, or converts the new box into a seam.**
3. **A scale form with a box in it that wants a person will be given a person, and giving it one is how a public authority gives its authority away on purpose, and there is no form in this empire for getting it back.** The Reed Cut district water office's flood authority is given, on the ninth of the first month, to **H. Wray** and **P. Wray** and to no body, in four lines on the face of the sheet, and the office enters in the same four lines that it **cannot compel the works, does not claim to, and cannot be compelled to do them itself.**
4. **A body may not be made a party.** Entered on a magistrate's own motion on the ninth of the second month: a body of persons is not a person, that court cannot make it one, no other court in that district can make it one, and there is no form in the district or the empire that does it. The application form for hearing a body of persons has a line at the top for **the head of the body**, and the Slade Cut Company has never elected one and would not know what to do with one, and the line is blank.
5. **A hearing that brings nine persons out of about ninety-one households binds nobody who was not in the room, and about forty were not in the room** — and a body cannot be summoned, has no clerk, no door and no list, and there is no board anywhere in that district with a name on it about a bank.
6. **A scale instrument is certified by a notary's table and nowhere else**, and that is the only thing that makes one worth anything against a dragon party. A copy made by a clerk of a district proves that a clerk of a district copied something. The Slade Cut Company has **a copy of a copy** made by a district clerk, and no certified copy. The certified copies are in a notary's table in **Auremar**, in a run, and **a run is a number of a book and the book is a hundred and forty years of a table.**
7. **The only door is a door a person walks through.** A person affected by an instrument may ask a notary's table a question about it. Not a body, not a household, not a letter, not a claim. **A table that took a body's question would not be a notary's table, it would be a funnel.**
8. **A document about nobody cannot be asked anything by anybody either.** This is the second half of the volume's inherited instrument and it is on the page for the first time, and **it is the same fact as the first half and not two facts:** the sentence that keeps ninety-one households from being joined to a duty is the sentence that keeps them from ever finding out what they owe, and both of those are what a shield is when it is not a road. **A shield is not a road, and the road to a document about nobody is a person being found, and finding a person is a different act and starts a case.**
9. **A burden moved onto ninety-one people is a burden distributed and not shared, and distributing it makes it permanent**, because a body that owes cannot stop, and a person who owes can be asked and can say no, and nobody who is owed twice by a company is in a position to say so.
10. **A training is a reading of a file, and it is not a certification, and no certification is granted or asked for.** It was held on the **twenty-second of the first month at about the second hour of the afternoon** in the Docket of Powers' third room, at the cost of the master's own afternoon and **with no fund in this office or any other**, with Tamsin Rook in the room because the watch requires it. **Marek Kest read no instrument of any kind in this batch and took no reading and carries no mark.** His finding: **a file is a box of what other people did**; nine items and about eleven lines with his name on them contained not one decision of his; and **putting a name on a box does not make the box about the name, it makes the name findable.** Marrow's half: **a file is the only instrument that can put a person in a room who did not want to be in one, and it does it with other people's hands, and it only has to be produced.**
11. **The method, which is a method and not a power: write down what you are reading, before you copy anything, and say who is not in the room.** It came from a woman called Ansel who kept a roll in a temple in a town Ivet Sarn has not been to since she was twenty-six, who made her do it every day for four years and never once explained it. Sarn has done it for nine years in a book with no office on it. **A method travels faster than its meaning and Volume 02 already said what happens to it.**
12. **A person who turns up is the answer to *and who is it that reads it to the ones who cannot read it*, and the whole of the cost of that answer is that the person who turns up is never paid and cannot be.** Tavi Venn gave the answer on the page, in a kitchen, in front of about eleven people, and her condition is that she will read it to anybody who comes and will not tell anybody what it means, and that if somebody wants a name in a box she will say no and will not care whose name it is.
13. **A resolution nobody can enforce is a promise, and a promise with a date on it is a record, and a record is the only thing a body with no office behind it has ever had.** The meeting of the twentieth of the first month ended with **no resolution** and the entry says so with the day on it.
14. **A gift is money with no figure on it and it is the worst kind.** Marek Kest paid **three pounds eleven and threepence** out of his own wage, **being three weeks of it at seventeen shillings and a penny the week**, and would not let it be called a gift or a fund, and asked that if anybody ever says a fund paid for the spring bank at the Slade that it is a lie with a clerk's name on it. A weigh-house ledger calls it **held**, after four minutes of argument among four people, and not *given*, not *paid*, not *advanced*. **The figure is three pounds eleven and threepence and the day rate is forty-five pence on the five and one, and those two are a lock: five men at eight pence and a boy at five is forty-five pence a day, six hundred and thirty pence is the fortnight the list was written for, and eight hundred and fifty-five pence is the nineteen days the list actually stood at, of which two hundred and twenty-five pence is the five days of the north ditch. The crew's own invoice is the one sum in this matter that has to add up, and it does.**
15. **A body of households in a clause of nineteen years back is a fact about a file in a magistrate's press and is not a seam and is not an instance of anything.** The instrument is of about nineteen years back, in scale form, with a seat's device at the head and no person's name, and its eleventh, fourteenth and nineteenth clauses give the work to *the body of the households from time to time having the use of it*, the providing for it to *the same*, and the cost to *the same, and by none other.* It has been applied in about eleven matters in nineteen years and never once to a body. **A balance has stood in about a hundred and forty files for about nineteen years and has never been added up and is owed by nobody, and the figure is kept out of the minute on purpose, and the minute says so**, because a clerk who thinks somebody owes something is the beginning of everything that has gone wrong in that district since the sixth year. **The minute expressly enters that this instrument is not an instance of anything else before that court and is not joined to the substituted wording, because a line between two things is a chain and a chain is an invention.**
16. **A list of works with one person's name at the foot of it is the only paper on that cut that anybody can point at, and there have been four such documents in ninety-one years.** Halla Wray's is the first in sixty years on that bank.
17. **The undertaking of the fourth of the first month is live, is not retired and is not amended.** It has now caught a **word three times**, a **fact once**, and, for the first time, an **absence** — that Marek Kest had a sheet in his coat for nine days that went to a household and did not bring it to Tamsin Rook, and the reason he did not is that he knew she would say no. **The rule the two of them made with each other in the twelfth month of last year is live, has been used three times, and is not used a fourth time in this batch.**
18. **No romance begins.** Marek Kest and Tamsin Rook are colleagues, and she is in a room in a district four hundred and thirty miles away **because a court order requires it and not for any other reason**, and both of them know that and neither of them says it, and in Chapter 0106 she states her own condition in a kitchen and it is not about him.
19. **The words *exception* and *precedent* do not appear of the Venn certification anywhere in Chapters 101–110, and the word *exception* does not appear at all.** The word *precedent* appears twice in Chapter 0108 and once in Chapter 0109, all three times of the general doctrine and of a district court's own finding of the ninth of the eighth, and never of the certification.
20. **The three halves of the winning sentence are not joined in this volume.** Leda Rell read the district court's finding of the ninth of the eighth out of a file in the back room on the ninth of the second month, and said out loud that she has no power to act on it, that neither court takes a recognition from the other, that the half which says *when* is nine days younger than the half which does not, and that neither court has read the other, and that **there is no form in this empire for one court to ask another court what it has found**, and that she has tried to write one four times in nine years and never sent it. A later volume may join them **only in a room with a witness in it and not on a page.**

## Volume 03 Batch 0001 Calendar, The First Week Of The First Month To The First Week Of The Third

- **First week of the first month:** Chapter 0101. The bank; the shed; the seventh of the first month; the list copied out again with a name at the foot of it; the weigh-house at about the sixth hour; the line about the money and any person may come and read it.
- **Second week of the first month:** Chapter 0102. The water office; the form and its box; the nine minutes in the back room on the ninth; the sheet issued on the ninth with two names and four lines; the copy into the day's book; the sheet up the river on the tenth.
- **Thirteenth to the fifteenth of the first month:** Chapter 0103. The store; the sheet arriving with eleven bushels on the thirteenth; the ninety-one-year page; the afternoon that checks four names; the first leaf written on the fifteenth; the line ruled under six years; Doreth Ferrow in the store at about the fourth hour on the fifteenth.
- **Twentieth of the first month:** Chapter 0104. The mill yard at about the seventh hour, about forty people, the miller in his door; **no resolution**; the entry in the book that night; the four shillings a year.
- **Nineteenth to the twenty-third of the first month:** Chapter 0105. The Docket; the third room; **the training on the twenty-second at about the second hour of the afternoon**, the reading-out in about nine minutes, the hour after it, the entry in the day's book, the door at about the fourth hour, his own book at about the ninth hour of the twenty-second.
- **Last day of the first month:** Chapter 0106. The kitchen at the Slade end; the letter of the twenty-fourth; the walk up the river on the twenty-seventh; the sheet read out twice; the four questions; the fifth answer; the condition.
- **Fourth and fifth of the second month:** Chapter 0107. The road up on the fourth at about the eleventh hour; the window; the shilling; the foolscap page; the nine minutes; the name given and refused; her own book at about the ninth hour on the fourth.
- **Sixth to the ninth of the second month:** Chapter 0108. The application on the sixth; the sitting on the ninth at about the tenth hour; the two hours and a quarter; the four lines; the transcript of the ninth of the eighth; the sentence; the second own-motion entry and the north-bank instrument; the cost with figures; the court rising at about the fourth hour and a quarter.
- **Fourteenth to the sixteenth of the second month:** Chapter 0109. The weigh-house on the fourteenth; the four minutes; the sheet asked for on the sixteenth at about the fourth hour; the nine minutes; the fourth question; the payment; the word in the ledger at about the fourth hour and a half; his own book at about the ninth hour on the sixteenth.
- **Twenty-first of the second month to the second of the third month:** Chapter 0110. Nineteen days of work ending on the twenty-first; the entry in the book on the twenty-second at about the seventh hour; the eleven names and the fourth day of the third month; the store on the second of the third month; the page read out twice and the sixth name read both ways; the name given to the book.

## Figures Named In Volume 03 Batch 0001

Recorded so that Batch 0002 does not invent replacements. **Four new named figures, and six new working people who are not named and stay unnamed.**

- **Halla Wray**, fifty-one, foreman of the Slade Cut, of the Slade end. **Piotr Wray**, twenty-nine, her brother, at the far end of the cut. **Rennick Adley**, thirty-eight, book-keeper of the Slade Cut Company. **Doreth Ferrow**, sixty-four, who has kept the Company's meeting for nineteen years. Full entries in `bible/characters.md` under **Supporting Figures Added In Volume 03, Batch 0001**.
- **Not named and not to be named without a card:** the **weigh-master at the Slade end**, about fifty-eight, who has weighed **about nine hundred loads of wet earth in nine years** and who is the keeper of the only line in that ledger that is not a load of earth; **her mother held the office before her and before her mother it was a man called Sill Brack, who is on about nine hundred lines of the Company's own book in the store and whose widow is a woman of about seventy-four in the second house past the mill, and he is not the Searle on the Company's page who is dead nine years with a daughter in the fourth house past the mill, and no chapter may merge the two**; the **clerk of the Reed Cut district water office**, about twenty-three, who said *then the box is empty, miss*; **Aven Tace**, the miller of the mill yard, about fifty, who is named once and is one of the thirty-one households behind the bank; **Raff Ordway**, sixteen, the boy on the list of works at five pence a day, who is **not in the box** and who is not in this matter as anything else. **The girl of eleven at the Reed Cut gate stays unnamed and unused in this batch. The Emberwatch clerk who itemised entry ninety-one stays unnamed. The registrar of the Cinder Court stays unnamed. S. Roke has a first initial only. The young clerk of the intake desk stays unnamed. The woman of about sixty who has cut plates stays unnamed.**
- **Sivra Oris** is bound above and is not new and is the POV of Chapter 0102. **In this batch she is the keeper of the Reed Cut district water office and not the First Seat and not that seat's agent and not a mouth for it, and she says both of the first two herself, unasked, in the first minute of a room. She is not a convert, she is not a rescuer and she is not a door to anything larger. Her sworn statement of the third of the fourth month is refused by her, in a yard, before anybody asks.**
- **Ivet Sarn is the clerk of the Mosswake Roll Court and Leda Rell is its magistrate and neither is new.** Tavi Venn, Mara Venn, Wendt Sayer, Odile Fenn, Joane Tranter, Silas Sowle, Halvard Riet, Oswin Marrow and Tamsin Rook are all already on the page and none of them is new.

## What The Volume 03 Batch 0001 Figures Are, And Are Not, In One Paragraph

**The Slade Cut Company is a cooperative of about ninety-one households with about sixty children under sixteen, kept in a book and not in any office.** It has worked a public cut for ninety-one years, about sixty of them with no paper naming anybody. It has **no money except four shillings a year**, which is the rent of a hired grain store at a shilling a quarter, and which the weigh-master has said out loud four times in nine years to four different people is a mad thing to spend the money on and is right. **It is not a Roll Assembly and is never described as one, and it is not an instrument, a court, an office, a creditor or a debtor.** It **cannot be compelled, cannot be instructed, cannot say no, cannot be summoned, has no clerk, no door, no head and no list**, and it has refused on the page to sign as a Company, to put a household in a box, and to choose a holder among itself.

## What A Volume 03 Batch 0002 Writer Inherits, In One Page

- **The bank holds until the water is in the fourth month, and the water is in the fourth month.** A crew of five men and a boy did nineteen days of work and were paid **three pounds eleven and threepence** out of a clerk's own wage, **being three weeks of it at seventeen shillings and a penny the week**, and **there are no unpaid days in this matter**; and the term the foreman holds it for **expires in the third week of the third month**, and the fund is empty, and about one hundred and forty feet of ditch on the south side and the scour at the third-of-the-way place are on nobody's list.
- **The certified copy of the ninety-one-year Slade Cut undertaking is in a notary's table in Auremar by a number that is a number of a book.** The only route to it runs through a person the instrument touches. The only way to be touched is to be harmed. **Nobody on the Slade has been harmed yet**, and the eleventh of the eleven names, **Tarn Skell, of the Slade**, is in the ninety-one-year page, in a map of nineteen years back as the head of a household of nine, and in four houses at the north of the ford — **and the book-keeper has found him and has not gone.**
- **The district has no authority over that bank** except two people's names and a dragon who has given her own authority away in writing, and one of the two names belongs to a man who refused to be in the box.
- **Marek Kest is four hundred and thirty miles away, eligible and untrained and watched, with no certification, no office, no fund and no standing to write the one instrument that would fix this district.** He has not written it and has not started a case. He has a method and **three pounds eleven and threepence of his own wage gone, which is three weeks of it at seventeen shillings and a penny the week and which he has said out loud in a weigh-house and entered in his own book**, and an eighth item he has not yet written down.
- **Leda Rell has entered that she does not know whether she was right** and has undertaken to hear every application a body brings through a wrong form. **Ivet Sarn has put the second half of her own sentence on her wall.** **Tamsin Rook is in a district because a court order says so and has given a name and a lane in a room on purpose and had it refused.** **The undertaking is live and has caught an absence.**
- **Untouched and unjoined and unpaid:** the two sealed pages with no third refusal; the Ashfall bundle unopened on the second shelf; the three red things that may never be joined in a sentence; the MC/FS circular and the First Seat quarterly return, which are **not named in this batch at all** and remain two documents, with Hallis Dren's return of the eleventh of the tenth still dormant, armed and known to nobody in the story; the counting-house mark, the year in the run, 44,819, the ninety-second leaf; Lowcross, open, unpaid, nineteen pounds three and fourpence; the thirty-one, the eighteen, the six and the fourth column.
- **The question the volume is for, asked out loud in a mill yard and not answered:** whether a company can be made to hold a duty that is public without making every child in it a debtor. **And, not on the page, not referred to, and not to be answered in Volume 03: what Ilyra Kest found in the Ashfall archive that was worth dying for, and why the original document names a blank heir.** Volume 03's door to it is a run number in a table and a category of person who may be made a party, and that is a route and not an answer.

## The Corrections Made On The Page Before This Batch Closed, And Checked Clean

1. **A calendar month name was in Chapter 0101** (*the bank at the Slade is eleven feet high in February*) and **is gone**; the line now reads *in the second month*. **A check of all ten files for the twelve month names returns nothing.**
2. **Two weekday names were in the batch** — *the following Friday* in Chapter 0104 and *the twenty-fourth of the first month is a Thursday* in Chapter 0108, the second of which also had the application made *on a Thursday in a mill yard*. **All three are gone** and the passage now reads *made by nine people in a mill yard on a wet evening in the fourth week of the first month*. **A check of all ten files for the seven day names returns nothing**, which is required, because the days of the week are not named anywhere in this matter and a room four days a week has days with no name and no form and no notice.
3. **A calendar month name was in Chapter 0110** (*when the water office wrote two of our names into a box in January*) and **is gone**; the line now reads *in the first month*.
4. **The public-necessity sentence and the phrase *the present holder* were in the opening paragraph of Chapter 0108**, in a description of what Leda Rell did in the fifth month of a year, and **both have been withdrawn from the page entirely.** The paragraph now reads that she signed a rule in the sixth year with about sixty households' letters in it, and joined a girl of fifteen as a guarantor in the fifth month of the ninth year off a summary of a file she had not opened. **The sentence is not quoted anywhere in this batch and the phrase does not appear in it at all**, which keeps the lock that the sentence was stated on the twelfth of the ninth and printed on the twenty-second and may not be put in an older file.
5. **Chapter 0108 was corrected twice more for accuracy of attribution.** The finding of the ninth of the eighth month **is not Leda Rell's**; it is the finding of the district court of Auremar's lower district, four hundred and thirty miles away, and she is a district court and so is that one and neither takes a recognition from the other. The page now says so, and has her read the transcript out of a file she has had for six months and never read out, and give the reason that reading it out would oblige her to say what she thinks about it. And the sentence *I have known the shape of it since the ninth of the eighth month* was inconsistent with a transcript six months in a file, and now reads *since I was a clerk in this court fourteen years ago*.
6. **Checked and found clean, with nothing to correct:** no name supplied for the authority of the sealed instruction; no finding that any record has been altered and no finding that any person has done wrong; no liability created at Lowcross; **no magic, no reading, no mark, no Roll-Answer, no certification, and no valid instrument written anywhere in the batch**; the two documents neither named nor joined; the three red things not joined; the second sealed page not opened and no third refusal; the counting-house mark still a mark; the year in the run not entered; 44,819 not read; the ninety-second leaf not looked for; Corvin Dray not re-litigated; **31 + 11 + 12 = 54 and 54 + 6 = 60 with eighteen returns that did not come back** unprinted and undisturbed; the fifth heading of the register of pending duties still *Copy sold*; the Venn ruling still in five terms with the fourth the new signature and the fifth a list of works; **the date the fifth term's schedule was nailed to the rail still printed nowhere and its age not restated, so the recorded ambiguity about the fourteen months is inherited unchanged**; the girl of eleven at Reed Cut unused, unnamed and given no reason; **and Nery Caudle and the mother of the girl of eleven at Reed Cut not merged, not moved, and neither given a daughter at the other place.**

## The Repair Pass Applied To Volume 03 Batch 0001 After Its Review, And Checked Clean

**`logs/batch-0001.review.log` is the transcript of the review phase and `logs/batch-0001.fix.log` is this pass. The review was run by the writer agent itself, because `.opencode/agent/novel-reviewer.md` declares `mode: subagent` and `scripts/novel_runner.sh` passes `--agent novel-reviewer`, which opencode refuses and falls back to the default agent; that is a controller-owned file and is not fixed here. Everything below is therefore a self-review and is recorded as one.** The pass changed no plot, invented no new fact outside the batch, restarted nothing, rewrote no scene, and deleted no chapter. Fourteen findings were returned; **all fourteen are answered here, and the two that are planning or process findings are answered in `state/current.md` rather than pretended away.**

### The six blocking arithmetic and continuity defects, fixed on the page

1. **The crew's invoice did not add up, and the batch's own rule says it must.** Five men at eight pence and a boy at five is **forty-five pence a day on the five and one**, not *five and sixpence a day*; the boy's seventy pence is five shillings and ten pence and not eleven shillings and eight pence; and the total is **eight hundred and fifty-five pence for nineteen days, three pounds eleven and threepence**, not one pound five and sixpence, which was exactly one fifth of the true fortnight figure. **The corrected arithmetic is now the scene's best line rather than a patch:** in Chapter 0109 the weigh-master gives the day rate and both totals and says *I have added that up twice*, and Marek Kest works the sum out himself in front of her and enters in his own book that he has not added up a bill in fourteen months and did it in a weigh-house. **His wage is now a figure on the page — seventeen shillings and a penny the week, so three pounds is three weeks of it — and he says that figure out loud too, on the grounds that a man who has spent fourteen months putting numbers on the outside of other people's bills has never put one on the outside of his own.** The old *nine weeks of my wages* is gone, because it made a clerk's week one shilling and twopence.
2. **Nineteen days had been welded to a fortnight's price, and the five extra days were unpaid.** The list is written for a fortnight; the north ditch came off the ninety-one-year page and went on it; the crew worked nineteen days. **The crew is now paid for nineteen days and there are no unpaid days anywhere in this matter.** The corrected figures were carried into `bible/characters.md`, `bible/terminology.md`, `outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0001.md` and `workspace/volume-03/batch-0002/PROMPT.md`, all four of which had copied the wrong sum.
3. **The store rent contradicted itself and the canon.** *It is a shilling a quarter. It is a shilling a year.* — four lines before *four shillings a year* in the same paragraph. **It now reads *It is a shilling a quarter and it is four shillings a year***, and the shilling comes out of the book **four times a year** and not *about nine occasions*. The figure of **four shillings a year** is unchanged and is the Company's whole money in sixty years.
4. **Nine acres became a hundred and forty.** Chapter 0102 put *about a hundred and forty acres of channel-side ground* behind the bank against **nine acres and thirty-one households** in three other places. **It now reads *the nine acres and the thirty-one households behind them*.** The hundred and forty in this matter is a number of files, of years, of a box four inches deep and of feet of ditch on the south side, **and it is never a number of acres.**
5. **The word-count record's own total was wrong, in the one file that says the reproducible count governs.** It claimed a manuscript total of 421,767 where the parts summed to 422,175 and the measured count was 422,208. **Every total in this file, in `state/current.md` and in `state/batch-summary.md` has been re-measured from the files, and the measured count is 422,409 after this pass.** Volume 02's own 184,006 is thirty-three under its measured 184,039 and that drift is now disclosed here rather than left in the Volume 02 record as an aside.
6. **The fund was empty and holding money.** The weigh-master had the works-fund head holding *about one pound five and sixpence in a good year and about four shillings in a bad one, and the four shillings goes on a store*, which put the fund's money and the Company's rent in one purse and made the fund's figure an accidental twin of Marek's payment. **The head is now separate from the rent**: it has held as much as three pounds across a good quarter and never more than a fortnight of the crew at one time, **and the four shillings a year does not come out of it and never did.**

### The six in-chapter contradictions, fixed on the page

7. **The silent man spoke.** Chapter 0108 had Marek Kest say a sentence *from the second bench, in about four seconds*, and had the magistrate say afterwards that *a man stood at the back of this room and did not say one word in two hours and a quarter*, and had placed him at the back and not on a bench. **The line about the wrong form is Doreth Ferrow's, which is where it belongs — she is the chair of a body of persons answering a clerk about the head of it — and Marek Kest now says nothing at all in that room, which is the load-bearing fact of the chapter.** He is entered as having said nothing, and the paragraph says why the magistrate read the transcript out.
8. **A malformed date.** *the second of the twenty-first of the fifth month of last year* is now **the twenty-first of the fifth month of last year**.
9. **A sentence that defeated itself.** *the oldest person who could have been asked is eighty-one and she was four* — at eighty-one she was not born ninety-one years ago, and as written the clause undercut the *not one person alive remembers* it was there to support. **It now reads *the oldest person on that bank is eighty-one and she was not born*,** which supports it.
10. **Three accounts of one visit.** Chapter 0104 had the man from the city coming up the road *on the second day of this month* and asking Adley in a yard, which was a third occasion and put him in the district before the sheet existed; Chapter 0105's own book entry had him at a window in the second week of the first month with *four days* to decide on the twenty-second, which puts the question on the eighteenth and not in the second week; and Chapter 0107 has him at the Mosswake window on the fourth of the second month. **There is now one question and one journey.** It came up a road to him by letter in the third week of the first month, Rennick Adley's request for a list of the households is a letter and a reply and not a second visit, and the entry in his own book reads *A question came up a road to me in the third week of the first month and I have had four days to decide*, which is the eighteenth to the twenty-second. **Chapter 0107 is his arrival, on the fourth of the second month, and it is twelve days before the weigh-house, so *I have been in this district for a fortnight* is now *for twelve days*.** Chapter 0110's reference to the kitchen reading as *the twenty-eighth of the last month* is also corrected to **the last day of the first month**, which is the day Chapter 0106 and Chapter 0102 both give.
11. **Searle could not be who Chapter 0109 said he was.** Chapter 0103's Searle is dead nine years with a daughter in the fourth house past the mill; Chapter 0109's predecessor in the weigh-house was *a man called Searle, who is on about nine hundred lines of a page from ninety-one years back and whose widow is a woman of about eighty-one* — a man on the ninety-one-year page has no eighty-one-year-old widow, and that page carries exactly eleven names and he is not one of them, and the error had already been copied into `bible/characters.md`. **He is now a man called Sill Brack, on about nine hundred lines of the Company's own book in the store, whose widow is a woman of about seventy-four in the second house past the mill**, and both the chapter and the bible entry say he is not the other Searle.
12. **Nine thousand loads against nine hundred.** Chapter 0101 had the weigh-master at *about nine thousand loads of wet earth* against *about nine hundred* in Chapter 0109, the bible and the handoff. **It is nine hundred**, which is also the number of lines in her ledger and the number of names in the Company's book.

### The prose finding

13. **The hedge had become a rhythm.** *About nine* appeared **eighty-seven times** and *about four* **sixty-seven times** in 36,052 words — one numeric hedge every hundred and forty-seven words — with seventeen instances of *about nine* in one chapter, and a shingle scan showed *a city four hundred and thirty miles away* recurring five times. **The two tics are now forty-one and twenty-two, a cut of about sixty per cent, and the figures that were being hedged in rooms where the narrator has just counted the room are now stated.** Nothing was cut to a different voice: the hedge is still this book's instrument, and it survives where the count is genuinely an estimate a narrator cannot know — *about nine hundred bushels*, *about nine hundred loads*, *about nine hundred files*, *about nine feet*, *about nine years*, *about four hundred and forty feet*, *about sixty children*, *about ninety-one households*. **The work was done by changing the sentences where the hedge was carrying no information, not by deleting the hedge.**

### The two process and planning findings, which are not prose and are recorded rather than hidden

14. **The volume audit phase was skipped and the volume outline was self-authored.** `PHASE_SYSTEM.md` requires the volume audit to pass before the next volume's outline is created; the run that wrote Chapters 101–110 wrote `outline/volume-03.md` itself, before any prose, in the way Batch 0001 of Volume 02 wrote `outline/volume-02.md` for its own volume. **`state/current.md` already disclosed this and it stands disclosed, and this is the second consecutive volume to skip it, because `workspace/volume-02/` has no `volume-close/` and `workspace/volume-01/` has one.** The audit is a controller-dispatched phase and no writing phase may create it, so it is not created here. **The consequence that could be fixed has been fixed: the four beats of `outline/series.md` that the self-authored outline dropped are now written into `outline/volume-03.md` as bound obligations, moving no chapter and changing no movement** — Stage 2 Burden-Bearer as the volume's climax, Tamsin Rook witnessing its first cost and refusing to romanticise it, Magistrate Rell's own flood records proving her selective enforcement indefensible, and the Kingsway Road Guild and the Sorn border households as named factions. **The same section records the one line of the series outline that is false — *Marek has conditional certification*, superseded by Volume 02's page — and says so.**


## The Volume 03 Batch 0002 Word-Count Record

Measured with `sed 's/[[:space:]]*$//' file | wc -w` on the committed files, after the six in-chapter corrections listed at the foot of this section **and after the repair pass recorded below it, which moved four dates and one age and added words to two chapters.**

| Chapter | Title | POV | Words |
|---|---|---|---|
| 111 | What a Bay Costs | Rennick Adley | 4,201 |
| 112 | A Column With No Heading | Wendt Sayer | 3,297 |
| 113 | Not One Day Past It | Piotr Wray | 3,480 |
| 114 | In What Way the Instrument Touches You | Tamsin Rook | 3,974 |
| 115 | What It Is For | Marek Kest | 4,225 |
| 116 | A Rule That Certifies Itself | Doreth Ferrow | 3,353 |
| 117 | Nine Stones | Tavi Venn | 3,620 |
| 118 | A House for Sixty Years | Bel Skell | 3,218 |
| 119 | What a Party Is | Halla Wray | 3,429 |
| 120 | The Line at the Top of It | Rennick Adley | 4,195 |

**Batch total 36,992. Average 3,699. Range 3,218 to 4,225.** **Seven of the ten are inside 3,200–4,000** — 112, 113, 114, 116, 117, 118 and 119 — **and three are over 4,000, and all three were kept:** **Chapter 115 at 4,225 carries a referral at the cost of a day and a half of a man of sixty's own afternoon with no fund, a register explained in about eleven minutes, four lines of a price stated standing, and a man of sixty asking a question in the ordinary way and getting the wrong answer in**, and is **25 words over the about-4,200 ceiling**; **Chapter 120 at 4,195 carries a form filed in the wrong form at a clerk's window with a blank line at the top of it** and is **5 words under that ceiling**; and **Chapter 111 at 4,201 carries a price that is not money said twice in a doorway and a rule about names said to an empty store** and is **one word over it**. **The ceiling is not rounded and all three are recorded as over or under it as they stand.** The manuscript stands at 459,401 words across one hundred and twenty chapters — 202,117 in Volume 01, **184,039 measured** in Volume 02, 36,253 in Volume 03's first ten, and **36,992 in this batch.**

**The prose check that matters in this batch is the hedge.** Batch 0001's review found *about nine* eighty-seven times and *about four* sixty-seven times in 36,052 words and cut both by about sixty per cent without touching the hedge where a count is genuinely an estimate. This batch is measured at **fifty-six instances of *about nine* and sixty-eight of *about four* in 36,992 words**, of which **seven of the sixty-eight are the locked figure *about four hundred and forty foot*** — that figure appears twelve times in the batch, seven of them hedged and five stated plain — **leaving sixty-one**, and **thirteen unhedged instances of *four hundred and thirty miles* are a separate count and are not instances of *about four* at all.** These figures are the whole-word counts and each of them reproduces with `grep -ohiE '\babout nine\b'` and its like over the ten files. **The hedge survives in this book and is not going to be removed**, because in a district where nobody has counted anything for sixty years the hedge is a moral position and not a tic: a narrator who says *about nine feet of beaten earth* is saying *I do not know and I have not pretended to*. What was cut is the hedge in rooms the narrator has just counted, and the two figures of a room and a count of a lane are now stated.

## The Rules Established On The Page In Volume 03 Batch 0002, And The Locks Held

1. **A rule a body writes for itself, with a date on it, is a certificate of a kind that certifies itself: issued by nobody, binding nobody, deniable by anybody, and the only paper a body with no office behind it has ever had.** It is the Slade Cut Company's rule of the meeting of the twenty-eighth day of the third month and it is not an instrument, not a resolution, not a vote, not a demand, not an office and not a Roll Assembly. **No precedent is claimed of it and the words *exception* and *precedent* are not used of it.**
2. **A rule that only binds the people in the room binds the people in the room.** About thirty persons of about ninety-one households were in a hired bay on the twenty-eighth day of the third month and about sixty were not, and the two figures are written on the back of a hand together and not apart, and the ones who were not in the room are the ones the rule binds. **It is the same arithmetic as a magistrate's own-motion entry of the ninth of the second month and it is expressly not entered as an instance of anything, and the thirty-one households behind the bank at the third-of-the-way place are not joined to the thirty-one Mosswake households in any sentence in these ten chapters.**
3. **A rule a body writes for itself is a record and a record is findable, and a thing on a bench in the middle of a room is findable by anybody who walks up to it.** The Company has a bench in the middle of a bay now and it is the largest room within two miles of the Slade end and it holds about thirty and **its door does not shut, its lintel has dropped about half an inch in about forty years and nobody has had it packed, and that was said out loud in a doorway before the room was taken and not found out in the fourth month with thirty people in it.**
4. **A gift of a room is money with no figure on it and the whole of the cost of it is that a thing which is free is a thing nobody can refuse.** A bay of a miller's building is free for the year, and the finding is that a body that cannot be paid for cannot be compelled and a room given to a body is a room nobody can be asked to open — and that a thing which is free is a thing nobody can refuse, which is the same sentence read from the other end.
5. **A name in a book is findable and that is the only thing anybody has ever wanted out of a book, and the honest thing to do about a body that cannot be compelled is to be easy to compel yourself.** The first name against an entry in the Company's book in sixty years was written on the sixth day of the third month, **Aven Tace, of the mill yard, as a person and not as a crew and not a debtor, with the day on it.** A name on a page is a thing somebody said; a name against an entry is a thing somebody can be asked about the entry. **It is not a debt, not a claim, not a vote and not a party, and there is no way in this empire to give a name back.**
6. **A miller worked out alone in about six years, with nobody to ask, that the moment a name goes at the top of a paper about a bank, that bank is a thing that can be asked about the name — and nobody in the district had ever told him he was right.** His paper is about **four hundred and forty foot of outer face at the third-of-the-way place**, with **nine dates in his own hand on which he saw the face move, two of which he can prove and seven of which he cannot, and he has written down which is which and why**, and at the foot a line saying he sent it to the district works in the sixth year and twice after that and that nobody has ever come for it. **It was in an envelope in his own kitchen for six years and that was the same failure as a thing in a box in a water office, and the only difference is that nobody ever asked him for the envelope.** Nobody had ever told him he was right, and that is what Rennick Adley carried out of the yard.
7. **A column with no heading over it is findable without being answerable, and a column with a heading over it is a list of persons who answer for something.** The **fourth column of the Reed Cut district works' return book has no heading over it and has had none for thirty-one years**, and it holds **the name of whoever reported the work**, and it is a column of *who spoke* and not of *who answers*. **About four hundred people in three years are in it and every one of them is a person who walked into a yard and said a thing, and not one of them is answerable, and a foreman who has had the book for three years has thought in the first month of every year for nine years about writing a heading over that column and has thrown the paper away twice.**
8. **Nine years of a district's own bookkeeping and not one act of the district's flood authority is the reason nobody on the Slade bank is in a box, and a foreman who knew it for nine years had never said it out loud and had never been asked and nobody is going to ask him.** He has now entered it, on his own initiative, with the day on it, and put his own name at the foot of it for the first time in nine years.
9. **A form is a form of one of two sentences — a person accepts, or a person refuses — and there is no form in this empire for a third sentence.** There is no form for a keeper to say *give it back* and no form for a person to say *I am here and I do not want to be asked again*, **and those are the same absence twice, and it is the only authority that office has ever given away and there is no form for getting it back.** A keeper can write nine words in her own day-book in about a minute and **they will not be available to anybody, not to him, not to his sister, not to a court, not to a clerk.**
10. **A sheet that is void on one person's word is four words long and it is the only thing in this matter that anybody has made that works, and the price of using it is that the district's flood authority is then undispensed and there is no name on that bank anywhere at all.** A keeper gives a date with it — **the last day of the third month, and not one day past it, because the flood season's memoranda begin in the fourth week and the standing of that sheet is settled upstairs and not by the man, and she is not going up in the fourth week to find out which way it has gone without her in the room** — and a refusal is worth more with a day on it and worth nothing without one. **Three people know the date — the keeper, the man, and a clerk in a city four hundred and thirty miles away who does not know it yet — and it is the pull out of this batch.** **The date was the fourth week of the third month until the repair pass and is now the last day of it**, because the fourth week precedes the thirtieth on a month of twenty-eight days or more and the batch runs to the last day of the third month; the phrase *not one day past it* and the chapter's title are unchanged, and the date is now live on the last day of the batch instead of three days dead.
11. **What a notary's table asks a person is four lines, and the fourth line is *in what way the instrument touches you*, and the fourth line is the whole of the door.** The first three lines are a name, where the person may be found, and the instrument by its day, its year and what it is called, **and the first three are a fact and the fourth is the door, and a clerk of that counter has never known a clerk refuse a form on the first three lines in eleven years.** The form is a shilling whether it is filled in or not. The form **may be put in on a day before the instrument takes effect upon the person putting it in**, and that provision is used about four times a year in this empire and **is not a rule of the Table, is not in the form, and cannot be put in by the Table.**
12. **The reason the fourth line cannot be answered by anybody on the Slade is not that the form is too short. The honest sentence is a sentence about ninety-one households, and a sentence about ninety-one households with one person's name at the top of it is a paper about a body with a person written on it, and the reason they cannot say it is that the day they say it they are a party.** Said out loud at a counter in about eleven minutes by a clerk of thirty-four, eleven years at that counter, who is not a lawyer.
13. **A clerk is a person who can be asked things, and a foreman can be sent for and a clerk cannot, and about nine people a year find that out.** It is the worst fact about the Notaries' Table and the reason the Table has not been able to do anything wrong in eleven years that anybody could put in a minute.
14. **The price of a question at that table is eleven weeks and not a shilling, and the eleven weeks is not a fee, is not charged, and cannot go on the board, because the board has four things on it and none of them is time.** The eleven weeks is the length of the road between a question and the person it is about, and no Table in this empire can shorten it. **A question put in on the nineteenth of the third month is answered in the first week of the sixth, and the water is in the third week of the fourth, and a question about a bank that is answered after the water is on the bank is a question about a different thing.**
15. **A form bought and not filled in is four pence of proof that the question was asked, and it is the only thing a person can do with a form whose fourth line they cannot answer.** A clerk keeps about four hundred of them in a box under the counter, **because a Table is the only place in this empire where a person can come up, ask a question, and walk away with a piece of paper that says they asked it, and in about four years somebody is going to want to know whether a particular person asked, and she will be able to say yes with a date on it, and that is the only power anybody at that counter has.**
16. **A register of persons keeps persons and not records, and that is the whole of why it is safe, and it is also the whole of why it cannot help.** The Docket of Powers keeps a register of persons who read; about fourteen people a year agree to have a name in it; **a name in it is a name a man with a tray can ask about, for ever, for anything at all, and it is the one door in this empire that a person cannot shut.** **The only procedure for being entered is a written application of one line stating the authority under which the person reads.** Marek Kest has no authority, did not write the line, was not entered, and did not ask to be, **and the reason the master of that office did not ask him to is that the line is one line and could have been written in about nine seconds with a false authority in it, and a good day is not worth a name in a register that was put in for a man who was not entitled to it.**
17. **The only thing a register of persons cannot do is say what a form does to a person, and in this empire the only two people who can say are one who has already been harmed and one who has not.**
18. **A referral is the third of the Docket of Powers' three powers and it is the only one of the three that does anything, and a referral is worth nine days, a page, and nothing else in this empire.** The court sitting in room four has an order of the eighteenth of the seventh that binds nobody who has not been heard, **there is no form anywhere for compelling a person to be brought to a room, and about four people sit in a room on an ordinary afternoon, and the whole of the work the order creates is done by clerks at counters and women with barrows, none of whom is in the room where the order was made.** Referring a question costs the master of the office a day and a half of his own afternoon and **there is no fund in this office or in any other.**
19. **A person who turns up to be counted is not a person who has been heard**, and the master of the Docket of Powers wanted to write that on the back of the order for four months and did not, because he is a register and keeps what is put in front of him.
20. **An examination of a question is not a hearing, because there are no rules for one, and an examination produces nothing and is entered as a question examined and not answered.** The reason there are no rules for one is that the office which holds a bar has never had to enforce anything.
21. **A person harmed by a work is not a person an instrument touches, and being harmed does not open the notary's door.** A man of thirty-eight in a lane at the north of a ford was the first person harmed in this matter in eleven weeks, and the fourth line of the form cannot be filled in by him or for him, **because the fourth line asks about an instrument and nobody on that bank has read the instrument and the certified copy is in a run that is a number of a book.** A two-wheel went into the ford on the thirtieth of the third month at the shallow place because the shallow place is not there any more, and a boy of fifteen is out of the water and cannot stand on that leg, and nobody was harmed before that, and the water is in the third week of the fourth month.
22. **The seventh work on the page from ninety-one years back is the ford, and the stones, and the two posts, and it is on nobody's list and has not been on anybody's list for about thirty years.** The two posts are depth marks and **a ford with no depth marks and no hard stones in the throat is not a ford, it is a place where the water happens.**
23. **A record kept openly for customers is a record anybody can read, and it is the first person made findable in this matter by a record nobody made on purpose.** A landing's book is a slate in a wooden frame on a nail in the open on the end post of a landing below the Slade, kept there for about nine years because a customer wants to know whether his stone has gone, and **it is a record and not a register and is not evidence of anything against anybody.** It carries *third week of the second month, nine stones, hard, off the ford, north of the crossing, the throat. Hob Bray. Thirty-eight. Paid.*
24. **A person who stops a public work without authority has done the only thing anybody in this matter has been waiting for, and he has done it to keep his own boy out of a ford, and there is no form in this empire for a person doing a thing like that and no form for stopping him.** Nobody has asked him to stop. **He has not put the stones back and has said why: if he puts them back then the ford is the ford again and he is the man who took nine stones out of it, and the ford is the ford whether they are in it or not, and somebody will come eventually.**
25. **A party is a person with a door: a person who can be found, who can be served, who can be put in a room, and who can say no, and the last of those is the only one of the four that is worth anything and it is the one nobody asks about.** Said out loud, standing, in a shed, in front of nine people, by a person who is one.
26. **A household can be made a party on purpose, in advance, by people who know what a party is, and the price of it is that the household is findable from the day of asking, for ever, and for everything, and not only for the thing at the ford.** A household that is findable is a household a man with a tray can ask about, and nobody in this matter can know in advance which tray. **Being heard is a different act from being harmed, and the hearing that would have been held before the cart went in is the one that was not held, and it is gone, and nobody can hold it now for that boy.** The order of the court sitting in room four says an instrument has no operation upon any person until that person has been heard about it in a room in a place that recognises the room, **and that is a fact about a person and not about a document, and it is the one thing available before the water, and it is available to one person at a time and never to ninety-one households at once.**
27. **The only thing a body that cannot be summoned, cannot be a a party, and cannot ask a court for anything can do is ask, in a form, for named persons to be heard, and the persons named will be parties from the day the form is filed and cannot be un-named.** The line at the top of that form is for the head of the body and it is blank and it stays blank, and there has never been a head of the Slade Cut Company and there is not going to be one.
28. **Not choosing is the only honest thing available to a body asked to choose, because a body chooses the four that come to meetings, and the four everybody picks are never the four the thing is about.** Four households at the north of the ford were named because there are four houses at the north of the ford and not because anybody chose them. It took nine minutes to say and six of the nine people in a shed repeated it afterwards.
29. **A person who files an application naming four households as persons to be heard has made four households parties on the day of filing and cannot un-name them, and a clerk will say so out loud and put it on the face of the form in plain words before it is signed — because a decision with nobody's name at the foot of it is worth about a fifth of one with a name.** **The figure of about a fifth is a clerk's own and is out of her own experience and is the only figure on the form.**
30. **A described person is a person, and is harder to serve and easier to lose than a named one, and is worth a good deal less than a fifth of a named party in every use the court knows of — and the alternative to entering a description is that the person is not there at all.** Of the four doors, two gave a name, one gave a name before she was finished being asked, and one refused a name and a description and let herself be described.
31. **A name is findable and a family is not. *Skell* is a list of four doors and *Tarn Skell's son* is not a thing anybody can look up.** Asked at all four doors on the last day of the third month, nobody could say. One said she had been given the name in a kitchen and had never asked. One said there had always been Skells. One said the name was not on any of the stones in the lane and that a man who wanted a family should try a churchyard instead of a lane. **Tarn Skell is not identified as living or dead anywhere in these ten chapters and no chapter states that the four houses are related to him.**
32. **A copy that goes to a body is a copy about ninety-one households and a copy in the hands of one man is a copy about one man, and the man is the only person in ninety-one households who can be asked about it.** A clerk of nine years has never seen anybody buy a copy and not send it to the body it is about.
33. **An undertaking a magistrate entered on her own motion is usable by somebody she has never met, about a matter she has not heard, with her not in the room and not having to be, because it is a written undertaking and not a form and it does not require anybody to be in the room. That is the first time in this matter one of her sentences has been used against the fact of her not knowing whether she was right.**
34. **A form may be a worse way of doing a thing than a man at a door and it may still be done, and the way it is worse is that it is colder, it takes four days instead of four minutes, it is done in a building that recognises the room, and neither of those facts makes it smaller.** A person he had never met is a party to a bank because of a sentence he wrote, and the sentence is at the top of a form in a clerk's hand with the day on it.
35. **A count of a ford is a number of crossings and not a number of people, and a ford that is crossed on a plank and not on the stones has no count at all, and there is nobody in this district to ask for one.** The lane counts itself, twice, in the morning, and gets the same number both times, and the number of people in a lane is not a number anybody has to answer for because a lane is a lane and a lane is nobody. **This rhymes with a count of a bar four hundred and thirty miles away and is not joined to it and is not said to be the same thing.**
36. **Nothing has ever been written about these four houses in sixty years by anybody, and that was not a protection and it was a luck, and the luck was that nobody had ever needed a name from that lane — and a house nobody wants anything from is the safest place on that river, and the moment something is needed from it the luck is over and the protection is gone, and there is no way to have the name and not the luck.** A woman of about seventy-one worked this out in about two days from a wall with her leg on a sack and said it out loud in a lane.
37. **A person who turns up has to be told what she is turning up about, and nobody told her, and she went anyway, and the going is the whole of the cost and it is not paid and cannot be.** The second half of the answer to the fifth question of Volume 02, worked out at a landing with a hand on a wet rope and told to nobody, **and not to be told, because stating it in a room would be asking somebody to pay her, and she would then be a person who reads for money, and that is a different person.**
38. **A cost nobody can be shown the bill for is not a smaller cost. It is an unpriced one, and an unpriced one is a cost somebody else finds out about later, and there is nobody in the room who will be standing there later.** Said once, in a room, by a person who was not talking to the man it was about.
39. **A gate a person can shut is the only thing worth having and about sixty of them are closed on purpose at the foot of this bank, and the price of closing them is that the person who closed them can be found, and that is the arrangement and it is the only one on offer.** Four of a body's rooms are now shut: the slade, the left, the ash, the wash.
40. **The price of a foreman's second yes is not the fortnight's wage and not the fund and not a dragon who has given her authority away. The cost is that four households will now be known.** She knows it because a miller told her the same thing in a doorway in about nine sentences and nobody had ever told him either, **and she had been doing to him for nine years what she was now proposing to do to four other people and had not known that was a thing a person could be caught doing.**
41. **A letter is a road and it is a slow one, and a foreman's answer that the district works cannot put a person's name against the Slade, with a figure on it, is the first thing anybody outside that cut has said in nineteen years to tell a woman of sixty-four that her no was the right one.** It was said by a stranger, in a different building, about a different thing, and it took four lines.
42. **The undertaking of the fourth of the first month is live and is not retired and is not amended, and it caught a word three times, a fact once and an absence once in the first ten chapters, and it catches nothing in these ten.** **The rule the two of them made with each other in the twelfth month of last year is live and is not used a fourth time in these ten chapters.**
43. **No romance begins.** Tamsin Rook put the price of the matter into a room in Auremar and did not soften it, **and that is not intimacy, and Marek Kest said he would not thank her for saying it because that would make it a thing she did for him, and she said she said it for the book.** She is in rooms in a city four hundred and thirty miles away because a court order requires it and both of them know it and neither of them says it.
44. **A person who is not asked has not been given a duty, and it is not that a file is a secret.** A clerk of nine years has known which press and which shelf and which year a file of the sixth year is in since she became a clerk, and has not opened it in nine years, and about a hundred and forty people have walked up and down that corridor since and she has not wanted anybody to ask her, **and a person came on the last day of the third month and put a hand on it and did not open it, and she wrote that down, and it is the only line in that book in nine years about a thing nobody asked her about.**
45. **Marek Kest performed no reading of any instrument in these ten chapters, took no reading, carries no mark, holds no certification, wrote no instrument and no valid one, and the bar of the twelfth of the ninth stays discharged.** He is not a party to any proceeding and has no way to start one. **He still has an instrument in a locked book that he wrote in four lines in the second month and did not send, and the whole of its cost is named labour and named liability, and it is not a gift and it is not a fund and he is not paid.** He wrote in his own book, for the second time in the matter and without crossing it out, that there is a document in a room he works in that he has not opened and that the reason he has not opened it is the reason he has not started a case, **and that is the batch's only reference to anything not named, and it names nothing and advances nothing.**
46. **Tarn Skell, of the Slade**, the eleventh of the eleven names on the page from ninety-one years back, is in the district's map of nineteen years back as *Skell, T., household of nine, north of the ford*, and there are four houses of Skells at the north of the ford now, and **this batch's finding about him is a negative and it is the batch's best small fact: a name is findable and a family is not.** He is not identified as living or dead and no chapter states that the four houses are related to him. **A later batch may not turn him into a living man or a dead man or a discovered descendant without paying for it on the page.**
47. **The Slade Cut Company has a room it cannot pay for, and a paper it cannot pay for, and a name against an entry, and four shillings a year.** It has still chosen nobody, resolved nothing, guaranteed nothing, and is not a party to anything. **The rent of the south end of the store is a shilling a quarter and comes out of the book four times a year, and the north bay is not hired and costs nothing and is not recorded as a gift and is not a resolution.** The first pence the book has ever cost anything except the rent in eleven years is the four pence for a copy of the order of the court sitting in room four, and Rennick Adley entered that.
48. **The dates in this matter are three and nobody in it can move any of them: a man's word on the last day of the third month that would void the only sheet in the district which gave a flood authority to a person instead of a body, a sitting of the district court in the first week of the fourth month, and a water in the third week of the fourth month.** **There is no form in this empire for asking any court to fix a date at which a water is expected.** A book-keeper has asked up four roads and a towpath and has found out that if there is a thing in this matter a book-keeper of a company is not to know about, then that thing is probably the only thing holding that company together. **A fourth date exists and is not one of the three, because a foreman put it on herself: the ford work is held for the second week of the fourth month, and not one day past it, because that is the last week before the water. The second week of the fourth month is also the week the clerk of the fourth counter said she would take a form filled in at its fourth line, and the two are not joined.**
49. **Nothing is ordered, nobody is liable, no fund has been found, and no court has sat about a bank.** The road is open exactly one step wide and the step is a person, and the road is a hearing, and the hearing is in front of the water, and nothing in this matter can be hurried.

## Volume 03 Batch 0002 Calendar, The Sixth Of The Third Month To The Last Day Of The Third Month
