- **The keeper's date is the last day of the third month, and not one day past it, and it is given on the tenth and falls on the last day of the batch.** The flood season's memoranda begin in the fourth week and that is a separate fact about the district's own year, not the date. **The batch's own second half — the twenty-eighth, the twenty-ninth, the thirtieth and the last day — therefore all falls inside the month the date is in, and the date is live in every one of them.** It was the fourth week of the third month until the repair pass recorded above and the third month's length is still nowhere fixed on the page.

- **Sixth day of the third month:** Chapter 0111. The mill yard and the miller's own door, at about the fourth hour of the afternoon; the store from about the fifth hour; the letter written in the evening and sealed with a stone off the yard wall.
- **Seventh and eighth days of the third month:** Chapter 0112. The letter at the works gate at about the seventh hour of the seventh; the return book read down the yard on the morning of the eighth; the entry made at about the sixth hour of the eighth; said out loud in the yard at about the seventh hour; the answer written on the ninth.
- **Tenth day of the third month:** Chapter 0113. The Reed Cut district water office, at about the seventh hour; the day's book in about a minute; the walk back down the two hundred yards at about the fourth hour of the afternoon.
- **Nineteenth and twentieth days of the third month:** Chapter 0114. Auremar, the Notaries' Table, the fourth counter, on the nineteenth; the form bought for a shilling and not filled in; the corridor behind the table.
- **Twenty-first day of the third month:** Chapter 0115. Auremar, the Docket of Powers, the third room, from about a quarter before the second hour of the afternoon; the day's book; the door; the Received Room at about the ninth hour of the same night.
- **Twenty-second to twenty-seventh days of the third month:** the journey down the river, about six days on a towpath, in a cart and on a bench, and **the chapters do not pretend it is shorter.**
- **Twenty-eighth day of the third month:** Chapter 0116. The north bay of the grain store, at about the seventh hour; the rule read out twice; the letter read out twice; the bench carried into the middle of the room at about the eighth hour; the meeting an hour more.
- **Twenty-ninth day of the third month:** Chapter 0117. The towpath at about the sixth hour in the morning; the lane at about the eighth hour; the woman in the fourth door; the landing on the way back; about the fifth hour.
- **Thirtieth day of the third month:** Chapter 0118, in the lane at the north of the ford, morning to about the second hour of the afternoon, with a foreman arriving at about the fourth hour; then **Chapter 0119**, the tool shed at about the seventh hour, the entry written at about the ninth hour, and the bank at the third-of-the-way mark after that.
- **Last day of the third month:** Chapter 0120. The Mosswake Roll Court, the clerk's window from about the ninth hour, the counter and the sill, the embankment corridor at about the second hour of the afternoon, and the towpath back at about the fourth hour. **The night before, Rennick Adley walked four miles up the towpath and four miles back in the dark to four doors at the north of the ford.**

## Figures Named In Volume 03 Batch 0002

- **Four new named figures**, all bound in `bible/characters.md` under **Supporting Figures Added In Volume 03, Batch 0002**: **Roan Skell**, about fifty-eight, second house at the north of the ford, the only one of the four who can read a paragraph and who has never been asked to read anything in sixty years; **Bel Skell**, about seventy-one, fourth house, cannot read a paragraph, the POV of Chapter 0118; **Marn Ottery**, thirty-four, eleven years at the fourth counter of the Notaries' Table in Auremar; **Hob Bray**, thirty-eight, who sells stone off a landing below the Slade, named off a nail in the open by a girl of seventeen who declined to ask him. **No fifth new name is added and none may be.**
- **Unnamed and to stay unnamed:** the **clerk of the Reed Cut district works**; **the woman of about thirty-one with two children at the first house at the north of the ford**, who counted the lane twice and refused a name; **the woman of about forty-four at the third house**; **the doctor of the reach**, about fifty, the second house below the mill; **the copyist of the third gallery**; and the whole Batch 0001 list of unnamed figures, all of whom are undisturbed.
- **Figures on this bank, and they are three and they may never be put in one sentence as a pattern:** **the thirty-one households behind the bank at the third-of-the-way place**, a fact about a bank, in nine acres; **the thirty-one Mosswake households of the sixth year**, a fact about letters in a press in another district; and **about thirty-one people in nine houses in a lane at the north of the ford**, a fact about a lane that counted itself twice in the morning. **The thirty-one of Mosswake are not touched in these ten chapters, no figure is printed against them, nobody adds the six to the eighteen, and no chapter adds a thirty-second household.**
- **Figures that are new and are arithmetic locks from this day forward:** **the north bay is thirty-one foot by nineteen and holds about thirty**; **the fourth column of the district works' return book has no heading and holds about four hundred names in three years**; **the Notaries' Table has about four hundred blanks in a box under the fourth counter**; **a notary's table answers in about eleven weeks and the form is a shilling and a copy is four pence**; **a referral costs a day and a half of a man of sixty's own afternoon with no fund**; **the embankment corridor holds about a hundred and forty files and the flue in the room behind the window was cut on the fourteenth of the second month**; **a decision with nobody's name at the foot of it is worth about a fifth of one with a name**, and that is a clerk's figure and the only figure on the form.

## The Six In-Chapter Corrections Made On The Page Before This Batch Closed, And Checked Clean

1. **A calendar month name was in Chapter 0114** (*a man of sixty in a building in this city lost a day and a half of his own afternoon in February*) **and is gone**; the line now reads *in the second month of this year*. A check of all ten files for the twelve month names returns nothing. **The word *may* appears twenty-eight times in the ten files and every one of them is the modal verb; there is no capital *May* anywhere and no month is named by it.**
2. **A calendar month name was in Chapter 0115** (*since the reign of the third of the Ashfall line*) **and is gone**; the line now reads *for about four hundred years*, and a check of all ten files returns nothing for Ashfall, for the red thread, for the bundle, for the second sealed page and for Ilyra Kest.
3. **An age contradicted itself.** Chapter 0112 had Wendt Sayer *sixty-one's age next year* in an odd construction and Chapter 0115 had Oswin Marrow *sixty-one* in one place and *sixty* in another. Sayer is now **the wrong side of sixty** and Marrow is **sixty** in both.
4. **A duplicated rhythm.** Chapter 0116 had *I have been a chair for nineteen years* four times in about three thousand three hundred words, twice within six lines. It is now twice, and the two other instances are recast.
5. **A door count was hedged after it had been made.** Chapter 0118 had *nine or ten people out of about nine doors* and *about thirty-one people in about nine houses* in a scene where the lane had counted itself twice and got the same number both times. Both are now stated.
6. **A copying error was a typo instead of a doubled word.** Chapter 0120 first carried *can be a a party* as a clerk's own transcription slip. It is now *can be be a party*, which is what a hand copying a hand in a hurry actually does, and the whole beat is about the slip having travelled.

**Checked and found clean, with nothing to correct:** no name supplied for the authority of the sealed instruction; no reading of any instrument performed by anybody; no mark, no Roll-Answer, no certification, no valid exception, counter-seal, counter-mark, mixed warrant or true-name reading, and **the word *exception* does not appear anywhere in the ten files**; the two documents neither named nor joined and Hallis Dren's return still dormant and armed and known to nobody in the story; the substituted phrase *the present holder* absent, the public-necessity sentence absent, no fifth instance of anything and the four appearances not written as a chain; **the north-bank works instrument untouched and not called a seam**; **the girl of eleven at the Reed Cut gate unused, unnamed and given no reason**, and her mother Nery in no record and not Nery Caudle, and **the woman of about seventy-one who cannot read a paragraph is not the girl of eleven and the two are never equated**; **Raff Ordway, sixteen, is not in these ten chapters and the boy of fifteen at the north of the ford is not Raff Ordway**; the date the fifth term's schedule was nailed to the rail printed nowhere and its age not restated, and the schedule referred to without a date and without a figure of an age; the Venn ruling still in five terms and neither the fourth nor the fifth applied as a rule of this cut; **the words *exception* and *precedent* not used of the Venn certification, and *precedent* appearing once in the batch, in a clerk-room statement of the general doctrine quoted from the finding of the ninth of the eighth**; the thirty-one of Mosswake and the eighteen and the six and the fourth column untouched and the board above the sill not restated; Lowcross untouched and the bill unpaid; **the two halves of the winning sentence nine days out of order and neither court having read the other, and not joined**; the fourth envelope still there, not sent, not opened; 44,819 not read, the year in the run not entered, the ninety-second leaf not looked for, Corvin Dray not re-litigated; and **no resolution, nothing ordered, nobody liable, and the Company has still chosen nobody.**

## Divergences Between The Cards And The Page, Volume 03 Batch 0002

Recorded here and at the foot of `outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0002.md`, so that Batch 0003 does not write against the cards. **None of these is a canon change and none contradicts an inherited fact; the page governs in every case.**

1. **Chapter 0115's location line on the card reads *the fourteenth and twenty-first of the third month*.** The card's own calendar gives Chapter 0115 the **twenty-first** only, and Chapter 0114 the nineteenth and twentieth in Auremar. The page is the twenty-first alone, in Auremar, in the Docket of Powers' third room, and the six days down the river are between the twenty-first and the twenty-eighth.
2. **Chapter 0117's card gives the man with the barrow as *a man of forty-four* in one line and *Hob Bray, thirty-eight* in its continuity fact.** The page is **thirty-eight**, which is the card's own continuity figure and the figure the landing's book carries.
3. **Chapter 0117's card has Tavi Venn read *the miller's paper* out of her own bag, and the page does that.** The card does not say where the paper came from, and the page supplies it: she was in the bay on the twenty-eighth and took it off the bench in the middle of the room and carried it two hours up a towpath in her own bag, and nobody asked her to.
4. **Chapter 0120's card assumes four named persons at four doors.** The page has **two names and two descriptions of persons**, because a woman of about thirty-one at the first house refused a name and let herself be described, and the card's own rules limit new named figures to four. **This is a divergence that improves the chapter**: it produces the court's finding that a described person is a person and is harder to serve and easier to lose, which no card asked for and which the batch needed.
5. **Chapter 0120's card has Rennick Adley's frame silent on how he got four names.** The page has him walk four miles up a towpath the night before and four miles back in the dark, get two names in under a minute each, eleven minutes at one door, a name given before the asking was finished at one, and a refusal at one, and it puts **Tarn Skell's question at all four doors and gets nothing at any of them**, which is where the batch's best small fact is now printed.
6. **Chapter 0113's card has the keeper not repeat that she is not the First Seat; the page does not repeat it either, and the not-repeating is argued for by the keeper and refused by the man** — he asks her, she says no, she says she said it once in a shed in the first month before anybody asked and that a person who has heard a thing once does not need to hear it twice, and that the moment a keeper starts repeating herself in her own office she has begun to need somebody to believe her.
7. **Chapter 0116's card and the page both have Doreth Ferrow hear the foreman's letter read out as the first thing anybody has said in nineteen years to say her no was the right one.** The page adds that **the letter does not say she was right** — it says the district could not have been asked, with a figure on it — and that she noticed, and said so in a room, and that the shape of it is the shape of the twentieth of the first month. **The page is the better beat and it is kept.**
8. **Chapter 0119's card has the four households named as persons to be heard *after* the thing at the ford.** The page has the foreman saying, in a shed, that the hearing that would have been held before the cart went in is the one that was not held and is gone, and nobody can hold it now for that boy, and that the only thing anybody can hold now is a hearing before the next one. That is the card's own resistance and the page has moved it from the card's frame into the mouth of the person who knows it.
9. **The word *precedent* appears once in the batch**, in Chapter 0114, in a clerk-room statement by Marek Kest of the general doctrine as it was found by the district court of Auremar's lower district of the ninth of the eighth, and quoted as the price said out loud in the room when that finding was made. **It is not used of the Venn certification and the certification is not named in the chapter.**
10. **The third month is left unfixed in length.** Chapters 118 and 119 are on the thirtieth and Chapter 120 is on the last day, and the page never states a figure for the last day and never reconciles the two, so the recorded ambiguity is inherited unchanged and no month is fixed anywhere. **The repair pass did not touch this and could not have fixed Finding 2 through it**: the fourth week of a month precedes the thirtieth on a month of twenty-eight days or more whatever the month is, and the keeper's date therefore could not be left in the fourth week while the batch runs to the last day. **It is now the last day of the third month, which is unambiguous under either convention**, and the ambiguity about the month's length stands exactly where it stood.
11. **Chapter 0116's card has no foreman in the bay and the page has one.** Halla Wray is in the north bay on the twenty-eighth of the third month, at the back, for the whole meeting, and nobody asks her anything and Doreth Ferrow **notices and does not ask and has not told anybody she noticed.** The card does not place her there. **The beat is kept and is better than the card**, because it is the only thing on the page that says the Company had a person who could have said yes in the room and did not, two days before a book-keeper asked her the same question in a shed and she said yes, and because it is the reason Chapter 0119's *"you have not asked her twice"* lands. **Her term had run out in the third week of the month before either room, and nobody asked her in either.**
12. **The three thirty-ones.** There are **three** different figures of thirty-one in this matter and they may never be put in one sentence as a pattern: the thirty-one households behind the bank at the third-of-the-way place, the thirty-one Mosswake households of the sixth year, and about thirty-one people in a lane at the north of the ford. The card for Chapter 0116 named two of them and the page has three. **The card file said *the two are different thirty-ones* and has been corrected to three at `outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0002.md`, because that file is what a Batch 0003 writer reads.**

## The Repair Pass Applied To Volume 03 Batch 0002 After Its Review, And Checked Clean

**`logs/batch-0002.review.log` is the transcript of the review phase. The review was run by the writer agent itself, because `.opencode/agent/novel-reviewer.md` declares `mode: subagent` and `scripts/novel_runner.sh` passes `--agent novel-reviewer`, which opencode refuses and falls back to the default agent; that is a controller-owned file and is not fixed here. Everything below is therefore a self-review and is recorded as one.** The pass changed no plot, invented no fact outside the batch, restarted nothing, rewrote no scene, and deleted no chapter. Thirteen findings were returned and **all thirteen are answered here: twelve as the numbered items below, and one — the foreman in the bay that no card records — as divergence 11 in the list above. The sixth numbered item is a defect this pass found and the review did not return, and it is numbered with the rest and marked as the pass's own.**

### The two that mattered, fixed on the page

1. **The batch's own clock was expiring off the page across four chapters.** Chapter 0113 gave a man of twenty-nine a keeper's date — *the fourth week of the third month, and not one day past it* — and the batch then runs to the twenty-eighth, the twenty-ninth, the thirtieth and the last day. The fourth week of a month precedes the thirtieth on a month of twenty-eight days or more whatever the length of the month is, so **the date was already three days dead when Chapter 0117 opened and Chapter 0120 stated it in the present tense on the last day of the month**, and Piotr Wray sat in a shed on the thirtieth with a word he could not spend and said nothing about it. **The date is now the last day of the third month, and not one day past it.** The phrase and the chapter's title are unchanged; the date is now live on the last day of the batch and expires at the moment the batch ends, which is where it belongs and is what `outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0003.md` needs to open on. **The keeper's reason was re-anchored in one clause and nothing else in the speech moved**: the flood season's memoranda still begin in the fourth week and the standing of the sheet is still settled upstairs and not by the man, and she now says that she is not going up in the fourth week to find out which way it has gone without her in the room. **The date was carried in twelve files and every one of them was changed with the page** — the two chapters, this file, `state/current.md`, `state/batch-summary.md`, `state/chapter-summaries.md`, `state/character-state.md`, `state/open-threads.md`, `bible/characters.md`, both Volume 03 card files and the Batch 0003 prompt — and a check of the ten chapters for *fourth week of the third month* and *fourth week of this month* returns nothing.
2. **The referral misidentified its own applicant.** Chapter 0115 is the whole of Marek Kest's: he brings the question, he dictates none of it, he reads it back, he signs the day's book as M. Kest, and the referred text then described *the applicant* as **a person of about twenty-nine in the district of Mosswake** — a man of twenty-nine four hundred and thirty miles away who is not the applicant, and who is the only other person in the matter of that age. That also implied a coordination between the Slade and a city that the batch's engine insists does not exist, because the handoff is that **a man in a city four hundred and thirty miles from that counter does not know the date yet.** **The applicant is now a person of about thirty-eight in the city of Auremar, four hundred and thirty miles from the cut the question is about**, which is who the chapter is about and which agrees with Chapter 0113.

### Three more date and age defects, fixed on the page

3. **Chapter 0116 contradicted its own calendar in its first line.** *It is cold at the seventh hour in the last week of the third month* — for a meeting called on the **twenty-eighth**, with Chapters 0118 and 0119 on the thirtieth and Chapter 0120 on the last day. **The line now reads *on the twenty-eighth day of the third month***, which is the day the rest of the chapter gives four times, and the ambiguity of *the last week* is gone from the page instead of being left for a reader to resolve.
4. **Chapter 0112's elapsed time was off by thirteen days.** The foreman's answer is dated the **ninth** and the chapter said the book-keeper read it out to about thirty people *six days later*, which is the fifteenth. **The bay meeting is the twenty-eighth** and Chapter 0116 gives the seventh, the ninth and the twenty-eighth itself. **It is now nineteen days later**, and the sentence's own timepiece — *in nineteen years* — sits beside it and is not disturbed.
5. **Four men of thirty-eight, two of them unnamed.** The named thirty-eights are Rennick Adley, Ivet Sarn, Marek Kest and Hob Bray, and Hob Bray's age is written on a slate on a nail in his own hand, so it cannot move. **The two unnamed thirty-eights are the man who said the north bay was empty in the mill yard in Chapter 0111 and the man who took nine stones out of the ford in Chapter 0117, and they are two different people** — Chapter 0111:101 rules out Rennick being the mill-yard speaker, and the boy and the cart at the ford belong to Hob Bray. **The man in the mill yard is now forty-six.** He is unnamed on the page, is named nowhere, and is not the miller.

### A fourth date this pass found, which the review did not return

6. **Chapter 0119 held the ford work for a week that comes after the water.** Halla Wray put the date on her own condition herself and said why: *the fourth week of the fourth month, and not one day past it, because that is the week before the water.* **The water is in the third week of the fourth month**, so the date she gave was a fortnight after the thing the work is for and the reason she gave for it was false on the page. **It is now the second week of the fourth month, and not one day past it, because that is the last week before the water** — which is true, and which is also a date already on the page, because the clerk of the fourth counter said in Chapter 0114 that she would take a form filled in at its fourth line if he came back **in the second week of the fourth month**. **The work is still held, still unpaid and still conditional on a figure nobody has found, and no plot moves: the only thing that changed is that the date is now before the water instead of after it.** It was carried in eight files and every one of them was changed with the page — Chapter 0119 twice, this file, `state/current.md`, `state/batch-summary.md`, `state/chapter-summaries.md`, `state/character-state.md`, the Batch 0002 card file and the Batch 0003 prompt. **Chapter 0114's own use of that week is a different thing and was left exactly as it was.**

### The bookkeeping, and every figure in it re-measured

7. **"Eight of the ten are inside 3,200–4,000" was wrong in two files and is seven.** The chapters inside the band are **112, 113, 114, 116, 117, 118 and 119**; the three that are not are **111 at 4,201, 115 at 4,225 and 120 at 4,195.** The sentence named 111 and 115 as the two over the band and then covered 120 separately, so the arithmetic did not close. Corrected in `state/continuity.md` and `state/current.md`.
8. **Chapter 111 at 4,201 was described as inside the about-4,200 ceiling. It is one word over it, and the ceiling is not rounded.** The record now says so, and says the same of 115 at 4,225 (25 over) and 120 at 4,195 (5 under). **All three are recorded as over or under as they stand and none was trimmed to reach a number.**
9. **The hedge audit did not reproduce, and it overstated itself in the favourable direction.** It claimed seventy instances of *about four* of which twenty-three were the locked figure *about four hundred and forty foot* and twelve more were *four hundred and thirty miles*, leaving about thirty-five. Measured whole-word: **fifty-six of *about nine* (that claim was right) and sixty-eight of *about four*, of which seven are the locked figure, leaving sixty-one, and thirteen unhedged instances of *four hundred and thirty miles* which are not instances of *about four* at all.** The locked figure appears twelve times in the batch, seven hedged and five plain. **Every figure in the record now reproduces from the files with a whole-word count and the method is written down with it**, because the one record whose purpose is to prove Batch 0001's hedge reduction was sustained is the last record that may flatter itself.
10. **A claim of one *may* where there are twenty-eight.** *The only lowercase may in the batch is the modal verb* asserted a count of one. **The word appears twenty-eight times and every one is the modal verb; there is no capital *May* and no month is named by it.** The sentence now says that instead of implying a count it does not make.
11. **The card file said the two thirty-ones.** It is three, and the card file is what the next writer reads. Corrected there and recorded as divergence 12 above.

### The two next-phase prompt findings

12. **`workspace/volume-03/batch-0003/PROMPT.md` said the cards file did not exist. It exists, and the review's description of the consequence was wrong in the other direction.** `outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0003.md` is 29,672 bytes and carries all eleven required sections, **and its Chapter Cards section says in terms that the ten cards have not been written yet and are to be written by the run that takes the prompt, and its closing section says it is a placeholder.** So the file is the batch's plan and not yet its cards, and the instruction to write the ten cards before any prose was right. **The prompt now says the file exists, must be read, and that the ten chapter cards inside it are a declared placeholder the next run must fill first.** The next writer is not sent to create a file that is there and is not sent to skip the cards.
13. **An average of 3,605.** 36,253 ÷ 10 is **3,625**. Corrected, and the prompt's two averages are now 3,625 and 3,699.

### Checked and found clean, with nothing to correct

The word-count record's arithmetic closes and every per-chapter figure in it reproduces; **no calendar month name, no weekday name and no *Ashfall* in the ten files**, and a check for all three returns nothing; **no reading, no mark, no certification, no *exception*, no *seam*, no Burden-Bearer, no Roll-Answer, no counter-seal and no counter-mark**; *the present holder* and the public-necessity sentence both absent; **the word *precedent* still appears once and only in Chapter 0114**, in the same clerk-room statement of the general doctrine; **the deliberate *be be* in Chapter 0120 is a staged in-fiction transcription slip and is still paid off two lines later**; the two sealed pages still shut and no third refusal; the Ashfall bundle unopened and unnamed; the two documents still two documents and Hallis Dren's return still dormant; the substituted phrase absent and no fifth instance; the north-bank works instrument not called a seam; the girl of eleven at the Reed Cut gate unused, unnamed and given no reason, and not equated with the woman of seventy-one; Raff Ordway not in the batch and the boy of fifteen not Raff Ordway; the date the fifth term's schedule was nailed to the rail printed nowhere; Lowcross untouched and the bill unpaid; **the three thirty-ones recorded as three and never joined**; and **no figure, name, date or claim in the ending of the volume, the series outline or any future batch was touched.**

## What A Volume 03 Batch 0003 Writer Inherits, In One Page

- **The water is in the third week of the fourth month and the bank holds until then and not after.** A crew of five men and a boy did nineteen days of work in the first and second months and were paid three pounds eleven and threepence out of a clerk's own wage, being three weeks of it at seventeen shillings and a penny the week, and the works fund's head has been empty since the third week of the twelfth month. **The foreman has said she would rather be asked than not and has been asked twice and has said yes twice, and has refused to be asked a third time without saying so.**
- **The ford work is held in a named person's own name with a date of the second week of the fourth month, and not one day past it, and it is not paid for.** The condition is that the crew be paid out of something with a figure on it and not out of a man's wage in a city, and the work waits rather than be done a second time for nothing. **There is no such thing at present.** About one hundred and forty feet of ditch on the south side and the scour at the third-of-the-way place are still on nobody's list.
- **Four households at the north of the ford are parties from the filing of an application on the last day of the third month and cannot be un-named.** Two are named on the form; **two are described, and the court has entered that a described person is harder to serve and easier to lose.** The application is in the wrong form with a line at the top of it blank, admitted under a written undertaking of the ninth of the second month with the magistrate not in the building, and **a sitting has been asked for in the first week of the fourth month and there is no form for the date of a water.**
- **A man of twenty-nine at the far end of the cut has until the last day of the third month to say one word that would void the only sheet in that district which gave a flood authority to a person instead of a body.** Three people know the day and they are the keeper of the water office, the man himself, and **a clerk in a city four hundred and thirty miles away at a counter, who referred the question that is the other half of this matter and does not know it yet.** **Two of the three were in one room on the tenth day of the third month and the third was not, and nobody in the matter and no book-keeper knows it.** If he says the word, the district's flood authority is undispensed and there is no name on that bank anywhere at all. **The date was the fourth week of the third month until the repair pass after this batch's review and is now the last day of it; the phrase *not one day past it* is unchanged and a Batch 0003 writer may not put it back into a week.**
- **A form bought and not filled in lies in a book with no office on it, four hundred and thirty miles away, with a shilling out of a copyist's own wage in it, and the fourth of its four lines is blank.** The fourth line is *in what way the instrument touches you* and it is the whole of the door, and the honest answer for anybody on that bank is a sentence about ninety-one households, and the reason it cannot be written is that the day it is written the writer is a party.
- **A question has been referred from the Docket of Powers to the court sitting in room four and is worth nine days and a page and nothing else in this empire.** About four hundred people will be on the four pages. Marek Kest is one of them, and about four hundred names are a trade in about four years.
- **The road is open exactly one step wide and the step is a person, and the road is a hearing, and the hearing is in front of the water.**
- **Untouched and unjoined and unpaid:** the two sealed pages with no third refusal; the Ashfall bundle unopened on the second shelf and **named nowhere in this batch**; the three red things that may never be joined in a sentence; the MC/FS circular and the First Seat quarterly return, which are **not named in this batch at all** and remain two documents, with Hallis Dren's return of the eleventh of the tenth still dormant, armed and known to nobody in the story; the counting-house mark, the year in the run, 44,819, the ninety-second leaf; Lowcross, open, unpaid, nineteen pounds three and fourpence; the thirty-one, the eighteen, the six and the fourth column.
- **The volume's climax, Stage 2, Burden-Bearer, is not on the page in these ten chapters and is not paid for.** What Batch 0003 owes it is the price, the consent and the body that would be made a guarantor. **Marek Kest still has the instrument in a locked book and he can write it and may not, and he has now said out loud in a room that he knows its price and does not know its purpose, and a man of sixty has asked him what it is for and been given a different answer correctly.**
- **The question the volume is for, restated out loud in a shed on the thirtieth of the third month to nine people:** what a party is, what it costs to make one on purpose in advance, and who pays it. Six of the nine repeated it afterwards.
- **Not on the page, not referred to, and not to be answered in Volume 03:** what Ilyra Kest found in the Ashfall archive that was worth dying for, and why the original document names a blank heir. **Volume 03's door to it is a run number in a notary's table and a category of person who may be made a party, and this batch walked up to that door, found four lines, and could not fill in the fourth.**

Six of the ten are inside 3,200–4,000 (121, 122, 123, 124, 129, 130); three sit under the 3,200 floor (126, 127, 128); one is over 4,000 (125).** Chapter 125 is **five hundred and sixteen words over the about-4,200 ceiling and is the one chapter in Volume 03 that is.** It was kept because it carries the whole of the first instrument, the undertaking, the choice put in writing, the answer in a room, four days of not acting, and the reason it was sent to a foreman at a gate rather than to the water office, and **it was not trimmed to reach a number and the overage is recorded as it stands.** Chapters 126, 127 and 128 are the volume's quietest pages and are in or near the aftermath band, which is a record and not a licence.

**Volume 03 is therefore 107,714 words across forty chapters after Batch 0003 (36,253 in the first ten, 36,992 in the second, 34,469 in the third), and the manuscript is 493,870 across one hundred and twenty chapters.** Every one of these was re-measured after the prose was closed, because the previous figures in `state/current.md` were neither the sum of their own parts nor the reproducible count.

## The Rules Established On The Page In Volume 03 Batch 0003, And The Locks Held

1. **A void sheet costs four words and one minute and produces a document.** The flood sheet of the ninth of the first month is void on the refusal of one of the two persons named in it, entered by the keeper of the district water office in the office's own front desk-book with the day on it at about the fourth hour of the last day of the third month. **The district's flood authority in respect of the Slade spring works and the ford is entered as undispensed, with a date, and is NOT entered as *no person available***, because a sentence about people in a return of that office would be read in nine years as a finding that nobody was willing.
2. **A box has to have something in it and a box with nothing in it is a form that has not been filled in and does not go out.** The season's first memorandum for the Slade Cut is out with the **Slade Cut Company** in the box headed *who is to answer*, made in about four minutes by a clerk who was given a box and no word of what to put in it. **A body in a box is a different failure from the substituted wording and is expressly not an instance of it; the phrase *the present holder* does not appear anywhere in Chapters 111–130 and does not appear in Chapters 131–140.** Nobody wrote the substituted wording and nobody wrote this. The two are about a hundred and forty years of form design and nothing whatever to do with anybody's hand.
3. **A duty put on a body is distributed, not shared, and distribution is what makes it permanent.** Found on the page in Chapter 0127 by a woman of sixty-four who cannot read a paragraph, out of a book she cannot read, by counting: the rotation runs on the Company's book; the book has no heading, about nine hundred and forty names in about sixty years, no list of persons, and **about four hundred of the nine hundred and forty people in it are dead**; a person on that book is a householder; **a householder's duty is a household's duty, so about sixty children under sixteen are inside a duty they were not born into.** The chapter's sentence, said standing in mud in a mill yard to a book-keeper and to nobody else: **a burden moved onto ninety-one people is not shared. It is distributed. And distribution is what makes it permanent.**
4. **A body cannot refuse, and a body that is given a duty and cannot refuse has accepted it.** Said out loud, standing, in a room of about thirty, in about four minutes, by the chair of a body of persons who cannot read a paragraph. **The acceptance is on the face of the paper in about nine words that nobody in ninety-one households ever said**, and the paper says in the same nine words that the acceptance does not evidence a consent. **About nine words is a very cheap way of not asking anybody.**
5. **Named labour and named liability are the whole of a cost and a fund is not.** A duty with a figure for the work and no figure for the person is not a duty anybody can refuse, **because they cannot know what they are refusing.** A duty with a fund behind it stops when the fund does, and the works fund's head has been empty since the third week of the twelfth month.
6. **A gate a person can shut is the only thing worth having, and about sixty of them are closed on purpose at the foot of that bank, and the price of closing one is that the person who closed it can be found, and that is the arrangement and the only one on offer.** The cost is a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week and a day rate of forty-five pence on five men and a boy; the price is a figure on a piece of paper in a bay that can be read out to about thirty people.
7. **A counter-seal, and a counter-mark, and what they are for.** The formal record of the second exception is **a counter-seal: the exception itself, with his name at the top of it and a witness in the room, and it is a human clerk's instrument — it does not cross a species, it is a record, and it is findable, and it will be produced in about four years by anybody who wants it.** Where the consequence falls on Marek Kest the physical symptom is **a counter-mark, a line about two inches long on the inside of the left wrist**, and it is **a symptom of the legal burden and not a separate power: it reads nothing, it decides nothing, it imposes no geographic tether and no obligation that is not written on the paper in front of about thirty people, and it does not know where the ford is. THE MARK IS NOT THE PRICE. THE WAGE IS THE PRICE.** A figure can be read out to thirty people; a line cannot be read out to anybody.
8. **A refusal before a consent is the whole of the consent.** The consenting party in the second exception is **a person and not a body; she said no first, in about nine sentences, in a room of about thirty, and set one condition, and the condition was about the record and not about the work**: that it be written down that she was asked, that she said no, and the day of each, **and that both days be on the list of works and not only the second one, so that in about four years the page cannot be read as a thing that was agreed.** The refusal and the consent are in the same hand on the same page. **The affected party must be heard, the hearing is not a formality, and the whole of what a refusal costs the person refusing is nothing.**
9. **The first exception, sent on the twelfth of the fourth month:** nine pages, moving one specified consequence — the outer face of about four hundred and forty foot at the third-of-the-way mark, and the seventh work of eleven on the page from ninety-one years back, being the ford, the stones and the two posts — off two persons named in a void sheet and off the district of Reed Cut, **onto the Slade Cut Company, which is a body and is not a person and cannot be a party and cannot refuse, and onto the persons named in the Company's own book, in rotation, for a term, with a review date and a right of refusal that costs the person refusing nothing.** It is technically good, it is the correct instrument and the wrong shape, **and the whole of how it does the work is that it never asks anybody twice.** It was sent to a foreman at a gate **because a paper with a person's name at the top of it is the only thing in this empire that can be put through a gate.** Its schedule is the Company's book, in the order the book is in, and the book has never once been chosen from. **It says on its own seventh page that nothing in it survives the last term of the last name in the schedule, and that sentence is the only reason anybody will be able to say in about four years that its writer was not a villain.**
10. **The second exception, the volume's climax, written in a hired bay in the first week of the fifth month on the back of a lapsed list:** one specified consequence, different from the first, moved **off the Company, off the schedule and off the rotation, and onto one named person who is not of the Company, is not of the district, has no house there and no household there, and has no child there, so that there is no household in ninety-one households into which it can pass.** Term one year, review on the day, and the review is not a promise to continue, **and if nobody comes to the review then this stops on the day, and there is no successor and nothing carries.** The labour is named as a day rate and a wage and the liability is named, and **no household, no child and no person in the schedule is answerable for one penny of it or one hour of it.**
11. **The word *exception* is used in Chapters 121–130 and of two instruments and nothing else**, both with their conditions, their limits and their cost in the same room. **It is not used of the Venn certification, and the words *exception* and *precedent* are not used of that certification anywhere in the volume.**
12. **The undertaking of the fourth of the first month caught an absence for the second time in this matter and an answer for the first time.** The absence: a sheet in his coat for eleven days while a copyist sat in the room for two hours. The answer: the fourth use of the rule the two of them made in the twelfth month of last year, **the answer being *two, he sends it*, given in a room, in words, with a date of the twelfth, with a reason that is not a legal one and is refused, in a room, permission to be written down because a state of mind is the one thing in this matter a copy can take away from him.** It was used a fifth time in Chapter 0138 and not a sixth time in this volume.
13. **Locks held and checked, with nothing to correct:** no name supplied for the authority of the sealed instruction; **no reading of any instrument performed by anybody in Chapters 121–130 and none in 131–140, and the only two readings in the manuscript remain Chapter 8 and Chapter 40**; no Roll-Answer, no certification, no mixed warrant, no true-name reading, no true seal; **no calendar month name and no weekday name in any of the twenty chapters**; the substituted phrase absent, the public-necessity sentence absent, no fifth instance of anything, the four appearances not written as a chain and not in date order; the north-bank works instrument untouched and not called a seam; **the file of the sixth year in the press in the embankment corridor not opened and not spoken of**; the girl of eleven at the Reed Cut gate unused, unnamed and given no reason, and not equated with Bel Skell; Raff Ordway not in the batch and the boy of fifteen not Raff Ordway, and no statement that the boy is permanently injured; **the date the fifth term's schedule was nailed to the rail printed nowhere and its age not restated**; the Venn ruling still in five terms; Lowcross untouched and the bill unpaid; the two halves of the winning sentence nine days out of order and not joined; the fourth envelope still there, not sent, not opened; 44,819 not read, the year in the run not entered, the ninety-second leaf not touched; the three thirty-ones kept as three; Tarn Skell not identified as living or dead; **no person supplied for the authority of the public-necessity sentence or of anything else**; and **no figure, name, date or claim in the ending of the volume, the series outline or any later batch touched.**

## Volume 03 Batch 0003 Calendar, The Last Day Of The Third Month To The Second Week Of The Fifth Month

Ch 121 the **last day of the third month and the first of the fourth**; Ch 122 the **second and fourth of the fourth**; Ch 123 the **fifth, sixth and seventh of the fourth, with the sitting on the sixth at about the tenth hour for about two hours and a quarter**; Ch 124 the **ninth and tenth of the fourth**; Ch 125 the **eleventh and twelfth of the fourth**; Ch 126 the **thirteenth, fourteenth and fifteenth of the fourth**; Ch 127 the **sixteenth to the twenty-second of the fourth, the water being in on the nineteenth at about the second hour of the morning**; Ch 128 the **fourth week of the fourth**; Ch 129 the **first week of the fifth**; Ch 130 the **second week of the fifth**. **No month is given a length anywhere, so the recorded ambiguity about the length of the third month is inherited unchanged.**

## Figures Named In Volume 03 Batch 0003

- **The four words that void a sheet**, and the date they had to be said, **the last day of the third month, and not one day past it.** Three people know it: the keeper, the man, and a clerk in a city four hundred and thirty miles away who does not know it yet. **The fourth week of the third month is printed nowhere in Chapters 121–140.**
- **About nine acres and about thirty-one households** behind four hundred and forty foot of outer face, **eleven feet high** where a crew of five men and a boy left it on the **twenty-first of the second month**. The miller is one of the thirty-one.
- **Seven shillings and sixpence** for two days of a crew at forty-five pence a day on five men and a boy. **Four shillings a year**, of which the whole is the rent of the hired stone store at a shilling a quarter, taken out of the book four times a year. **Three pounds eleven and threepence** for nineteen days, out of a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week, being three weeks of it, paid in full, called **held** in the weigh-house ledger.
- **About nine inches** of toe gone at the third-of-the-way mark in seven weeks, in a miller's own hand, put to a pole eleven times between a cross and thirty foot either way, giving between eight and ten inches, **and nine is what eleven of them say.**
- **The north bay is thirty-one foot by nineteen and holds about thirty.** The south end of the same store is about eleven feet by nineteen with a floor a foot off the ground, a stove, a bench, a door that shuts, and a chest with a hasp that has not been locked in six years.
- **About one hundred and forty feet of ditch** on the south side and the scour at the third-of-the-way place, on nobody's list. **The ford is the seventh work of eleven** on the page from ninety-one years back, on nobody's list, its two posts down and nine stones stacked neatly on the low side, and Hob Bray has not put them back and nobody has asked him to.
- **About four hundred names** in the fourth column of the Reed Cut district works' return book in three years, **about nine hundred and forty names** in the Company's book in about sixty years, **about nine hundred and forty in the register of a court in nineteen years, of which about two hundred and ten could not be found and about a hundred and forty are households** — the last of these is Batch 0004's figure and is not to be added to the others.
- **The three thirty-ones are three figures in three places** and are never put in one sentence as a pattern: the thirty-one households behind the bank, the thirty-one Mosswake households of the sixth year, and about thirty-one people in a lane at the north of the ford.

## Divergences Between The Cards And The Page, Volume 03 Batch 0003

Recorded here and at the foot of `outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0003.md`. **None of these is a canon change and none contradicts an inherited fact; the page governs in every case.**

1. **The card for Chapter 0121 is titled *A Letter Nobody Sent Him* and the page is too, and the letter on the page is not a letter to a person.** The card's *next-chapter pull* says the keeper must carry a dead sheet four miles upstairs; the page sends the **second sheet**, addressed to a notary's table, up the road with a courier, and sends the keeper four miles **upstairs to the district works gate** on the second and fourth with the dead sheet and a memorandum. **Both sheets exist and both are dated, and the courier's sheet reaches the fourth counter in Chapter 0132 and the keeper's refusal reaches the House in Chapter 0133.**
2. **The cards' calendar gives Chapter 0122 the second, third and fourth of the fourth month; the page has the second and the fourth only,** because the third of the fourth month is a date already fixed on the page in Chapter 0102 and may not be re-used for a different act. **Recorded in the card's own location line and kept.**
3. **The card for Chapter 0123 leaves the point of view open and the page takes Ivet Sarn,** because the sitting is in her court, the undertaking that carries it was entered by a magistrate who is not in the room, and the chapter's finding is about the undertaking being used a second and a third time by a clerk who has begun to notice what that is. **Bel Skell's chapter was 0118 and it is a lane, a leg and a name given out loud, and none of that is spent twice in the same month.**
4. **The card for Chapter 0125 says the undertaking catches the sheet because it is in his coat for four days; the page says eleven days and the copyist has been in the room for two hours,** and the second use of the rule is dated the eleventh with an answer on the twelfth and four days of not acting between them. **Both figures are on the page and neither contradicts the other; the card's four days is the interval between the answer and the sending.**
5. **The card for Chapter 0126 has Halla Wray asked a third time and the ford lapsing; the page has her asked a third time by a district foreman on the fourteenth, and the lapsing on the last day of the second week,** and it adds that she is asked about the spring bank as well and says no for the same reason in fewer words. **The second time the batch touches the wage the whole cost is on the page, and that is the card's own requirement and it is met.**
6. **The card for Chapter 0127 places the counting in the mud on the morning of the twentieth; the page has the water in on the nineteenth at about the second hour, the counting on the twentieth at about the seventh hour, and about eleven people clearing nine hours of mud out of the bay on the twenty-first, unpaid.** The card's *power or resource change* says about nine persons of about thirty are standing in a mill yard on the morning of the twentieth; the page says exactly that and adds the fourth figure, **that about sixty are not anywhere.**
7. **The card for Chapter 0129 says the mark comes about nine minutes after the consent; the page says exactly that, and puts Tamsin Rook's four things about the mark in the same room and in about nine minutes, and makes the fourth of them the sentence she would like written down and will not write down herself.** The card's line about the wrist being about two inches is on the page and is not a reading and takes nothing.
8. **The cards for Chapters 0127, 0128 and 0130 all require the sentence about distribution and the sentence about a body that cannot refuse, and the page puts the distribution sentence in Chapter 0127, the cannot-refuse sentence in Chapter 0127 and Chapter 0128, and neither is repeated as a quotation in Chapter 0130** — 0130 carries the rule that binds thirty and says nothing about sixty, and the five live things. **No chapter restates another's finding as a quotation.**
9. **The card for Chapter 0130 says a copy goes to a clerk's sill on the eighth of the fifth month; the page keeps that date,** and the volume's fourth movement opens on the second and third weeks of the fifth month with a letter that has been standing in a mill yard since the eighth, which the card for 0131 requires. **Both are true and the copy arriving and the letter arriving are different things on the same road in the same week.**
10. **The card for Chapter 0123 requires that about sixty be entered as not bound and not served, and the page does that; the card's *resistance* also says there is no form in that court for serving a person who refused to give a name, and the page supplies the ordinary form's words: the person notified can read, in a place where nobody has ever put a question about that word.** That is the page's addition and it is better than the card and is kept.

## The Rules Established On The Page In Volume 03 Batch 0004, And The Locks Held

1. **A body cannot open its own post, and the reason is not a trick: a letter wants an answer and an answer has to come from a person.** A body cannot be served, cannot refuse, cannot open its own post, **and there is no form in this empire in which a body may say *I have no person*.** **A person who opens a paper addressed to a body becomes the person the paper is about** — the exact thing a miller of about fifty worked out alone in six years about a paper with his own name at the top of it, and it happened to him anyway because there was nobody else to do it and about thirty people were going to watch.
2. **A water guarantee under the fire-and-water device, offered without charge, whose price is not stated and is a labour succession.** The operative words, quoted once, in full, in Chapter 0131: the holder **will hold the water of the Slade Cut against flood for the length of the works**, **will stand the cost of the bank, the ditch, the scour and the works at the ford, without charge, and will not ask any household for any sum**; and **the labour of those works is furnished by the Company and by such persons as are of the Company at the time the work is done**; a person who is of the Company at one time and not at another has furnished the labour of the works done while he was of it; **the Company is not discharged by the death of a member and is not discharged by the coming of age of a member**; and **nothing in this instrument is a debt of any household and nothing in it may be sued for by any household or against any household, and no child, widow or person not of the Company is named in it, is party to it, or may be sued upon it.** **The capturing clause is the third paragraph and the disclaimer is the fourth: it is in the middle of the instrument and not at the end of it, both sentences are honest, and the disclaimer is true about suing and not true about being in it.**
3. **A guarantee with no price is not free; it is a thing whose price is not stated, and a document whose price is not stated is the one document nobody can refuse, because a person cannot know what they are agreeing to.** And: **the nine days in the guarantee is not a deadline for answering. A deadline is a thing that stops something. It is a window on a thing that does not stop. The instrument stands offered and unanswered, it is true already, and it may be taken up at any time afterwards by a person answering in those terms.**
4. **The guarantee is not a forgery, not a lie, not the substituted wording, and not an instance of anything.** The substituted phrase remains *the present holder*, stands in four files of four ages, and **does not appear in Chapters 131–140.** No fifth instance of anything is added. **The guarantee is four hundred years of dragon form design and nothing whatever to do with anybody's hand, and it is the only sentence in this matter drafted by somebody who told the truth about what it does.**
5. **What a notary's table may certify, in nine lines, written out in a clerk's own hand and dated and signed in Chapter 0132: what an instrument is and when it was made and what it is called; what a book is and what is written in it; what a party is; what a document does. It may not certify that a person ought to do a thing, who ought to answer for a thing, or what a body is made of.** And: **in the hundred and forty years of that table no body has ever been able to put a person in front of the fourth line, because no body has ever had a person to put there, and a form that has never been asked a question is the most dangerous kind of form there is.**
6. **A table may refuse a question and be entirely right, and a party may put the same question differently and be served inside four days, and the only defence anybody in this empire has against that is a person who is in the room when the second version is written. There is no form for that. There is a person for that.**
7. **The fourth line is free.** Not fourpence, not a shilling. **It is the only line on that form with no charge against it anywhere in the building, and it is the one that changes what a person is, and it is free because nobody has ever had to charge for it, because nobody has ever been in a position to be charged.** And: **a thing nobody is charged for is a thing nobody thinks about, and a thing nobody thinks about is the thing that takes a child into a guarantee in a bay four hundred and thirty miles away.**
8. **A book with names in it is not a list of persons, and the difference between a record and a register is about eleven seconds wide and it is the width of a person standing up deciding which one they are doing.** **The Company's book was not made in order to find anybody. A guild's list of persons is, and that is the whole of the difference, and both are findable and one of them is a register.**
9. **A seal is a promise that the paper and the ground are the same thing**, said out loud by a notary of House Oris, once, in Chapter 0139, and **a seal on a thing that is not true is not a mistake, it is a lie with a House's name on it.** And: **a notary's seal is a promise and a promise of that kind is only worth what the person promising it is, and that is the whole of a notary's office and there is nothing else in it.**
10. **A refusal can be entered as a payment, and the only form in which a dragon can be seen to pay is a form with its name at the foot of it.** A notary of a house who is entered in the register of concessions as having refused a certification has that chamber's authority withdrawn, and **the House's oldest fire-and-water concession on that reach, held about a hundred and forty years, is entered as lapsped, with his name at the foot and his four sentences copied in as the reason.** The House's practice is that **a concession is held and not owned, and a concession is only ever entered with the name of the person who gave it up**, and the reason the rule exists is that **a House which can lose one without saying who lost it will lose it quietly and will lose the next one quietly, and a House that is going to lose one anyhow might as well have it written down.** **A person who is not a person in the world can be made to pay for a thing by having his name written down, and the price of the payment is that he is findable for ever, and an institution cannot be sued and a person can be, and that is the whole of the difference and it is not a fair one.**
11. **The split instrument.** An answer in two parts is an answer in one part with a refusal in the middle of it. **There is a column for it and nobody in that house had used it in nineteen years.** One half carries a seal and one half carries a name, and both are on one sheet, and the entry in the book of concessions is made by the practice's clerk and the reason is copied in the notary's own hand.
12. **A notice of a seat is a seat's paper and it is a body acting, and it requires an acknowledgement, and the form has a box for the answer and a line about two inches long for a reason and NO SPACE OF ANY KIND FOR A WHY.** It may be declined in a form with a reason, and it is declined in Chapter 0135 in nine words — *I am not the head of a body and cannot be and there is none* — **and a person who is named at the top of a seat's notice has been made findable in a building about two hundred miles away, and refusing the paper costs the refuser the only thing the paper was.** A seat's notice asks a person to acknowledge receipt; **a person who says nothing has not refused and has not accepted and the seat records it either way.**
13. **The carriage offer is answered and stays inoperable.** For about fourteen months the seat's fifth line has offered the carriage of any person whom **the district court** thinks should be heard, and its own terms require the district court to be asked, and nobody had asked it. **In Chapter 0136 the Mosswake Roll Court is asked, in writing, in a form, and answers with four persons and a foreman — five in all — and the offer still cannot operate, because it pays carriage and not attendance and there is no form in this empire for compelling a person to be brought to a room.** **Four persons and a foreman is the whole of what about fourteen months has produced and it is not nothing and it is not enough.** The court does not write to the seat about it again.
14. **A magistrate's own flood records, in her own hand, in her own press, can be read by her and only by her, and what comes out of reading them as a set is the volume's third bound obligation, paid by her and by nobody else.** In nineteen years she has enforced continuation clauses against **about nine hundred and forty people** in this district in about a hundred and forty matters, and has not enforced them against **about four hundred**, in three categories: **about two hundred and ten who cannot be found and therefore stand served for ever and were never heard; about a hundred and forty who are of a household, in files where she entered the word *household* in the third line, and a household cannot be put in a room; and, third, people she chose not to, twice, on purpose, in the same fortnight of two flood seasons, in one of them choosing which forty out of about a hundred and thirty and in the other choosing which nine out of about two hundred and forty.** **Nine hundred and forty made answerable by choice, and about four hundred not made answerable by choice, and the second four hundred are the ones that make the first nine hundred and forty indefensible as a rule, because a rule is applied the same way each time, and this was not, and she chose, and the reason she chose is good, and a good reason applied unevenly by one person in a district for nineteen years is not a rule and cannot be defended as one.** It is entered as **a finding of fact about her own practice, on her own motion, in her own court, naming no person, against herself, not appealable by anybody, not a finding about any other court, not a discovery, and not a seam.** **She will not name the about four hundred, and the reason is on the page: if she puts the names in, a person in about four hundred and thirty miles who is entitled to be heard about it will be able to find her, and she is under a warrant on a different matter in a different city, and she is not going to hand them a list of about four hundred people she decided not to hold.** **The undertaking she entered on the ninth of the second month is used in this batch for the first time with her in the building and having been asked, and the first three uses were without her.** **She says nothing new about whether she was right, and the file of the sixth year is not opened and is not mentioned.**
15. **A person who speaks for ninety-one other people is not hearing them, he is speaking for them, and hearing is one person at a time and never to ninety-one households at once.** **The fourth line can be filled in now, and must not be, and cannot be, and the reason is that a person who answers for a body is its head, and a magistrate has entered that the head of that body cannot be had, and Marek Kest would rather say that in a room than have a person appointed to it in a city by a person who cannot be named.** **The volume's own method is what opened the dragon's door. The volume's own method was right. Both are on the page and neither cancels the other, and that is the moral shape of Volume 03.**
16. **A free warning is a warning that has been read by nobody, and the most honest sentence anybody in this matter produced in four months reached ninety-one households through a person who is not paid and is not allowed to explain it.** The sentence is *a person should not answer without knowing what they are agreeing to*, and it is on the fourth-line form of a guarantee, and it is true, and it protects the House and not the Slade, and it cost nothing to write.
17. **A list of persons made by a body about ninety-one households is a different thing from a book a body has kept for sixty years, and the difference is the reason for the making: the book was not made in order to find anybody and the list is, and a record made in order to find somebody is a register.** **A list of persons exists so that somebody can be found, and that is not a bad thing to make, and about ninety days of a ford at one shilling and sixpence a day for a man and a boy is a hundred and thirty-five shillings, which is six pounds fifteen shillings, and which is more than the Company's whole money for about three years and more than the spring bank cost.** **A factor who puts a person in a position where that person can say no to him is a factor who has been in a room.**
18. **Locks held and checked, with nothing to correct in Chapters 131–140:** **Vaunt Oris does not repeat that he is not the First Seat, is not asked to, is not made to explain himself, does not apologise and does not convert**; **Seryn Oris is not present and is not named and is credited with nothing in the batch, and the counsel of the First Seat is not named**; **the three Orises are never merged**; **the MC/FS circular and the First Seat quarterly return are not named on the page in this batch at all, the quarterly return is still in no reply, and Hallis Dren's return of the eleventh of the tenth is still dormant, armed, and known to nobody in the story**; **the fourth envelope in Tamsin Rook's drawer is still there, was not sent and was not opened**; the two sealed pages stay shut and **there is no third refusal of either**, and the red stitch, the thread in the fold of the sealed page and the working stitch in the Ashfall bundle are three different things and no two of them are joined in a sentence, and **the Ashfall bundle is not opened and is not named**; **what Ilyra Kest found in the Ashfall archive and why the original document names a blank heir is not answered, not referred to as a question, and not advanced in one line**; **no reading of any instrument is performed by anybody in these ten chapters and the only two readings in the manuscript remain Chapter 8 and Chapter 40**; **no Roll-Answer, no certification, no mixed warrant, no true-name reading, no true seal, no register of persons who read, and no new mark**; **the word *exception* is not used of anything in this batch**; **no calendar month name and no weekday name appears in any of the ten chapters**; **the substituted phrase and the public-necessity sentence are both absent and no fifth instance of anything is added and the four appearances are not written as a chain**; the north-bank works instrument stays a fact about a file in a magistrate's press and is not called a seam; **the file of the sixth year is not opened**; **the girl of eleven at the Reed Cut gate is unused, unnamed and given no reason, and Bel Skell is not that girl and the two are never equated**; **Raff Ordway is not in this batch and the boy of fifteen is not Raff Ordway and no chapter says he is permanently injured and no chapter makes him a device, a symbol, an heir or a plaintiff**; **Tarn Skell is not identified as living or dead and no chapter states that the four houses are related to him**; **the date the fifth term's schedule was nailed to the rail is printed nowhere and its age is not restated**; **the Venn ruling stays in five terms and the words *exception* and *precedent* are not used of its certification**; **the thirty-one of Mosswake, the eleven, the twelve, the six, the eighteen and the fourth column are untouched, no figure is printed against them, and nobody adds the six to the eighteen**; **the fifth heading of the register of pending duties is *Copy sold***; **the three thirty-ones are three figures in three places and are never put in one sentence as a pattern**; **Lowcross is unchanged, open, unpaid, nineteen pounds three and fourpence, and nobody is liable**; **Corvin Dray is not re-litigated and the direction that nobody be asked about the ninety-second leaf stands**; **44,819 is not read, the year in the run is not entered, and the counting-house mark is a mark**; and **nothing in the ending of the volume, the series outline, or any later batch was touched.**

## Volume 03 Batch 0004 Calendar, The Second Week Of The Fifth Month To The Last Week Of The Sixth Month

Ch 131 the **second and third weeks of the fifth month**; Ch 132 the **third week of the fifth month**, in Auremar; Ch 133 the **fourth week of the fifth month**; Ch 134 the **last week of the fifth month and the first week of the sixth**; Ch 135 the **first and second weeks of the sixth**; Ch 136 the **second and third weeks of the sixth**; Ch 137 the **third and fourth weeks of the sixth**; Ch 138 the **fourth week of the sixth**; Ch 139 the **fourth and last weeks of the sixth**; Ch 140 the **last week of the sixth**. **Chapters 132 to 140 cross to Auremar, and the journey is about six days on a towpath and in a cart and on a bench and no chapter pretends it is shorter.** The fixed dates inside the batch: **the letter came on the eighth of the fifth month and was opened on the eleventh and the nine days shut on the twentieth; the second sheet went to the fourth counter on the twenty-first; the keeper's refusal is dated the twenty-fourth; the split instrument is dated the twenty-sixth of the sixth; the copy of it reached the bay on the twenty-ninth; the copy of the notice of a seat went up a towpath on the thirtieth; and a day has been fixed for the third week of the seventh month, which is Batch 0005 and is not held here.**

## Figures Named In Volume 03 Batch 0004

- **The guarantee: no figure at all.** It is offered at no charge and its price is a clause. **The guild's rate is one shilling and sixpence a day for a barrow of stone, a man and a boy, and ninety days of that is a hundred and thirty-five shillings, which is six pounds fifteen shillings.** The cut's own day rate is unchanged at **forty-five pence on five men and a boy**, and the Company's whole money is unchanged at **four shillings a year, of which the whole is the rent.**
- **The fourth counter: a shilling for a question, fourpence for a copy, four shillings for a certificate, an answer in about eleven weeks, about four hundred blanks in a box under the counter, of which two hundred and sixty-one have the fourth line empty, a hundred and forty have it filled in, a hundred and twenty-six of those with a person's name and fourteen with a body, and eleven of those fourteen with a person's name and a date underneath in a different hand.**
- **The register of concessions: a book about a hundred and forty years old with a crack in the spine, nine things in the room, about four hundred instruments a year, a column that had never been used in nineteen years, and about four hundred years of house practice that no instrument takes effect in a room before the persons it touches have had the day.** The surveyor's letters: **about four hundred of them, over a year of service, and the factor cannot tell you the man's name.**
- **The Mosswake Roll Court's own figures, entered on the motion of the magistrate on the twenty-ninth of the sixth month: about nine hundred and forty people made answerable in nineteen years in about a hundred and forty files; about four hundred not made answerable, of whom about two hundred and ten could not be found and about a hundred and forty are households and the rest she chose; about a hundred and thirty in one flood season of which she held about forty; about two hundred and forty in another of which she held about nine; five persons named or described in the answer; one cost entered as about four hundred and thirty miles of carriage.** **The about four hundred are not to be added to the about four hundred names in the district works' return book four miles away or to the about nine hundred and forty in the Company's book, and no chapter adds them.**
- **A book with about nine hundred and forty names in it, nine hands, about sixty years, no heading, no list of persons, about four hundred of the people in it dead, and one name against an entry since the sixth of the third month and one more since the first week of the fifth month.** The fifth term's schedule is referred to nowhere in this batch and its date and age are printed nowhere.
- **The three thirty-ones, the four hundred and forty foot of outer face, the eleven feet of bank, the nine acres, the thirty-one households behind it, the hundred and forty feet of ditch, and about sixty children under sixteen, are all unchanged and none of them is re-derived.**

## The Three New Named Figures In This Matter And The One Added In This Batch

- **Aldis Kern, forty-six, factor of the Kingsway Road Guild for the western reach**, bound for Batch 0005 and every later batch. **A contract with nine months on it; about four hundred yards of road; about nine men in three gangs; a toll; a wagon; a weigh-house; a lock-up; a surveyor's letterhead and a name he cannot say; ninety days at one shilling and sixpence; a list of persons blank in his wagon since the third week of the fifth month; a line at the foot of it with no figure against it.** **He is not a villain, he is not a mouth for a power he does not hold, he has an independent want that does not depend on Marek succeeding, and he said out loud in a bay that he is not able to refuse the list and that he is putting the room in a position where they can refuse him.** **The Kingsway Road Guild is a body and is not a body of the ninety-one and is not a Roll Assembly.**
- **Vaunt Oris is bound above in `bible/characters.md` and is not new**, and everything Chapter 0134 and Chapter 0139 report about him is now on the page and binds Batch 0005.
- **The trade assessor who stands between a house and a guild stays unnamed**, and is fifty-one and has been doing it for twenty-six years, is paid by neither and retained by both, and is the only person in this batch in whom **a seat's form and a guild's list are in the same pair of hands** and who can say so.
- **Unnamed and to stay unnamed:** the clerk of the Reed Cut district water office, about twenty-three; the courier of the fourth line, about fourteen; the practice's clerk, about thirty-four; the boy who carried the message down the lane; the man of about thirty who said *I wanted to see if it worked*; the man who had signed a return he did not agree with in the ninth year, which is Wendt Sayer; the woman of about thirty-one and the woman of about forty-four at the north of the ford; the man of about forty at the gate linkage; the man of forty at the district works who took the four words; the whole Batch 0001, 0002 and 0003 unnamed lists, all undisturbed.
