**Two observations, recorded because the next writer will otherwise think something is wrong.** **One, the batch is under the Volume 03 per-chapter mean.** Volume 03 ran 2,862 to 4,597 with a mean of 3,522; this batch runs 2,139 to 3,010 with a mean of 2,580. **Nothing was trimmed to reach a figure and nothing was padded, and the two thin chapters are the two institutional ones, Chapter 0152 at 2,139 and Chapter 0158 at 2,273, and each of them is a chapter that turns on one exchange and is finished when the exchange is.** **Two, no band or target is recorded anywhere in this repository for a chapter's length, and the prompt's instruction is that nothing is trimmed to reach a number and the bands and overages are recorded as they stand: they stand here.**

## The rules established on the page in this batch, which Volume 04 must then keep

1. **A run number is not a document. It is a number in a run, and turning it into a row means finding a book that does not travel, and certifying a row means certifying what a book is and what is written in it.** The first, third and fourth of the four items on a notary's table's fee board are the same question asked three ways and are all about documents; **the second is the one that is not.**
2. **A notary's table may hold a thing for a hundred and forty years, and a person may take it out for fourpence, in person, and only a person, and only the person the thing is about or a person with a written authority from that person. There is no provision for anybody else and there never has been, and the reason nobody has ever asked for one is that a person who says out loud that they have looked for a provision that does not exist has told a room that the provision is wanted.**
3. **The answer to a question is given to the person who asked, and the person who asked is a party from the day the day goes at the top of the form. So the door costs findability and the eleven weeks is not the price.**
4. **A table may refuse a question and be entirely right, and a party may put the same question differently and be served inside four days, and the only defence anybody in this empire has against that is a person who is in the room when the second version is written. There is no form for that. There is a person for that.**
5. **A refusal with a reason in it and no box for a why is a resolution of a kind, and it is findable, and being right is what makes it a document. There is no way in this empire to un-ask.** Carried forward from Volume 03 and cashed in, once, at a cost.
6. **The fourth line is free.** Not fourpence, not a shilling. It is the only line on that form with no charge against it anywhere in that building, it is the one that changes what a person is, and it is free because nobody has ever been in a position to be charged for it. **And a thing nobody is charged for is a thing nobody thinks about.**
7. **The fourth line is on a strip about two inches by one, gummed to the foot of the form, with a printed corner number so that the strip and the sheet can be matched. There is no knife on that counter and there never has been. To lift the strip you damage the sheet. Therefore a person who answers the only question on that form destroys the office's copy of it, and the office is left with a hole where the answer was and no way in this empire to make anybody produce it.**
8. **A form with a reason in it and no box for a no is a form in which nobody is offered a no.** And the specific case: **the no is under the gum, and a person cannot get to a no they do not know is there, and so eleven people in a hundred and forty years answered the question and the other about a hundred and forty a year did not, and most of the other hundred and forty never found out it was there.**
9. **A column with a heading over it is a machine for putting a name in a book so that the name can never be asked anything, and a column with no heading over it is the same book with the machine taken out.** And the second half, which is the batch's engine: **a person with nothing enforceable behind them can be made to answer for nothing at all, because there is nothing to be liable for and because nobody can serve a person who has nothing to serve. A blank heir is not a safe person. A blank heir is a cheap person.**
10. **A person who is made blank of enforceable rights is not made safe, and the blankness is a door with a person in it, and the person is nearly always the one who did the blanking, because she is the one who could. A family with nothing enforceable can be put to by nobody and can also be named as the road by which somebody else is put to, and both of those are true of the same piece of paper at the same hour, and the person who arranged the first one is the only person who can say which of the two was meant.** Entered in Chapter 0159 in the reader's own words. **This is the first half of the volume's new rule and the second half — the finding that a blank heir is a test for eligibility and eligibility is a thing somebody wants — is deliberately not entered, and the word *eligibility* does not appear anywhere in the batch and a clerk in a city has it in a book and it is not on this page.**
11. **A room with one door in it is a room a person can be put into, and a door that does not shut is a door anybody can be put through, and a door needs a person to shut it exactly as a duty needs a holder that can stop, and neither of them is a work and there is no column anywhere for either.** And the positive form: **a room with a door that shuts is a room a person can leave, and that is all a door is for.**
12. **A party is a person a book can be true about, and a body is not a party and cannot be made one, and there is no form in this empire for taking a party back out of a box.** The second half of that is not a rule of this batch's finding; it is a plain fact of the practice, and the size of the looking is stated on the page: about four minutes at a counter, not in every place.
13. **A thing somebody did in a room is a thing that can be asked about, and a person who is paid for standing in a room is a person who is findable, and the price of a door is the door.**

## The calendar of this batch, as printed

**The batch runs from the first week of the fifth month of next year to the last week of the seventh month of next year, which is where Chapter 0150 left off, and Chapter 0150 is dated the second of the fifth month of next year.** Ch 151 the first week on the road and the second week in Auremar; Ch 152 the second week into the third; Ch 153 the third week; Ch 154 the third week, later in it; Ch 155 the fourth week; Ch 156 the last week of the fifth into the first week of the sixth; Ch 157 the third week of the sixth; Ch 158 the fourth week of the sixth; Ch 159 the second week of the seventh; Ch 160 the third week of the seventh on the road and the last week of the seventh in Auremar. **The gap between Chapter 0158 and Chapter 0159 is about three weeks of the first season's last weeks and is covered in one paragraph and is not staged.**

**Four journeys and three completed.** Up in the first week of the fifth; down in the last week of the fifth; up in the third week of the seventh. **Twenty-four days, about three pounds, no fund.** The cards had said the descent reached the Slade in the second week of the sixth; the page puts the arrival on the second day of the first week of the sixth, because six days out of the last week of the fifth lands in the first week, and the cards file's calendar is corrected to match at *Divergences* item 11.

**Inherited dates spent or named in this batch, none of them moved.** The first season began on the second of the fifth month of next year and is about ninety days, so **its review is about the first week of the eighth month of next year; the batch names that week three times and does not spend the review.** The reader's engagement is **printed once, in Chapter 0156, as beginning on the first of the fourth month of next year, with a copy in a satchel, and no elapsed period is computed from it anywhere in the batch**, because Chapter 0150 also says she is three weeks into it and the first of the fourth to the second of the fifth is nearer a month than three weeks. The first exception was withdrawn by a document dated the twenty-fourth of the ninth month of last year and effective the second of the fifth month of next year, and neither date is printed in this batch. **The first door-to-door asking was the seventh of the seventh month and the day a chair fixed was the fifteenth of it**, and neither is printed in this batch.

**One date this batch is forbidden to have, and does not have.** The volume outline and the operative prompt both attach *about thirty-one years ago* to **a retired scale notary being trained by a woman called Kest**, and `bible/characters.md` and `bible/world.md` attach the arrangement that left the Kest property-and-debt record unusable to **twelve years before the opening case**, and make that arrangement an act of a named person about a named child. **The three are not reconcilable. No chapter in this batch prints a date, an age, or a number of years for the arrangement, for the redaction, for the register's rows, or for the woman who arranged it, and no chapter says how long ago anything in the Kest file happened, and no chapter says that the woman called Kest is Ilyra Kest.** This is recorded as a **proposed canon change** and it is the second one below.

## The figures this batch printed, and which of them are new

**Inherited and unmoved, and every one verified on the page of this batch or carried as a fact:** a shilling for a question at a notary's table; fourpence for a copy; about eleven weeks for an answer; about four hundred blanks in a box under the fourth counter; **two hundred and sixty-one of the fourth lines empty, a hundred and forty filled, in a hundred and twenty-six of those a person's name, in fourteen of them no person's name, and in eleven of the fourteen a person's name and a date underneath in a different hand**; a hundred and forty years at that counter; the fee board's four items; **seventeen shillings and a penny the week; three pounds eleven and threepence for nineteen days at forty-five pence a day, under the word *held*; forty-five pence a day for the reader; four shillings a year for a hired stone store; the north bay thirty-one foot by nineteen holding about thirty; about nine hundred and forty names in nine hands over about sixty years with about four hundred dead; about ninety-one households and about sixty children under sixteen; about forty-one people in rotation and about nineteen refusals and about four never asked; about four hundred and forty foot of outer face; a hundred and forty yards below the ford; about four hundred yards to the tool shed; about four hundred and thirty miles each way and about six days; the Lowcross bill at nineteen pounds three and fourpence with no line for a bridge in that fund in nineteen years; about a penny a day; four hundred and eleven on the far side; three hundred and eighty-eight crossings; about nine men in three gangs; a carter of thirty-one; a chair of sixty-four; a reader of seventeen; a girl of eleven at a Reed Cut gate; Rosamund Crane, eleven, who reads; a woman of about seventy-one at the fourth house at the north of the ford; a woman of about thirty-one entered as a description and not a name; a boy of fifteen who cannot stand on the leg; Raff Ordway, sixteen.** The **fifth heading of the register of pending duties is *Copy sold*** and is not printed in this batch, and the fourth column and the sixth space are not printed in this batch.

**New figures established on the page in this batch, and available to the rest of the volume.** **About thirty rows in one run of impressions are not scale instruments, and no column in that book says which is which.** **A run number is a number in a run and about nine hundred blanks a year go out of a rack and about four hundred forms a year have the fourth line filled in.** **Ninepence is what he paid a carrier of about fifty at a lock for one line, out of a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week, and it is in no book.** **A day of that wage is a little over two shillings and fivepence, six days is a little over fourteen shillings, three journeys are twenty-one days, a fourth will be about twenty-four, twenty-four days is about three pounds, and a year of that wage is about forty-four pounds.** **About thirty of the four hundred blanks in the box have a body in the box at the top.** **A copyist's hand that slopes about nine degrees to the right and that everybody in three galleries can tell from anybody else's** — the same hand appears in Chapters 0155 and 0160 and is described identically in both, once, and that is deliberate and not a slip.

**Two figures a reader should know are inherited and odd and are the page's, not this batch's.** **One, a form bought and not filled in is priced both ways on the page and has been since Volume 03: the blank is a shilling whether it is filled in or not, and a form bought and not filled in is fourpence of proof that the question was asked. Both sentences are the manuscript's own and are both in this batch.** **Two, about four hundred years of that building's small commerce is said to be in a box under that counter, which cannot be true of a register a hundred and forty years old. The sentence is Chapter 0132's, word for word, and this batch quotes it and does not improve it, and a repair phase may not touch it in a closed volume.**

## The locks, checked over the ten files, and how

Every one of these was searched over `chapters/volume-04/chapter-015*.md` and `chapter-0160.md` and held, and the method is given because the Volume 03 audit's own method note is that a claim of absence is cheap and a claim of uniqueness is the one that costs.

- **Calendar month names and weekday names.** `grep -rniE "\b(january|february|march|april|may|june|july|august|september|october|november|december|monday|tuesday|wednesday|thursday|friday|saturday|sunday)\b"` over the ten files returns **fifteen lines, and every one of the fifteen is the modal verb *may*.** **No calendar month name and no weekday name appears anywhere in the batch.** **Five weekday names were in fact written and every one was found and corrected on the page before the batch closed:** *Thursday* twice and *Friday* once and *Tuesday* once in Chapter 0155, and *Wednesday* once in Chapter 0158, all of them instances of a day being named rather than counted, and all five are now counted days.
- **The substituted phrase *the present holder*: absent. The word *exception*: absent, and there is no Stage 2 instrument in the batch, so the word has nothing to be used of. *Certification*: the word appears five times and every one of the five denies that he holds one.**
- **The two documents, the quarterly return, Hallis Dren's return of the eleventh of the tenth, the substituted wording, the public-necessity sentence, the three red things, the two sealed pages, the counting-house mark, the year in the run, forty-four thousand eight hundred and nineteen, the ninety-second leaf: every one of them is absent, and no chapter of the batch names the Ashfall bundle or refers to what Ilyra Kest found as a question.**
- **The three Orises: none of the three is named in the batch, and the House Oris water notaries are held off the first batch as the volume outline holds them.** Oren Tal is not in the batch and is not asked to explain himself. **Aurelia Pell is not in the batch and belongs to the second.**
- **Pell Vey: one hand, once, in a column headed *by whose authority*, in Chapter 0157, described and not named there, and **that is not the documentation of his involvement, which the volume puts in its last two batches. His daughter and he are never merged and the same surname is not a door into anything, and in this batch only the hand and the name on a line are on the page.**
- **The Venn ruling is never said to have four terms and the word *term* is not used of it anywhere in the batch. The schedule on the rail is referred to once, in Chapter 0159, without a date and without a figure of an age, and the four old nails and the four new nails are on the page and the four old ones are still in the rail and nobody pulls anything down.**
- **The three thirty-ones: no sentence in the ten chapters sets two of the five side by side, and the batch prints the about nine hundred and forty names of the Company's book four times and the about nine hundred blanks a year of a rack twice and never in a sentence with the about nine hundred and forty people a magistrate made answerable, which is not printed in this batch at all.** The guild's hundred and thirty-five shillings is not printed in this batch.
- **The guarantee: not paraphrased into a new order, not answered, not extended, and named three times, in Chapters 0158, 0159 and 0160, as a thing standing offered and unanswered with about sixty children under sixteen inside it. No child is named. The third instrument of Volume 03 is not withdrawn, varied, extended or disowned.**
- **The girl of eleven at the Reed Cut gate is in Chapters 0156 and 0159, is read to, is given no reason, is not a device, is not a symbol, is not Bel Skell, is not Rosamund Crane, and is owed nothing. Bel Skell, about seventy-one, of the fourth house at the north of the ford, is named once in Chapter 0159 as a person being asked at her own door four times a year as she asked in writing. The boy of fifteen and Raff Ordway are not in the batch and are not merged with anybody. The woman of about thirty-one at the first house is not in the batch and is not asked for a name.**
- **The relationship: no romance begins and nothing on the page reads as intimacy in the ordinary sense. The V3 half of the milestone is not re-paid and not re-staged. The paper that puts her in his rooms and the watch that requires a person not from the office in his rooms are two instruments and are never merged, and Chapter 0155 says so in Tamsin Rook's own mouth. Her third line forbids advice and she says the words *that is a fact and not advice* before she says the fact, four times in the batch. The rule of the twelfth month of last year is used once, in Chapter 0155, in writing with a date, and answered in a room in words with a date, and the answer is that the rule does not apply — a correct non-use, and the second correct non-use anybody has made with it.**
- **The fourth envelope in her drawer is mentioned once, in Chapter 0155, in a sentence that does not touch it: it is not opened, not sent, and it is four lines long, and nobody looks at it.**
- **No System panel appears in the batch and none is required. The manuscript's system is intrinsic and has never been a panel. The mark on the inside of his left wrist is not mentioned once in the batch except as something that does nothing, and it is not the price.**

## The craft bounds, measured, and the method stated before the claim

The outline binds emphasis at about a fifth of a chapter's **lines** and about a fifth of a chapter's **prose text**, and requires a batch to say which of the two it measured and with what method. **Both were measured, over all ten files, and both hold.**

**On lines:** lines carrying a `**` over all lines, all-lines denominator. **Minimum 6.0% in Chapter 0156, maximum 14.6% in Chapter 0152, median 9.4%.** Ten of ten at or under a fifth. **This is the measure Volume 03 passes on, where the volume runs 2% to 22% with a median of 14%, and the batch's median is lower than the volume's.**

**On prose text:** characters inside `**…**` as a share of non-blockquote characters with the asterisks themselves removed from the denominator, and with the asterisks paired inside the paragraph. **Minimum 14.9% in Chapter 0151, maximum 19.6% in Chapter 0155, median 17.9%.** Ten of ten at or under a fifth. **This is the measure Volume 03 fails on, where the volume runs 3% to 42% with a median of 23% and thirty-two of fifty chapters are over a fifth, and the batch's maximum is under the volume's median.** A batch that claimed only the lines measure would have been claiming the easy half.

**The pairing test, run because it is the one that produced a false baseline in Volume 03's own review.** A paragraph-level and blockquote-block-level parity test over all ten files, counting every paragraph and every blockquote block and never pairing across a blank line, **returns zero odd blocks.** There is no `**` in this batch that is opened and not closed inside its own paragraph, and the text measure and the lines measure above agree on all ten files, as they now do on all fifty of Volume 03.

**No more than one emphasised clause in any one speech turn:** the runs left standing are a clause, a sentence, or a pair of sentences opening a turn, and a blockquoted form is the form and not a default.

**`about nine`, measured with `grep -o 'about nine' file | wc -l` summed, which is the method the inherited figure was measured with and which counts *about nine hundred*, *about nine minutes*, *about nine seconds* and *about nineteen*.** **Minimum 1 in Chapter 0152, maximum 8 in Chapters 0155 and 0158, median 6.5, ten of ten at or under the bound of about eight.** **The thins were made by deleting a counted clause or referring to a thing instead of its number, and not by substituting a sibling figure: no figure the state files record verbatim was changed, and `about four`, `about forty`, `about thirty`, `about twenty`, `about sixty` and `about nineteen` were not swapped in for any of them.** Two canon figures were corrected on the page rather than thinned: **the box under the counter is about four hundred blanks and not about nine hundred** — the rack is the nine hundred — and **three pounds eleven and threepence** is printed without an *about* in front of it in the two places a stray *about three pounds and eleven threepence* had crept in.

**A duplicate audit, run over the ten files, returning zero.** Every paragraph longer than eighty characters and every sentence longer than seventy is counted across the batch: **no paragraph and no sentence occurs twice.** Three near-verbatim duplications were found by a reader and fixed before the batch closed: the arrival of the reader at a Reed Cut gate in Chapter 0156 and again in Chapter 0159, which are now two different moments doing two different jobs; the four questions asked by a girl of eleven, kept whole in Chapter 0156 and cut back in Chapter 0159; and the chair of sixty-four being in a bay four hundred and thirty miles away in Chapter 0158 when she is on the bench against the north wall of the room the chapter is set in.

## The two proposed canon changes, recorded and not acted on

1. **The date of the arrangement that made the Kest record unusable is not reconcilable between the volume outline's prompt and the bible, and this batch has no date for it at all.** `outline/volume-04.md` and the operative prompt attach *about thirty-one years ago* to a retired scale notary being trained by a woman called Kest. `bible/characters.md` makes the arrangement an act of Ilyra Kest made twelve years before the opening case, when Nell Kest was eight, and makes Marek the son of a woman who died when he was seventeen, which places the fire about twenty-one years before the fifth month of next year. **A card and a bible file appear to disagree, the card wins for that chapter, and the conflict is recorded here as required: the winning card is *print no date, no age, and no number of years for the arrangement, for the redaction, for the rows in the run, or for the woman*, and the page follows it. The disagreement is not resolved by this batch and is not resolved by recording it, and a later volume that wants a date has to decide which record it is obeying.**
2. **The building is a *hired stone store* in this batch and is not called anything else anywhere in it, and nobody on the page is told which of the page's two names is right.** Chapters 0156 and 0158 use it. Neither the floor of the south end of it is printed nor either of the two measurements of it. **This is the fourth of the four items the review of the Volume 03 handover put under the Volume 04 locks, and it is a question and not a repair, and this batch did not decide it and could not have.**

## What this batch owes the second batch, in one place

**An answer to a question bought at a shilling in the second week of the fifth month of next year, due in the first week of the eighth month of next year, which is a day a clerk of thirty-four can name and will read out at a counter.** **The first review of a season of about forty-one people, about the first week of the eighth month of next year, which nobody on that bank is going to be paid to walk to.** **Two dates in one week about four hundred and thirty miles apart and no form in this empire that puts them in one box.** **A party and a bay in one room, and a person on a list of works who is a description instead of a name and cannot be served as one.** **A column headed *by whose authority* with about thirty rows that are not instruments, and one hand, and the documentation of that hand is not this volume's first batch's.** **About nine hundred blanks a year with a strip of paper gummed over the only line on the form that changes what a person is, and eleven torn in a hundred and forty years, and whoever else in this empire printed a slip nobody carried.** **A woman who arranged something, about whom this batch knows three things and no name, no date and no age: that she could, that she used the same act twice, and that the machine that has been arranging it is a strip of paper about two inches by one.**
